PREFACE TO READING 

COME, Holy Spirit, fill the hearts of Thy faithful and enkindle in them the fire of Thy love. 

V. Send forth Thy Spirit and they shall be created. 

R. And Thou shalt renew the face of the earth. 

Let us pray

O God, who by the light of the Holy Spirit didst instruct the hearts of the faithful, grant that in the same Spirit we may be truly wise and ever rejoice in His consolation. Through Christ our 

Lord. Amen.

IFA 

PROLOGUE A Thought To Ponder 

F A divine messenger arrived from heaven and offered you two gifts, and only two, to be infallibly granted, what two things would you ask for?

The question deserves deep thought, on your part, because if you fail to answer intelligently you have already missed the purpose of living. After prayerfully weighing the merits of many possibilities, you reply: "Tell the good God that I ask first for supreme happiness; and, second, that this happiness be eternal.”

Supreme happiness and an eternity of it- God could grant no more; and the rational creature, from the essence of his nature, could not ask for anything less. Precisely what your rational mind must demand, the Almighty

Creator has already provided for you, as a reward for a short little span of knowing Him, loving Him and serving Him.

If the thinking man listens to the deepsea soundings of his soul, he will strive to respond to the three prime essentials, the principal divisions of this book: 

Is There a God? 

Did He Speak?

What Did He Say?

Chapter I  

RECENT television program ran with the title: "Youth Wants To Know." The fact is, everyone wants to know. The mind craves a well-balanced dietthe truth. Falsehood never satisfies. Sooner or later, counterfeits fizzle out. Truth remains. "For truth has such a face, and such a mien, As to be loved needs only to be seen." 

God is truth. 

Dryden, The Hind and the Panther (17th century)

In life's journey nothing can be wiser, more important, satisfying, or far-reaching than the knowledge of eternal truths. "WHAT IS TRUTH?"

When Christ appeared before the court of Pilate, the governor asked Him: "Art thou a king then? Jesus answered: 'Thou sayest that I am a king. For this was I born, and for this I came into the world, that I should give testimony to the truth. Every one that is of the truth heareth My voice.' Pilate saith to Him: 'What is truth?'” (St. John 18:37-38) 3

“FOR THEM ALSO” 

Pilate was too cowardly to wait for the answer. He lacked the courage to accept the truth. 

Philosophers define truth as "conformitas rei"-literally, "conformity of things," or the agreement between what is in the mind and what we express. St. Thomas 

Aquinas says: "Truth is the equation of thought and thing...

If we speak of truth as it exists in the intellect, according to its proper nature, then there are many truths in many created intellects. . . . 

But if we speak of truth as it is in things, then all things are true by one primary truth; to which each one is assimilated according to its own entity. Thus, although the essences of forms of things are many, yet the truth of the divine intellect is one, in conformity to which all things are said to be true." (St. Thomas Aquinas, Summa Theologica, I, Q. 16, no. 6)

CONFORMITY TO FACT 

People speak of a man of veracity, which means the one in question has the quality of being true or real, or genuine. "He is too genuine to be anything else but humble," is a fine tribute to one's veracity. In the final analysis, truth is conformity to the will of God, as expressed in the ten commandments. A virtuous man rings true. "Chimes are sweet when the metal is sound." The

Almighty Creator endows rational creatures with free will and intellect, making men 

... a little less than the angels." 

(Psalm 8:6) 

He exhorts them to reason. In a word, God expects to reap where He has sown. Probe the human heart; you find an innate desire for truth, perfect life, love, strength, happiness, and justice. Even the tough conflicts of life cannot destroy these ideals. 

To live rationally, an intelligent person wants to know: (1) Is there a God? (2) Did He speak? (3) What did He say?

The mind, weighing cause and effect, answers the first question. The written and unwritten revelation of God removes all doubt regarding the second and third questions. By the light of his own mind, an intelligent being, through right reasoning, reaches the conclusion that God exists.

GOD IS KNOWABLE FROM REASON 

God is knowable from creation. Every effect must have a cause. The world around us is an effect. It could not have caused itself because a thing must be before it can act. Creation demands unlimited power and wisdom.

The existence of God, then, becomes evident to the normal intelligent man and woman. This is known as the argument of causality.

The human mind soars from nature to nature's origin, from the contingent to a first necessary cause. The design of the universe, its order and adaptation of means to ends, its co-ordination of plans, specifications and development - all reflect the first Intelligent Cause, the one Necessary Being, the Prime Mover, the Supreme Lawgiver, the Sublime Architect and Builder of the world, the divine Ruler of all creation. The universe points to 

Almighty God, creator and sustainer of all things. 

Men speak of nature-but nature, its order and symmetry, the complex human body, all manifest the divine wisdom, the divine intelligence and all-absorbing beauty of God. "For the invisible things of Him, from the creation of the world are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made. His eternal power also and divinity: so that they are inexcusable." (Romans 1:20) 

THE VOICE OF CONSCIENCE 

Then, too, there are laws written on the heart of man. 

These enable our mind to form a judgment upon our acts, be they good, bad or indifferent; i. e., conscience - the still small voice of God - warns as to what is right, what is wrong; conscience speaks of duties, commands; its dictates are clear and unmistakable. Laws predicate a lawgiver. This final absolute authority is God. 

The actual experience of normal human beings refers us to God. The still small voice is audible, even among

the most unlettered. Conscience warns us that certain acts are bad; that man is responsible to an unseen Supreme Judge. In a word, there are laws written on the human heart. Whenever you have a law, you must have a lawgiver.

Conscience indicates that all of us are responsible for our conduct to an unseen judge, a Supreme Rewarder of good. If one could not arrive at these logical conclusions, he would not be normal. The greatest convert of modern times wrote on this subject: "Conscience always involves the recognition of a living object towards which it is directed. Inanimate things cannot stir our affections; these are correlative with persons. If, as is the case, we feel responsibility, are ashamed, are frightened at transgressing the voice of conscience, this implies that there is one to whom we are responsible, before whom we are ashamed, whose claims upon us we fear. If, on doing wrong, we feel the same tearful, broken-hearted sorrow which overwhelms us on hurting a mother; if, in doing right, we enjoy the same sunny serenity of mind, the same soothing, satisfactory delight which follows on our receiving praise from a father, we certainly have within us the image of some Person to whom our love and veneration look, in whose smile we find happiness, for whom we yearn, towards whom we direct our pleadings, in whose anger we are troubled and waste away. These feelings in us are such as require for their exciting cause an intelligent Being. Thus the phenomena of conscience avail to impress the imagination with a picture of a Supreme Governor, a Judge, Holy, Just, Powerful, All-See-

ing, Retributive" (John Henry Cardinal Newman, Grammar of Assent). "HE WHO IS" 

The word "God" comes from the Hebrew YAH WEH, which means "He who is," i. e., "the essence of being." Many deny the existence of God. Can man, by the light of reason, know that God exists? 

You understand that logic is the science of right reasoning, clear thinking. Apply logic to the question at hand. Every effect has a cause. The created world around us is an effect. It demands a cause. The world in which we live shows intelligent designing and, therefore, calls for an Intelligent Cause. Some theorists who admit the necessity of a Cause ascribe it to the forces of nature; but again, since the forces of nature demand a cause, you must get back to the First Cause, who is God. A cause must be proportionate to the effect. The world in which we live must have an all-powerful and intelligent Cause.

TRUTH DOES NOT CONTRADICT ITSELF 

To assert, as some do, that the world made itself is opposed to right reasoning because a thing must be before it can act. If the world made itself, it would have acted before it existed, which is obviously absurd. You know that this earth is composed of a fixed number of elements, such as lime, carbon, gold, silver, salt, oxygen, nitrogen, and many others. Again, it is unthinkable that each of these elements made itself. Why is there an exact amount of each? The Supreme Intelligent Being created each for

a precise purpose. Moreover, the rational creature must recognize his limitations.

The longing of his heart and the aspirations of his soul cry out for a Supreme Being, understanding, sympathetic, and lovable. We are like "an infant crying in the night, an infant crying for the light, and with no language but a cry" (Tennyson, In Memoriam, LIV, 5).

METAPHYSICAL VIEWPOINT 

Philosophers advance the metaphysical argument for a necessary being, one that has existed forever on account of its intrinsic nature. If the Almighty Creator is necessary, if He has in Himself the reason for His existence, then you conclude logically that God exists. If, on the other hand, God is not a necessary being, then He must have been made by another. Go back as far as you likeeventually you must come to a necessary being. 

Seventy-five years ago a group of atheists contended that the world had come together by a "fortuitous concurrence of atoms." This argument falls apart when you ask, "Who made the atoms?" "Who put them in motion?" Two other schools of confused thinking failed to reason logically crackled, and went up in smoke. One of these, influenced by the eighteenth-century French Revolution, held that God is not knowable from reason, but that knowledge of His existence depends on revelation. 

These were known as traditionalists. Another group of so-called philosophers adopted the opposite extreme. 

These opined that God is the direct object of all our knowledge and that we must discern other beings in God. 

They were known as ontologists. Others, unwilling to

accept the "evidence of things that appear not" (Hebrews 

11:1), asserted that the earth came from nature in the form of a "nebula." But they lost track of the origin of this "nebula." It could not have made itself. There had to be, then, a first cause who created all things out of nothing. That First Cause, clearly, unmistakably, is God the Creator.

ORDER OF THE UNIVERSE 

To the metaphysical proof, add the physical argument. 

The order of the universe, the adaptation of means to ends, the seasons of the year following one upon another, the separation of land and water, sun, moon, and stars, the millions of constellations moving about the heavens in perfect harmony, all point to a Supreme Intelligence who not only made this world, but who also directs and sustains it. 

Cicero (106-43 B. C.), famed Roman senator and pagan philosopher, declared: "When we look up to heaven and consider the heavenly bodies, what can be clearer and more obvious than that there is a Divinity, a most exalted Mind, by whom these orbs are ruled?" In fact, science points out more marvels in the order of creation in a minute than the atheist can explain in a century. 

PLATO'S VIEW 

Plato, the Greek philosopher (427?-347 B. C.), admitted the existence of God and other sublime truths which he deduced from the created universe. Plato referred to God as "He who is." He held that God created the world from the motive of benevolence. St.

Augustine (354-430 A. D.) interpreted Plato as main taining that the world of ideas exists in the mind of God.

HISTORY'S VERDICT 

Moreover, history records that all nations have worshiped a Supreme Being in some form or other. Even though they were often mistaken as to what constitutes the deity, they manifested their conviction that worship is due to a higher power than themselves. When, some years ago, scientists reported finding barbarous tribes that had no knowledge of God, further investigation revealed the fact that these barbarians did have this knowledge but did not allude to it in their public life. 

In the face of these proofs, atheism is untenable. It is true, however, that perverted education, corruption of morals, and the pride that blinds, have warped the intelligence of many who acknowledge no one to whom they are responsible. These, by the way, also have a deplorable superiority complex. They are practical, not theoretical atheists because the normal intellect of man can, unaided by revelation, arrive at the knowledge of God. 

SHALLOW THINKING 

Pantheists, who hold that all things are God, do not reason correctly because they do not advert to the fact that all things are defective; but no defect of any kind can coexist with the infinite perfection of God.

Agnostics, as the word connotes, pretend to know nothing on these vital subjects. Like atheists, agnostics admit no external directive for moral conduct. Hence their absurdities often lead to crimes and unspeakable

violations of the natural law which wreck their own lives and frequently the lives of others. "Whose end is destruction; whose God is their belly." (Philippians 3:19)

To refute them by logic, one could weigh the assertion of agnostics that no one knows anything on subjects of importance. Neither, then, can the agnostics know anything about their own claims.

LIVING AUTHORITY 

The First Vatican Council (1870) crystallizes the sound and unmistakable teaching of the Catholic Church on this subject. "The Holy, Catholic, Apostolic, Roman 

Church believes and confesses that there is one living and true God, Creator and Lord of heaven and earth, almighty, eternal, immense and incomprehensible, infinite in intellect and will and in all perfections; who, being one singular, altogether simple and unchangeable spiritual substance, is to be regarded as distinct in reality and essence from the world; most blessed in Himself and of

Himself, and unspeakably elevated above all things that exist or can be conceived" (Session 3, Chapter I). 

By way of example, one day a city official stopped me on the street. He was frustrated and on the brink of suicide. He admitted that he had no religion and could see no reason for living a life of constant suffering. As an opening wedge I asked: "Do you believe in God?" He countered: "Yes, I have a mind" - a perfect answer

because the rational creature, from the light of his own mind, can know that God exists. 

Other questions were in order: "Do you believe in Jesus Christ?" "Yes, I have to." "Why do you have to?" "Because it's history." "Do you believe that Jesus Christ was divine?" "Yes, because He performed miracles that required divine power, such as bringing the dead to life and giving sight to the blind. Even people who do not believe in the divinity of Christ agree that He was the holiest and best person who ever lived. To be such He would have to speak the truth at all times, and it's history that 

Christ claimed divinity for Himself.” "Do you accept the fact that Christ instituted seven sacraments?" "Yes." "For the same reason "Precisely," he answered. - an historical fact?" "Do you believe in the primacy of the Pope?" "Of course I do, because no organization can function without a head." "What in the world keeps you out of the Church?” "No one ever invited me."

This man found comfort and enlightenment in the 

Catholic Church.

The following related episode clarifies the above arguments. When a mere child, St. Jane Frances de Chantal silenced a blatant, shallow atheist who loudly boasted

his atheism. "Tell me, sir," asked Jane, "where does a hen come from?" Replied the atheist: "Why, from an egg, of course." "And whence the egg?" she asked. "Why from another hen." "But which was the first, the hen or the egg?" "The hen I suppose." "Then whence the hen?" The atheist did not have the honesty to admit "from God"; so he shrugged his shoulders.

A sophist of our day might pretend to dislodge the atheist by asserting, as do the evolutionists, that a first hen came from a lower species of fowl. This solves nothing. The next question then is: "Where did the fowl come from?" "From some other animal or plant life.”

The final query demands to know: "Whence came the first living thing?" Schooled in sophistry and deception, the atheist answers: "From an unknown atomic structure." 

REASON CHALLENGES 

But again the rational creature must resume: Who made the clod of earth or the nebula or the tiny atomic structure? And precisely what is it? It could not have made itself. One conclusion and only one can satisfy the mind of man: namely, the first Cause made all things out of nothing: Him we adore as God, the Creator.

THE WORD OF GOD 

While the light of reason makes known the existence of God, He is more perfectly known from revelation.

The truths contained in revelation are more certain because they rest on the omniscience and truthfulness of God. The knowledge we acquire is not always clear and conclusive. Divine testimony, on the other hand, makes

no mistakes, either in the subject matter or in the manner in which it is revealed. By means of the Bible and through the channels of Tradition, the Holy Spirit teaches us. Therefore, we say in the Apostles' Creed, "I believe in God," not only because we are guided by reason but because God has revealed His existence. We therefore have a corresponding duty to recognize God as our supreme good and our final goal. "The heavens show forth the glory of God: and the firmament declareth the work of His hands." (Psalm 18:1) "Before the mountains were made, or the earth and the world was formed; from eternity and to eternity Thou art God." 

St. Paul teaches the Hebrews: (Psalm 89:2) "For he that cometh to God must believe that

He is; and is a rewarder to them that seek Him." (Hebrews 11:6)

This definite instruction justifies baptizing people in cases of emergency if they express belief in God and in His reward of the good.

The Old Testament confirms the above logical conclusion. "All men are vain, in whom there is not the knowledge of God: and who by these good

things that are seen could not understand Him that is. Neither by attending to the works have acknowledged who was the workman.... For by the greatness of the beauty, and of the creature, the Creator of them may be seen, so as to be known thereby... . For if they were able to know so much as to make a judgment of the world, how did they not more easily find out the Lord thereof?" (Wisdom 13:1, 5, 9)

It is evident that the Sacred Writer had reference here to a knowledge of the true God, which is derived from created things.

St. Paul teaches that even after most men had ignored divine revelation, God still made Himself known to them through His mercy and benevolence. When addressing the people of Lystra, during the first Christian century, this brilliant educator stressed the sound reason that not only the learned but the unlettered can know our divine 

Creator from creatures: "We... preaching to you to be converted from these vain things [i. e., the worship of idols] to the living God, who made the heaven and the earth and the sea and all things that are in them: who in times past suffered all nations to walk in their own ways. Nevertheless He left not Himself without testimony, doing good from heaven, giving rains and fruitful seasons, filling our hearts with food and gladness." (Acts of the Apostles 14:14-16)

REJECTION OF GOD INEXCUSABLE 

The Apostle of the Gentiles challenged the pagan philosophers of his day because they suppressed the conviction of their intellects regarding the existence of God, so obvious from creation: "Because that which is known of God is manifest in them. For God hath manifested it unto them. For the invisible things of Him from the creation of the world are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made. His eternal power also and divinity; so that they are inexcusable. Because that, when they knew God, they have not glorified 

Him as God or given thanks.... And they changed the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of the image of a corruptible man....' 

(Romans 1:19-21, 23) 

St. Paul tore into these stuffy heathen hypocrites who deceived the people. He also reproached the Jews because divine revelation had been given to them. Ancient philosophers acquired the knowledge of a personal God, distinct from nature. If they had attained no such knowledge, they could not have been blamed for failure to recognize the existence of a supreme God. Knowing Him as their Almighty Creator, they had the duty to worship 

Him. God is perfectly justified in expecting gratitude, love and obedience from rational creatures whom He creates with intellect and free will and whom He continues to sustain. St. Paul denounced the philosophers

of his day for another reason. Notwithstanding their knowledge of the true God, they set up false idols for the people to worship and thus deprived them of the right to be taught the truth.

HEATHENS 

Theoretical atheism has no foundation on which to stand. Only "the fool hath said in his heart: "There is no God'" (Psalm 13:1). "Fool" here means a demented person. There are, however, pagans also-people who live as if there were no God and who want no God to whom to be responsible - who make the same denial.

Prescinding from the logical aspect, one vital truth explains our existence - God loves us. God is creative love. He speaks to mankind through the ancient patriarchs and prophets, through Tradition, and through His Son, our Lord Jesus Christ. These channels of truth comprise revealed religion, which focuses on the redemption of mankind, the central hinge of Christianity.

Jesus, the Son of God, suffered and died on the Cross to atone for the sins of the human race. Greater love than this no one has. He loved us first. He chose us. "He made us, and not we ourselves." 

(Psalm 99:3) 

He sustains us. 

That is why: "In Him we live, and move and are.” (Acts of the Apostles 17:28) 

He will love us last.

What would you think of children who ignored their parents? Next to God, we owe everything to our parents.

We owe God infinitely more. He sent the immortal soul to inform this clay element. We breathe His air, drink 

His water, depend on His food for necessary nourishment. We live on His time. "The earth is the Lord's and the fullness thereof: the world, and all they that dwell therein." (Psalm 23:1) 

One who constantly uses God's gifts and ignores the 

Giver defeats the purpose of living. 

The indifferent assert: "I don't go to church but I worship God in the privacy of my own room at home. 

Indeed, I often beg God for help." One should pray privately, but private prayer alone does not suffice. We owe God the best that Jesus made possible by permitting us to continue from "the rising of the sun till the going down" to offer the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass. When you go to church, you tell the whole world that you believe in God. 

While on earth, our divine Lord declared: "For he that shall be ashamed of Me, and of

My words, in this adulterous and sinful generation; the Son of Man also will be ashamed of him, when He shall come in the glory of 

His Father with the holy angels." (St. Mark 8:38)

DID GOD SPEAK?

Chapter II REVELATION 

N A wide, general sense, revelation - the unveil- - manifests or discloses what was unknown. heretofore.

IN Fing In a strict theological sense, revelation is the manifestation by God of Himself or His divine will, or whatever He decrees man should know. A few distinctions clarify different aspects of this subject.

Theologians teach that revelation may be natural in form, i. e., made known through visible creation. For example, from the created world around us we can deduce the omnipotence, goodness and wisdom of the divine Creator. Revelation is supernatural in form when 

God speaks to us either directly through Himself (as in a cloud to Moses) or indirectly, as to the Jewish people through His messengers, the prophets. Divine truths thus manifested are articles of faith to be accepted by Catholics because God has revealed them. 

Again, the subject matter of divine revelation may be natural truths knowable from nature, or supernatural those which cannot be discovered without the aid of revelation. Contrary to Catholic teaching, rationalists claim falsely that man needs no revelation. But to acquire a sufficiently developed knowledge of the truths of religion, man needed a supernatural revelation.

How can genuine revelation be screened from the spurious? By positive, negative, internal and external 23

signs. For instance, God must respect His own sanctity and hence cannot inspire anything contrary to reason or morally wrong. This is the test by which one may detect false revelation. 

Moreover, God never contradicts Himself or His former revelations. Therefore, whatever revelation pretends to oppose or condemn divine statements already madesuch as Christianity or any doctrines it teaches is definitely false. Miracles, extraordinary manifestations of

God's omnipotence, and prophecies or predictions free from natural causes, are considered substantial proof that the revelation results from divine intervention. Reflect that God, eternal truth, can have no part in falsehood.

God does not work miracles or inspire prophecies to promote error or immorality. God, Wisdom itself, employs the best means-miracles and prophecies- to make known His holy will to man.

Deists reject the fact of revelation on the plea it is unknowable. Indifferentists pretend that true revelation cannot be distinguished from a false one and, therefore, illogically conclude that it makes no difference what one believes.

PRIMITIVE 

In a chronological sense the manifestations of Almighty God to man may be divided into three revelations, Primitive, Patriarchal and Mosaic. Primitive supernatural religion began with the creation of man, because 

Adam and Eve received from God a revelation supernatural in form and substance-i. e., truths not acces-

REVELATION 

25 sible to rational man. God briefed Adam on how his body had been formed and united to his soul: "... till thou return to the earth, out of which thou wast taken: for dust thou art, and into dust thou shalt return." (Genesis 3:19) 

Likewise Eve acquired similar knowledge: "This now is bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh." (Genesis 2:23) 

PATRIARCHAL 

In His loving solicitude for mankind, God selected Abraham, of the tribe of Sem, to protect the supernatural truths already revealed and to announce the coming of the promised Messias. The racial background of Jesus had been especially blessed and sanctified. The Jewish people had been chosen for a sublime mission. When the Almighty assigned Abram to Chanaan, He encouraged him to leave his own country and to fulfill his destiny, having in mind salvation through our Saviour: "And I will make of thee a great nation, and 

I will bless thee.... In thee shall all the kindred of the earth be blessed." (Genesis 12:2, 3) 

This prediction was re-emphasized on behalf of Isaac, son of Abraham: "In him all the nations of the earth shall be blessed" (Genesis 18:18).

Jacob, the last of the patriarchs, when about to die, spoke to his son Juda and through him to the tribe of Juda:

"The sceptre shall not be taken away from

Juda, nor a ruler from his thigh, till He come that is to be sent; and He shall be the expectation of nations." 

The Pharisees rejected Jesus and imprecated His blood upon their children. This blasphemy met with condign punishment, in the destruction of Jerusalem (A. D. 70), when the Roman Legions under General Titus put to death the inhabitants of the city and reduced to ashes the celebrated temple which had stood for six centuries. 

THE MOSAIC REVELATION 

In His wisdom God entrusted to Moses the revelation contained in the original law and other guiding precepts. 

Some of these were fundamental and binding on all mankind, such as doctrines pertaining to the unity of God, the Creator, to the coming Messias, the immortality of the soul, the natural moral law as outlined in the ten commandments. Other parts of the Mosaic law related to the Jewish people. These included definite briefing on the moral law, the ritual for the people of Israel, sacred observances, and other objectives foreshadowing the coming of Jesus Christ.

St. Augustine refers to the Old Testament as "the veil of the New," and the New Testament as "the unveiling of the Old" (De Civitate Dei, XVI, 26). The law of 

Moses dealt with a double sanction: one regarding the temporal life; the other the life to come, i. e., eternal reward or eternal misery.

REVELATION 

27 In the plan of God the Mosaic law, as set forth in its declaration, was to be succeeded by the new law of the Messias, which it was His divine mission to bring to men. 

As a basic principle to guide our study, it is evident that God uses the most practical and surest means of proving the authenticity of His messengers that is, miracles and prophecies. These did not require the expert technique of a few scholars to pass on them; the testimony of the senses sufficed.

The doctrines of the Catholic Church are based on Scripture and Tradition. The word "Scripture" includes the books of the Old and the New Testament. 

Because both Scripture and Tradition contain the doctrines of our religion, we refer to them as sources of faith or “the fountainheads." They are also called rules of faith, in view of the fact that our blessed Saviour made the Catholic Church His divinely commissioned teaching authority. "And Jesus coming, spoke to them, saying: 'All power is given to Me in heaven and in earth. Going therefore, teach ye all nations; baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Ghost. Teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you. And behold I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world.'" (St. Matthew 28:18-20)

TRADITION 

The Bible is not the sole source of the doctrines of our faith. There is also another fountainhead of divinely revealed truths known as Tradition. The word "tradition" stems from the Latin word "tradere," which means to hand over; and from its Greek equivalent, "tradesis."

In a theological sense, Tradition means the transmission of the truths of salvation by the teaching authority of the Church.

In a more restricted sense, Tradition may be defined as the transmission of truths or precepts over and above those found in the Sacred Scriptures. The Bible is the written revelation, and Tradition - that is, the doctrines and institutions handed down orally from the time of our Lord to the present is the unwritten revelation. 

Our Lord Jesus Christ delivered the glad tidings of salvation through the spoken word, and so the Apostles continued to preach by word of mouth. History records that the Gospel of St. Matthew was written after the 

Ascension of Christ. The Apostles, who heard the command of Christ to go and teach all nations, did have recourse to certain written instructions; but no one of the 

Apostles pretended to commit the entire Church doctrine to writing. The fact that some of the disciples of our 

Lord committed to writing certain doctrines in the form of epistles does not imply that this was the sole source of Christian teaching. St. John, the last of the Evangelists, concludes his Gospel: "There are also many other things which Jesus did, which, if they were written every one,

REVELATION 

29 the world itself, I think, would not be able to contain the books that should be written." (St. John 21:25)

It is evident that not all the doctrines which were entrusted to the Church were written down. In fact, substantial arguments prove the contrary.

You will not find in the Bible any statement regarding which Books are canonical or any assertion that the works of the New Testament are inspired. Those, therefore, who deny Tradition would have no grounds to stand on as regards the inspiration of the writings of the Apostles. Only Tradition proves the inspiration of the writings in the Bible.

We must also acknowledge that the Bible, far from being the sole source of truth, does not include sufficient knowledge of the truths of salvation. For example, the

Bible does not mention the baptism of infants or the number of the sacraments. Those false religions that refuse to accept Tradition still cling to and rely on the Apostles' Creed as a foundation. This, in itself, is a virtual acknowledgment of Tradition and also admits, in effect, that the Bible cannot be the sole rule of faith. 

Our motive for credibility, the reason for believing, is the authority and truthfulness of God. He speaks to man when He communicates with him through His representatives by word of mouth. For the first four centuries the great mass of Christians had to rely on the truths of salvation taught them by word of mouth. If the Apostles had not written down certain doctrines, the

early Christians would still have been blessed with the faith. The faith they held was, and is, of obligation. 

St. Paul expressly exhorts Christians to accept the unwritten traditions of the Apostles in the same way as their written words. "Therefore, brethren, stand fast; and hold the traditions which you have learned, whether by word or by our epistle" (2 Thessalonians 2:14). The obligation, therefore, to believe oral tradition existed before the Scriptures. Even after the Bible came into existence, the truths taught orally continued in full force. 

The acceptance of these truths, handed down by Tradition, is an obligation binding all Catholics the same today as they have through these nineteen hundred years. 

We have a duty to believe, not only the actual word, but the sense, of the Bible. Few people know the ancient languages and the framework in which they were written. 

The meaning intended requires study by experts. We have a solemn duty to accept Tradition, which is a necessary adjunct to enlighten and aid us in comprehending the Scriptures.

THE BIBLE 

We are assured by divine authority that the Sacred Books of the Bible contain the word of God. The Bible, especially the New Testament, was written by men of deep faith and piety who confirmed their teachings by miracles and thus proved themselves to be messengers of 

God. Therefore, under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, they taught and wrote the word of God. 

Keep in mind the distinction between the Bible and books on Catholic doctrine. Treatises on Christian doc-

REVELATION 

31 trine, catechisms, theological works, and spiritual guides may contain the word of God, but they are not divine revelation. The Sacred Scriptures are the word of God, written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, having God for their author. The Bible is a letter addressed to us by Almighty God. To prove that such a letter is authentic, one must show the authorship.

It is the teaching of the Catholic Church that God is the author of the Bible. God so inspired and guided the human writers and kept them from error that He may justly be called the author of the Bible. The Evangelists may be called the human instruments who taught and wrote the word of God under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. 

To use an old example, picture a great artist playing an organ. He really produces the music played, while the pipes of the organ with their particular tone values and ranges (writer's style) are the human writers. Again, we turn to St. Paul: "All Scripture, inspired of God, is profitable to teach, to reprove, to correct, to instruct in justice." (2 Timothy 3:16) 

Here St. Paul has in mind the books of the Old Testament and values them especially because of their inspiration.

The entire record of Christian antiquity, as well as the decrees of the Council of Florence and of the Vatican Council of 1869-1870, teach the divine inspiration

of both the Old and the New Testament. The early 

Fathers of the Church in the first, second and third centuries referred to the Sacred Scriptures as the spoken, dictated, written word of God. Those who would not accept the Scriptures as the word of God were held to be infidels. The Apostles and their successors, who received authority from our divine Saviour and who were entrusted with what is known as the "deposit of faith,' decided which Books in the Old and New Testaments are to be held authentic. The expression used to designate authentic Books is "canonical," that is, standard 

Books; this excludes thirteen Books labeled as apocryphal, or doubtful. Many non-Catholic denominations retain the apocryphal writings and exclude some of the canonical Books.

The Books of the Bible were not assembled in the form in which we now have them until the fourth century. 

Jesus Christ called a group of fishermen, His Apostles, to whom He told the story of salvation, and He commanded them to tell that story to the world just as He had told it to them.

The Supreme Court of the United States interprets the meaning and application of the Constitution. The 

Catholic Church, which is divinely commissioned by Jesus Christ, is the Supreme Court for interpreting the Bible and Tradition.

Chapter III THE TEACHING AUTHORITY OF THE CHURCH 

TH HE Church encourages the reading of the Bibleappropriate passages are read every Sunday at all

Masses. Priests and religious read or chant parts of it every day. The Church does not permit private interpretation of the Bible because of the great danger of error. 

To intelligently interpret the Bible one would have to be equipped with understanding of the ancient languages together with the various dialects in which they were written. Only expert scholars could research the background of these writings to determine with what fidelity translations have been made.

On the other hand, Catholics are obliged to accept the articles of faith proposed for their belief. They learn the teachings of the Church by a systematic course of instruction. Neither Scripture nor Tradition can be the sole rule of faith. The rule of faith is the standard which determines and regulates our faith. To be sure, what we believe must conform to the Bible and Tradition. These fountainheads can be called rules of faith; but to interpret the expression strictly, the rule of faith is that which guides our understanding of the Scriptures and Tradition. A supreme, living teaching authority, the 

Church, constitutes the rule of faith. Practical Catholics reverence and obey this teaching authority. 35

THE SUPREme Court 

We have the same system in the Constitution of the United States: a Supreme Court rules on the meaning and interpretation of each phrase in the Constitution. We recognize and follow its interpretation. The absolute requirements of the rule of faith can be found only in the teaching office of the Catholic Church. There has to be an authority which is competent to direct us in making clear the sources of our faith. This divinely commis. sioned authority of the Church, the guardian of truth, preserves the deposit of faith and the unity of the Church. 

Without such a divinely appointed authority to guard the deposit of faith and the unity of the Church, confusion and divisions and subdivisions of creeds would result. 

Witness the scandal caused by the sects that have rejected Mother Church. To be effective, the rule of faith must be outward, that is, visible. It must function so as to preclude dangers to the faith from whatever source could compromise or destroy the unity essential to the Church. 

Such functioning could be verified only in the living visible authority which interprets the Bible and Tradition, and knows how to apply them in concrete cases. Now, to complete the related logical reasoning, the rule of faith must be infallible that is, supported by the indwelling and consequent guidance of the Holy Spirit. 

PROTECTION AGAINST ERROR 

To be workable and effective, a rule of faith must have the infallibility of the Church behind it; then only can it decide important matters of faith in such a way

THE TEACHING AUTHORITY OF THE CHURCH 

37 as to preserve integrity. Only an infallible authority can secure the bond of the spirit and the unity of faith. 

Over nineteen hundred years of experience verify the inerrancy of the Church in the discharge of her teaching office. This we also know from the promises of Christ, who assured His Apostles and their successors of His perpetual protection: "Behold I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world." (St. Matthew 28:20) 

It follows that our Saviour Jesus Christ could not fulfill His promise to be with the teaching body of the Church unless He so guided it that His doctrines would be taught without error. This, in fact, is the foundation of Christian life, and the basis of firm hope. "Now, faith is the substance of things to be hoped for, the evidence of things that appear not." (Hebrews 11:1) 

Moreover, Jesus promised through the Apostles and their successors an Advocate in the Third Person of the Blessed Trinity: "I will ask the Father: and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you forever, the Spirit of truth.... (St. John 14:16-17)

The Holy Spirit fulfills His divine mission to enlighten and guide the Church, to preserve the faith and

to continue the teaching of divine truths to the end of time. The Holy Spirit will remain with the Church forever. Recall the promise of Christ to build His Church upon an imperishable rock: ee ...

Upon this rock [that is to say, upon this visible foundation] I will build My Church. 

And the gates of hell [that is, the hell] shall not prevail against it.” power of (St. Matthew 16:18)

If the insidious machinations of the enemies of Christ could undermine and destroy the faith, the foundation of the Catholic Church, the promises of Christ, would not be fulfilled. St. Paul in his letter to his co-worker St. 

Timothy refers to the Church as the "pillar and ground” of truth (1 Timothy 3:15). Pillar and ground denote stability, and Mother Church could not be firm and persevering in the truth unless she taught it with infallible authority. The Apostles were so convinced of this fact that they issued decrees binding all the faithful: "It hath seemed good to the Holy Ghost and to us" (Acts of the 

Apostles 15:28). What is predicated of the Apostles must also be predicated of their successors, since Christ gave both the same mission and the same promise. 

We are therefore justified in concluding that the living teaching authority of the Catholic Church constitutes the rule of faith. Consequently, as regards interpretation of the Scripture and the Church's teaching, Christians are bound to believe all that God has revealed and all that the Church proposes for their belief, whether or not it

THE TEACHING AUTHORITY OF THE CHURCH 

39 be found in the Bible, because all the sources or fountainheads of faith are entrusted to the custody of the Church.

As a corollary, we are bound to have faith not only in God but in the truths of revelation proposed to us by the Catholic Church, whether in ordinary teaching or by extraordinary definition, such as the definition of the 

Immaculate Conception in 1854. Another example is the more recent definition of 1950 which declares the 

Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary into heaven an article of faith. It is also logical to conclude that a dogma that is to say, an established truth revealed by Almighty God and taught by the Church-carries the corresponding obligation of acceptance on the part of the faithful. 

DOGMAS 

Let me relate to you an experience of many years ago which throws light on this subject. A prominent doctor of medicine asserted that it was impossible for his mind to accept any dogma whatever. I pointed out to him that his position was illogical; that a dogma is nothing more than an established truth. There are dogmas in medicine, dogmas in law, dogmas in engineering, and in every branch of the sciences. Rather than append a number of footnotes, it seems practical to add here that the principal dogmas of the Catholic Church are included in various creeds: 

NAMES OF THE CREEDS 

(1) The Apostles' Creed, so called because it was composed by the Apostles, dates from the first century

and is the most ancient of all formulas or symbols summarizing Catholic doctrine. The Apostles' Creed is recited daily by Catholics. 

(2) The Nicene Creed emanated from the First 

Council of Nicaea (325 A. D.). It refuted the Arian heresy and at the same time stressed the divinity of Christ. 

(3) The First Council of Constantinople (381 A. D.) 

issued its own creed in refutation of the Macedonian heresy. It supplements the Nicene Creed and re-emphasizes the divinity of the Holy Spirit, condemning those who deny this dogma. The Creed that we say at Mass is actually the Nicene Creed as revised at Constantinople, and is sometimes referred to as the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed. (4) The Creed of St. Athanasius (d. 373) is named after this saint because he ably defended the doctrines of the Blessed Trinity and the Incarnation of Jesus Christ.

The Athanasian Creed was accepted by both Greeks and Latins. It has been universally recognized by the teaching authority of the Church. In the Roman breviary, which priests recite every day, the Athanasian Creed forms part of the prayers of the Divine Office. (5) Another creed, known as the Lateran profession of faith, was sponsored by the Fourth Lateran Council (A. D. 1215) under Pope Innocent III. It defends the dogma of the Blessed Trinity. (6) Pope Pius IV, in the year of 1565, authored the profession of faith of the Council of Trent to refute

THE TEACHING AUTHORITY OF THE CHURCH 

41 the errors of the so-called reformers of the sixteenth century. (7) Following the Vatican Council of 1869-1870, Pope Pius IX declared the doctrines of the primacy and infallibility of the Pope.

Chapter IV  

AVING determined by the light of reason and from HA the Bible the existence of God, the study of the nature of the Supreme Being follows. 

Recall the answer given by our divine Lord to the question posed by the Samaritan woman. She asked whether God should be adored on Mount Garizim or in

Jerusalem. Jesus replied: 

"The hour cometh and now is, when the true adorers shall adore the Father in spirit and in truth." (St. John 4:23)

PURE SPIRIT 

God is pure spirit, which means a simple and immaterial substance, intelligent and perfectly free. Man, composed of body and soul, is not a pure spirit. The word "pure" signifies a spirit altogether independent of material. Because He is pure spirit, the adoration of God could not rightly be restricted or confined to one particular place. In a word, worship without limit of time or space is due Almighty God. Other attributes of God are His immensity, unchangeableness, and immortality. 

Although our finite minds cannot grasp the perfections 42

of God because they are infinite and incomprehensible, still we can catch some tiny rays from this radiant light. 

Four of the divine attributes point to His transcendent majesty always in contrast to our insignificance namely, His wisdom, power, holiness and goodness. God made all the laws that regulate the universe. The Bible describes God as holding the universe poised in the hollow of His hand. Indeed, before His Divine Majesty "... the whole world is as... a drop of the morning dew that falleth down upon the earth" (Wisdom 11:23). "GOD IS LOVE"

Inasmuch as the supreme wisdom of God designed, created and constantly sustains the order of the universe, including all human beings, it follows that God must be intelligent, free and personal, apart from the forces of nature. God alone can be called infinitely perfect, because He possesses every perfection in its highest degree without limitation. Hence, instead of saying God is wise, we say God is Wisdom itself—God is power, beauty. 

God is love.

When Moses inquired of the Almighty His name, God answered: "I AM WHO Aм.... Thus shalt thou say to the children of Israel: 'He who is, hath sent me to you.'" 

(Exodus 3:14) 

Here our divine Lord, who exists in Himself and is the first cause of all creation, attributes to Himself the sum total of perfection, being without limit to distinguish

Himself from all other things which have their limitations. Every perfection stems from the divine essence. 

ATTRIBUTES TWOFOLD 

This treatise does not pretend to cover the subject completely. Briefly, and for clarification, let us refer to 

God's attributes as twofold: a. Some pertain to His divine essence; b. Others relate to His activity, such as His intelligence and will.

While being and action are identical in God, our limited minds make a distinction between them. Thus we speak of the divine essence as unchangeable, eternal, immense and omnipresent. The Sacred Scriptures confirm this reasoning. "In the beginning, O Lord, Thou foundedst the earth: and the heavens are the works of 

Thy hands. They shall perish.... But Thou art always the selfsame: and Thy years shall not fail." (Psalm 101:26-27, 28)

One may not consider God's eternity in terms of seconds or a succession of instants or changes. In the existence of God there is neither past nor future — only the present. Otherwise, He would not be unchangeable or infinite. 

In considering the question: If there were no universe, would God still be immense and omnipresent, let us first define the terms.

Immensity is that divine attribute by which God cannot be measured or contained within the limits of place and space. This perfection, belonging to or identical with the divine essence, would be predicated of God even if He had created nothing. The other attribute, omnipresence, indicates the result of immensity and implies the actual presence of God in visible creation. For example: the sun constantly shines but only those objects within its orbit are illuminated. So, while God's immensity requires that He be everywhere, He is actually present only in the things that exist. 

Is God present in rational creatures? Yes - and in three ways: by His knowledge, comprehending all things; by His divine will, sustaining all things; and by His essence, pervading all things, yet entirely distinct from the creature.

In regard to the Divine Intelligence, Omniscience, and Wisdom, God from eternity knows all things that exist and all that can exist just as they are- by a single act and at the same instant. His knowledge is without limit. It covers past, present, future and includes the free action of His creatures. "Neither is there any creature invisible in His sight: but all things are naked and open to 

His eyes.... Again: 

(Hebrews 4:13) "Thou hast understood my thoughts afar off: my path and my line Thou hast searched out." 

(Psalm 138:3)

KNOWLEDGE AND WISDOM 

God's infinite knowledge extends to the future and even to the future conditional — i. e., what would or could happen under a given circumstance. "Woe to thee, Corozain! Woe to thee, Bethsaida! For if in Tyre and

Sidon had been wrought the miracles that have been wrought in you, they had long ago done penance in sackcloth and ashes" (St. Matthew 11:21).

Supreme wisdom is an attribute of God, because He knows how to order and dispose all things to attain their ends. "How great are Thy works, O Lord! Thou hast made all things in wisdom" (Psalm 103:24). 

Regarding His divine will, God is almighty and free; infinitely holy and just; bountiful, merciful and longsuffering; truthful and faithful to His promises. Without any doubt Holy Scripture proclaims that God can do all things by an act of His will. His very omnipotence, and freedom in exercising it, give security to us, His children. Indeed we have reason to trust implicitly and to exercise the utmost confidence in the manifest and loving providence of God.

FATHER OF MERCIES 

We poor creatures are comforted and privileged to think of God as our loving Father of mercies, who has compassion on our infirmities. 

Because of our limited intellectual powers, we cannot describe in anything like adequate terms the attributes or perfections of God. They are infinite and, therefore,

God is incomprehensible - i. e., beyond our understanding. "Behold, God is great, exceeding our knowledge" (Job 36:26). 

Applied to God, the word "ineffable" means that the perfections of God cannot be fully expressed, because they are too lofty for language to reach. When, therefore, we try to use words to designate the perfections of God, we do not use them in exactly their literal sense, but rather as an analogy.

Keep in mind that while analogies help, our knowledge of God is based on faith and, in a lesser degree, on reason.

ESSENTIALS - ACCIDENTALS 

In describing creatures, we distinguish between essence and attributes or qualities which grow from the essence. For example, creatures are composed of body and soul. While these are essential, accidental qualities such as health, culture, learning, adaptability, may vary. 

In God, however, there are no such distinctions. Theologians employ the term "metaphysical essence" to describe that perfection of God from which all other perfections derive.

THE FIRST VATICAN COUNCIL 

The Vatican Council proclaimed the attributes of 

God: "The Holy, Catholic, Apostolic, Roman Church believes and confesses that there is one living and true 

God, Creator and Lord of heaven and earth, almighty, eternal, immense, and incomprehensible, infinite in intellect and will and in all perfections; who, being one sin-

gular, absolutely simple, and unchangeable spiritual substance, is to be regarded as distinct in reality and essence, from the world; most blessed in and from Himself, and unspeakably elevated above all things that exist or can be conceived, outside Himself" (1870, Session 3, Chapter I). 

Theologians further describe these attributes under two heads: the quiescent and the operative. The quiescent attributes refer to God's being, the operative to His modes of acting. No matter how long we may have studied, we hark back to the definition learned in the first grade: "God is a pure spirit, infinitely perfect."

Our teachers told us that a spirit has no material body; that a thing is perfect if it has no defect; that "infinite" signifies not restricted by space or time. God alone is infinite.

ESSENCE OF BEING 

When the Almighty appeared to Moses in the burning bush, the patriarch asked Him: "If they say to me: 'What is His name?' what shall I say to them?" God replied to Moses, "I AM WHO AM" - which means the essence of being. 

Note an important distinction between human knowledge, which is discursive because it passes from one truth to another; and divine truths, which are not discursive. Our faltering human intelligence, of necessity, proceeds in a slow and laborious manner. By a process of reasoning, our minds assemble available truths into a systematic whole. God is referred to as an absolutely simple substance. This means that in Him there is not

a combination of parts, as you find in created beings. 

For example, in the soul of man there is a distinction between acquired perfections and the substance itself. 

The soul is one thing; the wisdom which it may possess through the experience of years is something else. The mind is one thing and the act of thinking something else. 

Prudence is one virtue of the soul, justice another. Not so, however, in describing the divinity, because God's essence is identical with His being. In a word, you can say God's goodness is, in itself, His justice. The effects of goodness are different from those of justice, because of the limitations of our minds. 

St. Paul exclaims: "O the depth of the riches of the wisdom and of the knowledge of God! How incomprehensible are His judgments, and how unsearchable His ways!" A brief commentary brings out the homage due 

God from His creatures. "O the depths" is to be understood in the sense of immensity. "Of the riches" refers to the infinite mercy, the amazing goodness, of our divine Benefactor. "Of the wisdom." Here we ponder God's wisdom and knowledge in their operation, bestowing countless blessings on man. "How incomprehensible" - beyond our power to understand. "Unsearchable" means not possible for us to investigate. "Judgments,... ways" - the eternal designs of God, "who orders all things sweetly," and their wonderful accomplishment.

Chapter V THE BLESSED TRINITY 

HE Athanasian Creed reaffirms the Catholic belief 

THE 

in the mystery of the Holy Trinity. Because God revealed this truth we know its existence, but we do not comprehend it. Our limited intelligence cannot understand how the one individual nature of God subsists in 

Three Persons. The Three Persons are not three Gods but only one God. Reason cannot prove the existence of the Trinity. We accept it on the infallible word of God. 

This doctrine is accurately outlined by St. Athanasius (d. 373) in the creed which bears his name.

ONE IN DIVINITY 

"The Catholic faith is this: that we honor one God in Trinity and Trinity in Unity. Neither confounding the Persons nor separating the Substance. For there is one Person of the Father, another of the Son, another of the Holy Ghost. But the divinity of the Father, the 

Son, and the Holy Ghost, is one, the glory equal, the majesty co-eternal. Such is the Father, such is the Son, such the Holy Ghost. The Father is uncreated, the Son is uncreated, the Holy Ghost is uncreated.... The Father is God, the Son is God, the Holy Ghost is God... and yet there are not three Gods but one God.... The

Father is not made by anyone, nor created nor born. 

The Son is of the Father alone; not made, nor created 50

THE BLESSED TRINITY 

51 but born. The Holy Ghost is of the Father and of the 

Son, not made nor created nor born but proceeding." 

THREE PERSONS

The above throws light on the doctrine of the Blessed 

Trinity. You know the real distinction between the Three

Persons, but you will also note that no distinction is mentioned between any of the Persons and their divine nature; and each of the Persons is God, whole and entire. 

There are not three Gods but only one God. It would be contrary to reason to assert that Three Persons are one Person, or that there are Three Persons and yet there are not Three Persons. But, it is no contradiction to state that these Three Persons have one nature. In a word, contradiction is ruled out because the words "three" and "one" do not refer to the same thing. "Three" refers to persons, "one" refers to divinity. It should be observed, too, that the nature or essence makes a being what it is. Thus the nature of God is the union of all perfection. A person, on the other hand, is described as an individual substance in a rational nature. 

There is no contradiction in declaring that the same individual substance is a triple principle, the three principles in God having the same perfection in common. 

Now you may ask, By what authority do you believe in the doctrine of the Blessed Trinity? We believe it on the authority of God Himself. "The Lord hath said to Me: 'Thou art My 

Son; this day have I begotten Thee.'' 

(Psalm 2:7)

"From the womb before the day star I begot

Thee." 

(Psalm 109:3) 

St. Paul uses both of these quotations.

DIVINE REVELATION 

The New Testament clearly reveals the doctrine of the Blessed Trinity. While this doctrine was foreshadowed in the Old Testament, it is explicit in the New. 

The fact of the Blessed Trinity is a mystery of our religion. No one presumes to explain this mystery; nevertheless, we are enriched and enlightened by knowing of its existence. For example, no one has ever defined electricity or the X-ray because no one knows their essence. 

We do, however, profit by their use.

When our Saviour received baptism in the Jordan: "The Holy Ghost descended in a bodily shape, as a dove, upon Him. And a voice came from heaven: "Thou art My beloved Son. In 

Thee I am well pleased." (St. Luke 3:22)

After the Last Supper, our divine Lord spoke thus: "I will ask the Father: and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you forever: the Spirit of Truth." (St. John 14:16-17)

THE BLESSED TRINITY 

53 "I ascend to My Father and to your Father, to 

My God and your God." (St. John 20:17)

THE CHARITY OF GOD 

In these texts observe the names of the Three Persons of the Blessed Trinity, evidently distinct from one another.

To further clarify this doctrine, consider the word "spiritus," from the Latin, meaning "a breath." ("Spirare" to breathe.) We refer to God the Father as unbegotten; God the Son was begotten of the Father, and 

God the Holy Ghost proceeds from both. In a word, between God the Father and God the Son there is a constant breathing back and forth of divine love, and that is the Holy Spirit.

We pray for our readers St. Paul's words to the Corinthians: "The grace of our Lord Jesus Christ and the charity of God, and the communication of the Holy Ghost be with you all. Amen" (2 Corinthians 13:13).

Chapter VI  

IN 'N THE Apostles' Creed we profess: "I believe in God, the Father Almighty, Creator of heaven and earth." 

God created heaven and earth for His own glory and for our eternal happiness. This fact gives an objective to religion. "He made us, and not we ourselves" (Psalm 

99:3). Lovingly He cares for us and sustains us every second of our lives.

The word "create" means to make out of nothing or to use or assemble out of pre-existing material originally produced by God. Creation belongs to God alone. 

Man can invent, discover, modify, research, but not create. Reason refutes naturalists, evolutionists, materialists, and other theorists who assert that the elements forming the earth always existed. Elements which are changeable cannot be self-existing and eternal.

Divine revelation instructs us that in the beginning God created heaven and earth, which were void and empty. In learning the true doctrine of creation, you distinguish between the divine Creator and the matter and force of the universe. God, therefore, is personal, distinct from what He created, and in no way identified with things. The phrase "heaven and earth" refers to the universe. Taken in a spiritual sense, heaven is the abode of saints and angels rewarded with everlasting happiness. In a material sense, heaven, or the heavens, 54

consists of the firmament, the space between the sky and the earth, the sun, moon, and stars. The globe on which we live is properly called the earth.

The great Greek philosopher Plato in his writings of the fifth century before Christ bequeathed to mankind irrefutable arguments against materialism. He considered the material world necessary, but he taught that a wise God had put it in order. Holy Scripture confirms this fundamental truth: "In the beginning God created heaven, and earth. And the earth was void and empty, and darkness was upon the face of the deep. And the spirit of God moved over the waters. And God said: 'Be light made.' And light was made." (Genesis 1:1-3) 

"BE LIGHT MADE" 

The words "in the beginning" indicate that no event had happened before. Our divine Lord taught that He had glory with His Father before the world was (St. 

John 17:5). Time is the measure of succession in things that change. Before the creation of the world nothing changed because with the start of the world, time began. 

Moreover, Genesis employs the word "create" in its original Hebrew meaning, ascribing creation to God alone. Before the divine act of creation "the earth was void and empty," mere chaos. The words "Be light made" point to God's unlimited power.

Divine Wisdom directs all things to their best end. 

To answer the question: What force impelled God to

create the world? nothing impelled God because He acted freely. "Whatsoever the Lord pleased He hath done.” 

(Psalm 134:6) WISDOM AND POWER 

God, of His own almighty wisdom and power, drew the blueprints, the plans and specifications for the universe. The word "universe" includes the heavens and the earth and all they contain. By creation God intended the immediate good of intelligent creatures for whom all other things are made: "And everyone that calleth upon My name, I have created him for My glory: I have formed him and made him.” (Isaias 43:7)

God not only created the world, He continues to uphold it. 

If He ceased for one moment to preserve the world, it would terminate. This truth, confirmed by reason, explains St. Paul's reference: "By Him all things consist.' 

(Colossians 1:17)

As a corollary, the Almighty Creator provides the materials which enable man to make progress. It must also be evident that our divine Lord lavished on mankind ample means for spiritual progress and security. Not-

withstanding its complex marvelous machinery, the airplane could not function effectively were it not borne by the air. In a word, man may discover or research to improve and perfect his inventions, but man does not create. 

Creation belongs to God alone. 

THE SIX DAYS 

The Book of Genesis, written under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, having God for its author, informs us that on the first day of creation God made light; on the second day, the firmament; on the third day, dry land and plants; on the fourth day, the sun, moon and stars; on the fifth day, fishes and birds; on the sixth day, brute animals and rational man. On the seventh day God rested, which means that He discontinued the work of creation.

Regarding the manner in which the author of the Book of Genesis obtained knowledge of creation, we do not know. Obviously the account rests on revealed truth. 

Research verifies the Biblical statement that life on earth developed as God intended.

POPE LEO XIII 

There are, of course, many theories still unproved, most of them untenable. Pseudo-scientists reject the entire Book of Genesis in an effort to explain the creation of the world, but they offer no conclusive proof. In fact they differ widely among themselves. Until recent times, the literal meaning had support from a vast amount of

authority.

In his encyclical Providentissimus Deus (On the Study of Holy Scripture, 1893) Pope Leo XIII taught that if anyone had a good reason to depart from the literal meaning, he could do so.

Some scientists incline to the periodic theory, which interprets the six days as indefinite in point of time. 

Certainly, the Almighty Creator was not restricted by our solar system. Each one of the days of creation could have been ten thousand years, for nothing is impossible with God. The extent of the six days of creation is not known. 

The solar system got under way on the fourth day. The 

Church holds no brief from her divine Founder to solve the problems of science. This much is certain-the pretensions of would-be scientists have not refuted the Book of Genesis. Moreover, the Almighty Creator does not expect us to pry into His secrets: "For who hath known the mind of the Lord? 

Or who hath been His counselor?"

THE ANGELS 

(Romans 11:34) 

The word "angel" comes from the Greek and signifies a messenger. When the Bible states: "In the beginning God created heaven, and earth" (Genesis 1:1), the word "heaven" means not only the place but all its inhabitants. The Nicene Creed declares that God is the

Maker of all things, visible and invisible, material and spiritual. The prophet Daniel (7:10) envisions "ten thousand times a hundred thousand" standing before the throne of God.

Angels are pure spirits - i. e., independent of matter both in their substance and their actions. The angels were created brilliant and with immediate intuitive knowledge. They are sent as messengers from God to man.

Whatever powers have been conceded to the angels are exclusively under the control of their divine Creator. 

Solomon prayed: ... render to everyone according to his ways, which Thou knowest him to have in his heart (for Thou only knowest the hearts of the children of men)." (2 Paralipomenon 6:30)

Even though the angels are superior to us, of a higher level of creation, they do not know our inmost thoughts, nor the future. From the writings of St. Paul, however, it would seem that the angels have means of communicating thoughts to one another. St. Paul refers to "tongues... of angels" (1 Corinthians 13:1). St. Thomas Aquinas taught that each individual angel differs specifically from all the rest. 

Nine choirs of angels are mentioned in the Bible.

These nine choirs consist of three hierarchies or divisions, listed as angels, archangels and princedoms or principalities; powers, virtues, and dominations; thrones, cherubim and seraphim.

From the very time of their creation, Almighty God saw fit to endow the angels with sanctifying grace.

Nevertheless, they were put through a test of obedience

to the commands of God. Those who obeyed were admitted to the happiness of heaven. When little children clustered about Him, Jesus said: "e ... their angels in heaven always see the face of My Father who is in heaven." (St. Matthew 18:10)

Commentators on the Sacred Scriptures opine that a third of the angels rebelled through pride. These, under the leadership of Lucifer, were cast out of heaven: "I saw Satan like lightning falling from (St. Luke 10:18) heaven."

Hell was made for those angels who proved unfaithful and defiant. They were condemned to "everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels" (St. 

Matthew 25:41). 

The question is asked, Can fire torment spirits? If it cannot torment spirits naturally, it could receive supernatural power to do so, because nothing is impossible with God. So far as their eternal status is concerned, good and bad angels resemble good and bad men. 

Holy Writ indicates a guardian angel for every soul. "Are they not all ministering spirits, sent to minister for them who shall receive the inheritance of salvation?" (Hebrews 1:14) 

The Book of Tobias enlightens us on this truth. The guardian angel of Tobias assumed a human form. Our

guardian angels pray for us as the archangel Raphael did for Tobias: "... I offered thy prayer to the Lord." (Tobias 12:12)

The angels inspire us to do good. They protect us against evil, both spiritual and physical. "For He hath given His angels charge over thee, to keep thee in all thy ways." 

(Psalm 90:11) 

The fallen angels are permitted to tempt us, that we may gain merit by resisting, because God knows how to draw good out of evil. From the Book of Job we learn that evil spirits cannot tempt us without God's permission. 

And no one is tempted beyond his strength: "God is faithful, who will not suffer you to be tempted above that which you are able: but will make also with temptation issue, that you may be able to bear it." (1 Corinthians 10:13) 

THE CREATION OF MAN 

From the Bible we learn: (a) man's origin; (b) his nature; (c) his supernatural elevation; (d) his fall. By way of preface, let us recall what we learned in the first grade. "Why did God make you?" Answer: "God made me to know Him, to love Him, and to serve Him in this life so as to be happy with Him forever in heaven."

Note that knowledge comes first- "to know Him” and then to love Him. Knowledge and love inspire service.

A number of years ago one of the highest-paid columnists in the United States expressed his opinion in a syndicate serving more than six hundred daily papers. "If anyone could discover whence man came," he asserted, "why he was living, and whither he was destined, he would make the first page of every newspaper in the 

United States." This absurd drivel, unworthy of a thinking man, reveals a lack of faith and knowledge. The answer to that question has made the first page of every primer catechism used in our parochial schools, where the children learn, along with their A B C's, why they are living; who created them; and to whom they are finally responsible. The child has a right to these fundamentals. 

The correct answers can be accepted by all rational people irrespective of creed.

The immortal soul of man is sent by God to inform this clay element. Here again there are many theories as to the precise moment when the immortal soul takes up its abode. Catholic theologians teach that the soul is created by Almighty God and infused at the moment of conception. The Bible indicates deliberation, so to speak, on the part of God before the creation of man: "Let us make man to Our image and likeness; and let him have dominion over the fishes of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and the beasts, and the whole earth, and every creeping creature that moveth upon the earth. And

the Lord God formed man of the slime of the earth, and breathed into his face the breath of life; and man became a living soul." (Genesis 1:26; 2:7) 

Therefore, all men are descended from Adam. No one can read the Scriptures intelligently and accept the unproved theories of evolutionists and others on the subject. 

Catholic dogma does not enter the field of science to determine the age of mankind on earth, or to assign the actual dates covering events of the Old Testament. 

Ancient manuscripts and the scientific interpretation of the Scriptures are not all in accord. St. Jerome (340?- 

420), one of the great scriptural authorities, reckoned 3,941 years from the creation of Adam to the birth of 

Christ; St. Clement of Alexandria (150?-?220) counted 

6,621. The Roman Martyrology indicates 5,519 years.

History shows that Egypt, Babylon and China, according to their reliable historians, could have had an earlier beginning.

Archaeologists calculate from geological strata, but these deductions are not conclusive. The terms "stone age," "neolithic age," "paleolithic," and "tertiary period" denote stages of civilization.

The little catechism teaches: "Man is a creature composed of body and soul, and made to the image and likeness of God." Following the creation of the material world, on the sixth day God created man.

MATTER AND FORM 

Every object in life is made up of matter and form. 

For example, this book is composed of paper and print. 

The form is book. The desk before me has wood for its material. The form is desk. 

At one information forum a gentleman posed a question: "Does wind have matter and form?" To be sure, wind is air in motion. Man is made up of matter and form. God formed this body of ours, composed of chemicals, salt, lime, phosphorus and some forty-five other elements, from the slime of the earth. He breathed into it the breath of life—an immortal soul endowed with a twofold faculty of understanding and free will, whereupon man became a living person. 

The soul of man is spiritual - independent of matter. This could not be predicated of plants and brute animals, which depend on matter and a life principle. 

You cannot even say that our thoughts are created, because they do not exist in themselves but derive from the soul.

EVOLUTION 

The immortal soul, therefore, both in its origin and existence does not depend upon any material substance. 

For this reason, the assertion that man resulted from spontaneous evolution has no scientific proof. You have always understood that the natural sciences depend on facts. To assume something to be true and then fail to prove it, offends logic or the science of right reasoning.

Evolutionists have not proved their theory that a rational man developed from inorganic matter. In your own study of logic you learned that the cause must be proportionate to its effect. In a word, what is not contained in the cause cannot result from it. Evolutionists ignore logic when they assume that life can be generated from what is inanimate, or that a spiritual being (an immortal soul) can result from material things. It is evident, then, that our human race descended from Adam and Eve, our first parents. The Book confirms this fact: "There was not a man to till the earth.... 

And Adam called the name of his wife Eve: because she was the mother of all the living." (Genesis 2:5; 3:20) 

St. Paul reaffirms the same truth when briefing the Athenians: "God, who made the world and all things therein,... hath made of one, all mankind, to dwell upon the whole face of the earth." (Acts of the Apostles 17:24, 26)

IMAGE AND LIKENESS 

Theologians teach that man was created to the image and likeness of God. First, a likeness to God in His attributes; and, second, the image of God as a model. 

Man resembles God in the fact that he has been given the prestige of dominion over this earth. Such dominion presupposes certain endowments in man. These attributes are natural and supernatural. Adam was both a

natural and supernatural image and likeness of God, inasmuch as his soul had a threefold faculty of spirituality, freedom and immortality. These are natural gifts because they belong to the integrity of human nature. Body and soul are essential to man. From the soul result the intellect and free will. These prerogatives constitute man's natural likeness to God, and they are found chiefly in the soul, not in the body.

As a matter of clear reasoning, precept and punishment presume free agents. If man did not have free will, he would not be threatened with punishment: "God made man from the beginning, and left him in the hand of his own counsel. He added His commandments and precepts... 

He hath set water and fire before thee: stretch forth thy hand to which thou wilt. 

Before man is life and death, good and evil: that which he shall choose shall be given him." (Ecclesiasticus 15:14-15, 17-18)

Free will, like reason itself, raises man above brute creation. "e 

MAN'S SOUL 

The soul of man is immortal. The twelfth chapter of Ecclesiastes says: ... and the dust return into its earth, from whence it was and the spirit return to God, who gave it" (Ecclesiastes 12:7). The Bible reveals that there will be an eternal reward or eternal punishment in the world to come; this revelation proves the immortality

of the soul. The Christian religion rests on this fundamental truth, because if God's laws had no sanction religion would lose its force. Moreover, the fundamental that Jesus, the Son of God, by His suffering and death on the Cross redeemed us from eternal damnation, would have no meaning.

That the soul of man is immortal contributes to his likeness to God. That the soul alone, above all creation, lives forever resembles the eternity of Almighty God.

Included in man's natural image of God are his spirituality, freedom and immortality. As such, they may be called natural endowments, because they belong to a rational being.

When the human soul is called a spirit, a simple substance independent of matter is understood. You comprehend that the human soul is a substance. You know this from the consciousness that emanates from both mind and will.
