This substance is permanent and unifies the functions of the mind so that it can retain the impressions of memory and the conclusions of reason. 

The word "simple" in conjunction with the immortal soul means unextended, indivisible, and without composition of parts. Therefore, the soul cannot die, because death is the dissolution of component parts; but the soul has no parts, being a simple substance. The soul, being a spiritual substance, is independent of matter. You may conclude this from the fact that the effect of being cannot supersede the being itself. Once you establish the fact that the soul of man is capable of spiritual acts, transcending the powers of the senses, you prove its spiritual nature.

TWOFOLD Faculty 

As you have experienced, you can conceive things purely spiritual without any relationship to the material. 

For example, some of the attributes of God - His eternity, His goodness, mercy and love-reach beyond our perception. The soul, in fact, performs spiritual acts when it analyzes material things in an immaterial way. 

The soul, and the intellect of man as a faculty of the soul, discern a hidden substance that supports the sensible properties. For example, the sensible properties of a loaf of bread are its color, its taste, its smell, its touch, and whatever appears to the senses; yet we know that these properties do not constitute the bread. There is a substance underneath these accidents that supports or holds the accidents together. You have had the experience of performing acts of the will by which you love virtue in the abstract. You find comfort in thinking of eternal happiness. These objects of your thinking are independent, apart from the senses. God endowed your immortal soul with a twofold faculty — intellect and free will. The will of man can choose or reject an object or an argument presented to the mind since the intellect has the power to regard it, under different aspects, as beneficial or injurious. You yourselves are conscious of this freedom in the human will which is fundamental to our moral and social life. If a person had no free will, he could not be blamed for violating either the laws of God or man. 

We have stated that man was created to the image and likeness of God. Because of his supernatural endow-

ments, man was also made to the supernatural image and likeness of God. What is supernatural, as the word indicates, goes beyond the natural attributes of man, either in the manner in which it is bestowed or in its substance. A natural gift, given in a supernatural way, could be called supernatural only in manner. For example, perfect eyesight is a natural gift; but if one were to lose sight entirely and then receive a miraculous cure, sight would become a gift supernatural in manner. Sanctifying grace exceeds human nature. 

It is the teaching of the Church that when God created Adam and Eve, He endowed them with the supernatural gift of sanctifying grace and also the virtues of faith, hope and charity, which are infused in the soul with sanctifying grace. 

God said: "Let us make man to our image and likeness." (Genesis 1:26) 

Here both the natural and supernatural image and likeness of God are implied. "Only this I have found, that God made man right." 

(Ecclesiastes 7:30) 

Holy Scripture uses the term "right," which means just, in referring to the justice of the saints, which consists in sanctifying grace. Our divine Lord restored the primitive order lost by sin. What Christ restored to us,

however, was sanctifying grace. This doctrine may be clarified by referring to The Book: "Blessed be the God and Father of our Lord

Jesus Christ, who hath blessed us with spiritual blessings in heavenly places, in Christ...

In whom we have redemption through His blood, the remission of sins, according to the riches of His grace, which hath superabounded in us, in all wisdom and prudence." (Ephesians 1:3, 7-8) "And if sons, heirs also; heirs indeed of God and joint heirs with Christ: yet so, if we suffer with Him, that we may be also glorified with Him." (Romans 8:17)

Logically, then, you may conclude that man was originally endowed with sanctifying grace. Men and women have no inherent right, or what you might call a natural right, to the happiness of heaven. Man's title to the Beatific Vision rests on the grace which Jesus merited for us: "Behold what manner of charity the Father hath bestowed upon us, that we should be called and should be the sons of God.... We are now the sons of God; and it hath not yet appeared what we shall be. We know, that when He shall appear we shall be like to 

Him; because we shall see Him as He is." (1 John 3:1, 2)

OUR FIRST PARENTS 

God conferred supernatural gifts on Adam and Eve, such as extraordinary knowledge and understanding and wisdom - indeed, a wisdom that showed "both good and evil" (Ecclesiasticus 17:6). Adam's profound knowledge enabled him to give the creatures on the earth names corresponding to their natures. Also, Adam, being inspired by the Holy Spirit, understood the indissolubility of marriage: "Wherefore a man shall leave father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife." (Genesis 2:24) 

Adam and Eve possessed complete control over their sensual appetites and were exempt from the weakness of concupiscence — i. e., from the inordinate passions and rebellion of the flesh against the spirit: 

"And they were both naked, to wit, Adam and his wife: and were not ashamed.” (Genesis 2:25) 

In the plan of God, the physical bodies of our first parents were to be immortal: ec "For God created man incorruptible, and to the image of His own likeness He made him.

But, by the envy of the devil, death came into the world." (Wisdom 2:23-24) ... the body indeed is dead, because of sin." (Romans 8:10)

Adam and Eve were also exempt from suffering. The fact that the gifts bestowed upon our first parents were supernatural constituted their likeness to God.

Adam had a sublime destiny as father of the human race, not only according to the flesh but also according to the spirit. These supernatural gifts, particularly sanctifying grace, were included in original justice, and were destined not only for himself but for all his posterity: "Increase and multiply, and fill the earth, and subdue it, and rule. . . over... the earth." (Genesis 1:28) 

Not only the angels, but our first parents had to be tested to determine if they were fit mettle for the kingdom of heaven. In disobeying God's command, Adam and Eve failed to recognize their sovereign Good and their supreme Benefactor. They turned away from their divine Creator, and in so doing they incurred the pain of loss which is banishment from the presence of God. 

In fact, having rebelled against their chief Benefactor and sovereign Good, they incurred, like Satan, the misery of eternal punishment. All the descendants of Adam and

Eve, who constitute the entire human race, have inherited their sins: "Wherefore as by one man sin entered into this world and by sin death; and so death passed upon all men, in whom all have sinned. . . . Therefore, as by the offense of one, unto all men to condemnation: so also

by the justice of one, unto all men to justification of life. For as by the disobedience of one man, many were made sinners: so also by the obedience of one, many shall be made just." (Romans 5:12, 18-19) 

Therefore, all men have sinned in Adam. Baptism not only makes us children of God but remits the guilt of original sin. This means that with the grace won by 

Jesus Christ we are spiritually restored to the friendship of God and given a title to heaven.

Moreover, the effects of original sin extend both to the natural and supernatural prerogatives of all mankind. Original sin effaced the supernatural image of God in the souls of men and women and lost for all of us the adoption that God originally conferred in sanctifying grace. It also canceled out God's plan of natural happiness, which is related to sanctifying grace. "... unless a man be born again of water and the Holy Ghost, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God." (St. John 3:5)

While original sin effaced the supernatural likeness, it did not efface the natural image of God in man. It afflicted man, not by depriving him of his natural gifts, but by removing supernatural justice. Original sin also dimmed man's understanding and weakened his will. 

Baptism removed the guilt of original sin, but the remnants or the effects of sin remain. Among these are proneness to evil and weakness of will.

IMMORTALITY OF THE SOUL 

From the beginning of time pagans have tried to deny man's immortality - the divine gift that elevates man above the brute animal. Unbelievers of all ages have attacked the doctrine of immortality. They try to exclude a future life.

In the Psalms David declared: 

"He that loveth iniquity hateth his own soul." 

(Psalm 10:6) 

These benighted people seem to prefer darkness to light. 

They hedge themselves with the flowers of the field, with the "grass... which is today and tomorrow is cast into the oven. . . (St. Matthew 6:30). 

"The sensual man perceiveth not these things that are of the Spirit of God." (1 Corinthians 2:14) 

Identifying themselves with the beast, they want no other pleasure than that which animal passion can afford. They are of the earth, earthly.

Our Saviour taught men the objective of living and tried to enlighten them as to the truth that beyond this life looms another, infinitely more important for every rational creature.

Jesus Christ pointed out the definite path to follow, teaching: "If any man will come after Me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross and follow Me." (St. Matthew 16:24)

Before He ascended into heaven, our Lord promised to send the Holy Spirit to continue His teaching, to bring the light of truth so that all men may know the purpose of creation and our final goal - the happiness of heaven.

Chapter VII THE PROVIDENCE OF GOD 

THE infinite wisdom, goodness and boundless mercy

TH of God are manifest to all. The Almighty Creator not only made the world but constantly sustains it and governs it by eternal laws. Just as thinking and learning depend on the mind, so the conservation of the world depends on its Almighty Creator. "For in Him were all things created in heaven and on earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones, or dominations, or principalities, or powers. All things were created by Him and in Him." (Colossians 1:16)

By definite, fixed laws, the mineral and vegetable kingdoms conform to the direction of their Creator. Instinct and appetite govern the animal kingdom. Rational creatures are endowed with a conscience which serves as a subjective norm and guide. The will of God, expressed in His laws the divine positive law (the commandments) and the natural law comprises the objective norms of morality. 

THE EARTH AND THE SUN 

You will note how the earth is poised a proper disstance from the sun so that it can neither burn nor freeze.

The same loving providence extends to every individual. 76

THE PROVIDENCE OF GOD 

77 While the problem of evil remains obscure, we can be certain that God does not will evil. "In the morning I will stand before Thee, and will see: because Thou art not a God that willest iniquity." 

The all-wise Creator has endowed His rational creatures with intellect and free will. He will not contradict Himself by withdrawing either gift.

The works of creation are directed to co-operate and thus serve as secondary causes in fulfilling the plans of Divine Providence. God permits evil which He can turn into good.

It is true that the designs of God permit suffering, not a moment of which need be lost. Suffering often converts the sinner. The just increase in virtue, mount to higher and higher forms of moral beauty by offering up their sufferings in union with the sufferings of Christ. "For whom the Lord loveth, He chastiseth: and He scourgeth every son whom He receiveth." (Hebrews 12:6)

To be sure, there are many things in life which we do not understand but which are directed by a loving Providence to their appointed end and aided in the attainment of that end. "[God] made the little and the great, and He hath equally care of all." (Wisdom 6:8)

"for them also"

We are like children who do not understand the wise directives of parents. Their foresight protects us from many dangers, both physical and spiritual. In the mature judgment of later years we thank the goodness of God for the prevision and care, the corrections and denials of our parents.

The one all-absorbing and, indeed, most stimulating truth is the fact that God loves us. In fact, God is creative love. He loved us first, long before we could be aware of it. God's love summoned every one of us into life. The teaching of our divine Lord confirms this consoling truth: "Can a woman forget her infant, so as not to have pity on the son of her womb? And if she should forget, yet will not I forget thee." (Isaias 49:15) "Are not two sparrows sold for a farthing? 

And not one of them shall fall on the ground without your Father. But the very hairs of your head are all numbered. Fear not, therefore; better are you than many sparrows.' (St. Matthew 10:29-31)

Chapter VIII THE NECESSITY OF RELIGION 

OW that you have studied the undeniable fact that NOW the existence of God can be known from reason and revelation, the question logically follows: What is man's relation to God? In a word, what does religion imply; what is the proof for the necessity of religion? 

Does the rational creature have definite obligations to his Creator?

SUBJECTIVELY 

Religion, from the Latin "religare," means to bind whatever binds the creature to his Creator. In a broad sense, religion is man's living union with God. The word "living" connotes vital acts, thoughts, words, deeds; spiritual exercises by which we worship God. Worship, prayer, form the groundwork of religion. Subjectively, the true Catholic alerts himself to all things which pertain to God. He learns and lives the fundamental truths of religion. He participates in the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass, frequents the sacraments of confession and Holy Communion. He receives divine nourishment in order to develop and maintain a stalwart character capable of conquering temptation, becoming capable indeed of dynamic Catholic leadership. Aided by divine grace, he keeps the commandments of God and the precepts of the Church. In a word, he practices his religion. 79

The necessity of religion results from the rational nature of man, endowed with intellect and free will. 

OBJECTIVELY 

Objectively considered, religion consists in the divine liturgy of the Church-i. e., acts of public and private devotion the commandments of God, and the precepts of the Church; whatever informs, inspires and stimulates us to union with God. The subject matter of the true religion consists in the teachings of Jesus Christ. 

NO COMPROMISE 

Like the coins of our country, religions are true or false, genuine or counterfeit. False religions either worship idols or worship the true God in a false manner.

A religion is true when it worships the true God in a manner sanctioned by Him. Some pagan cults worship statues and trinkets which they imagine are animated by 

God. Some, like the Mohammedans, worship the true 

God in a false manner.

All nations, ancient and modern, pagan or civilized, bear testimony to the fact that the rational creature, directly or indirectly, clearly or in a confused manner, recognizes the command of his conscience to acknowledge and worship the Creator. Religion is the foundation of justice, and therefore the rights of our neighbors and our own rights depend upon it. Man's power of reasoning helps him to arrive at these sound conclusions.

THE NECESSITY OF RELIGION 

81 

RIGHT ORDER 

The God of wisdom must demand right order in all things. Right order requires that the creature, in conformity with his nature, adore his Creator. Irrational and inert things perforce do the will of their Maker. 

Man, endowed with intellect and free will, uses his intellect to acknowledge God's supremacy, his free will to obey God's laws. Even though he may not be enlightened by faith, the normal person perceives the plain duty to pay homage and submission to God, gratitude to the chief Benefactor, and filial love to our Father in heaven.

Religion, both natural and supernatural, surpasses all other sciences. The value of a science depends on the certainty with which it establishes its conclusions. The human mind cannot be satisfied by half-truths, opinions, doubts, or assumptions. The value of a science depends upon the dignity of the subject matter and the importance of its objective. True religion rests upon the authority of God—that is, absolute certainty. Scientists depend on the light and accuracy of reason. Theologians depend on the truthfulness of God, who can neither deceive nor be deceived. God and all that relates to Him form the subject matter of religion. Profane sciences deal with material things. Even though the mind of man cannot fathom the mysteries of religion, his intellect is ennobled and enriched by their study.

The importance of its objective places religion at the top of all the sciences. Religion has for its final goal the moral and spiritual welfare of rational beings; to teach them the eternal truths that point the way to heaven.

St. Augustine said in his Confessions: "Thou hast made us for Thyself (O Lord), and the heart of man is restless till it rests in Thee." George Washington declared: "Religion is the strongest prop of government.' 

Why? Because justice depends on religion. "Without justice," wrote St. Augustine, "states are nothing else than large bands of robbers, as bands of robbers are nothing else but little states" (De Civitate Dei, IV, 4).

Chapter IX THE IMMACULATE CONCEPTION N DECEMBER 8, 1854, Pope Pius IX solemnly 

ON proclaimed and defined that the Blessed Virgin Mary "in the first instant of her conception, by a singular privilege and grace, granted by God in view of the merits of Jesus Christ, the Saviour of the human race, was preserved exempt from all stain from original sin” (Bull Ineffabilis Deus; see Denzinger, Enchiridion Symbolorum, no. 1641).

Destined to be the Mother of Jesus, Son of God, the Blessed Virgin Mary had a sublime privilege bestowed upon her. She was conceived without the stain of original sin. She was therefore never, for one instant, under the power of the devil. This special grace came to her through the merits of Jesus, her divine Redeemer, not because He removed the stain from her soul but because He excluded it. At the first instant of her animation sanctifying grace adorned her soul, before any sin could have taken effect. This special prerogative, her Immaculate Conception, should not be confused with the virgin birth of Christ, nor does it imply a miraculous birth of Mary. Many, in error, refer to the Immaculate Conception as if it were identical with the birth of Jesus. From the first instant of her conception, by a special divine privilege and in anticipation of the merits of Jesus Christ, her Son, the Blessed Virgin Mary was preserved from 83

original sin. As the descendants of our first parents, Adam and Eve, all rational beings (the Blessed Virgin alone excepted) enter this life with the stain of original sin on their souls. This taint results, not from actual guilt on our part, but because our human nature was wounded or impaired by the sin of our first parents; in virtue of their transgression this sin was transmitted to us. This original condition which we inherited, deprived us of God's grace. In a word, as descendants of Adam and Eve, all rational beings inherit their nature and its weaknesses, which consist primarily in the loss of sanctifying grace because it was lost by Adam, "in whom all have sinned" (Romans 5:12). 

The mercies of God we shall sing forever, because, by sending a Redeemer to make infinite atonement for us, our heavenly Father worked out a plan whereby every rational creature can be restored to the inheritance lost by Adam's sin that sin which effaced the supernatural image of God in the souls of men. Even though original sin did not destroy the natural image of God in man, it did obscure man's understanding and weakened his will. The majesty of God required that the Precious 

Blood shed for our salvation should be originally supplied by a virgin absolutely unstained by any sin.

The dogma of Mary's Immaculate Conception is sufficiently indicated in the Bible. It also rests firmly on 

Tradition. Ancient apologists of the Church such as 

Origen and St. Paschasius, Doctors of the Church such as St. Ephrem and St. Thomas Aquinas, taught that the 

Blessed Virgin Mary was free from original sin. These

THE IMMACULATE CONCEPTION 

85 and many eminent Church Fathers refer to her as The Immaculata.

Commentators concede that Jesus is the seed of "the woman" and that "the woman," like her Son, will be at enmity with the devil because her divine Son conquers Satan. Now, if such enmity existed between Satan and the Blessed Virgin Mary, it must follow that she had not once been subject to original sin, which enslaves men to Satan. The same enmity exists between the seed of the woman—that is, Christ—and Satan, typified by the serpent.

This victory of our Saviour over Satan includes the victory of the woman, His Mother, over the enemy of mankind. Moreover, it was the eternal plan of God the 

Father to prepare a dignified and spotless dwelling place for His divine Son. St. John the Baptist was sanctified in his mother's womb. The Blessed Virgin Mary merited an even higher degree of divine grace, in proportion to her dignity, by being preserved from original sin. Therefore, when His Holiness Pope Pius IX defined the doctrine of the Immaculate Conception on December 8, 1854, he did not create a new doctrine, because the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin Mary had always been true and had been believed by the faithful for centuries. The Pope simply confirmed the doctrine that had already existed and took it out of the realm of doubt or controversy.

POET'S SALUTE 

The celebrated poet William Wordsworth, himself not a Catholic, has touchingly memorialized the Immacu-

late Conception of the Blessed Virgin Mary in these classic lines. "Mother, whose virgin bosom was uncrossed

By the least shade of thought to sin allied, Woman! Above all women glorified 

Our tainted nature's solitary boast." 

-The Virgin

Chapter X THE BIRTH OF CHRIST THE DIVINE PROMISES 

WHEN, following their sin of disobedience, Ad mighty God drove our first parents, Adam and

Eve, out of the Garden of Eden, He did not leave them hopeless. He promised them a Redeemer. Condemning the serpent who had tempted Eve, God said: "I will put enmities between thee and the woman, and thy seed and her seed: she shall crush thy head, and thou shalt lie in wait for her heel." (Genesis 3:15) 

The seed of the woman destined to crush the head of the serpent is our divine Redeemer, who is to be victorious over Satan.

One hundred and ten prophecies in the Old Testament foretell and describe the Messias, who was expected by the people during a period of more than four thousand years. 

The Messias

The word "Messias" is derived from the Hebrew and means the "anointed one." The word "Christ" comes from the Greek and has the same meaning. The words 87

"Christ" or "Messias" have deep significance when we know that the humanity of Jesus, by virtue of His hypostatic union with the Divinity, was sanctified and anointed with the oil of gladness. 

God promised a Redeemer first to Adam and Eve and then to the patriarchs, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. Their people understood this promise of the Redeemer to come, and looked to that great event. "In thy seed shall all the nations of the earth be blessed." (Genesis 22:18; 26:4)

St. Paul's reference to the promised Redeemer as a descendant of Abraham clarifies the doctrine: "He saith not: 'And to his seeds,' as of many; but as of one: 'and to thy seed,' which is Christ." (Galatians 3:16)

THE PRINCE OF PROPHETS 

Isaias, more than seven centuries before Christ, in many passages foretold in detail the coming of the Messias. According to the prophecy of Jacob (Genesis 

49:10), our Saviour's advent would occur at the time when the scepter had been taken away from Juda. Jesus had been announced as the Descendant of the tribe of Juda and of the house of David. Isaias foretold that the 

Messias would be born in Bethlehem and of a virgin mother. "And there shall come forth a rod out of the root of Jesse [David's father]: and a flower

THE BIRTH OF CHRIST 

89 shall rise up out of his root. And the spirit of the Lord shall rest upon Him: the spirit of wisdom and of understanding, the spirit of counsel and of fortitude, the spirit of knowledge and of godliness. And He shall be filled with the spirit of the fear of the Lord.... 

The wolf shall dwell with the lamb; ... the earth is filled with the knowledge of the Lord, as the covering waters of the sea. 

(Isaias 11:1-3, 6, 9) 

The minor prophet Micheas told of the Messias who was to be born in Bethlehem of Juda: "And thou, Bethlehem Ephrata, art a little one among the thousands [towns of a thousand inhabitants] of Juda: out of thee shall He come forth unto Me that is to be the Ruler in Israel: and His going forth is from the beginning, from the days of eternity." (Micheas 5:2) 

Isaias foretold that the Messias would be born of a virgin: "The Lord Himself shall give you a sign. Behold a virgin shall conceive and bear a Son: and His name shall be called Emmanuel." (Isaias 7:14) 

The name "Emmanuel" means "God with us."

To the oft-repeated question whether God could have forgiven man without the expiation of the suffering and

death of Jesus on the Cross, the answer is yes. On the other hand, right order, being upset by the sins of our first parents and their children, would seem to require adequate atonement that would glorify God and help mankind. In the wisdom of God this was accomplished by the promised Messias. When we say that Jesus made atonement to His Father in heaven, we attribute to the 

Father what is common to the Blessed Trinity. While the distinction between the Persons in God is real, Jesus could teach: "e the Father is in Me, and I in the Father." (St. John 10:38)

Theologians refer to this union as "circuminsession." 

The prophet Zacharias envisioned that Jesus would be sold for thirty pieces of silver (Chapter 11:12-13); also, that He would be reviled and spit upon (Isaias 

50:6); that He would be given gall and vinegar to drink 

(Psalm 68:22). 

THE PROMISES FULFILLED 

Jesus of Nazareth, both directly and indirectly, proved 

Himself to be the promised Redeemer sent by God. Our

Saviour, by the very fact of His miracles and the fulfilling of all the prophecies, left no doubt in any reasonable mind that He was the long-expected Messias. Moreover, He claimed this for Himself.

Jesus Christ proved Himself to be the true Son of God and true man. It was His mission to fulfill what had been prefigured in the Old Law. Our Saviour removed the cause of the rift between Jews and Gentiles,

MATER ADMIRABILIS

THE BIRTH OF CHRIST 

91 so as to realize the ideal of "one flock and one Shepherd" (St. John 10:16). Instead of the Mosaic law, He substituted new precepts. "You have heard that it was said to them of old..., but I say to you" (St. Matthew 

5:21 ff.). Jesus was justified in teaching that He had come, not "to destroy but to fulfill [the law]" (St. Matthew 5:17).

The scepter- that is, the independence of the Jewish people had been wrested from them by the Roman conquest. In turn this meant that the leadership of the house of Juda had terminated. The Jews themselves acknowledged this before Pilate: "We have no king but Caesar." (St. John 19:15)

Many quotations from the Old Testament confirm the revelations of the New Testament that Jesus of Nazareth was the Messias promised.

THE ANNUNCIATION 

When the fullness of time had come, Almighty God sent the archangel Gabriel to a virgin whose name was 

Mary. The Blessed Virgin Mary-"woman above all women glorified"-had been privileged by God to be conceived without sin. The Virgin Mary, a humble and pious maiden, lived a holy life in the small town of Nazareth.

At this point it should be observed that in ancient times a number of young people took the vow of perpetual virginity. After reaching a certain age, everyone had to marry to secure protection and to preserve his or

her good name. Young women who had taken the vow of virginity found young men who had also vowed their lives to God in chastity. St. Joseph, who had taken the vow of virginity, won the consent of the Blessed Virgin Mary to be her spouse. It was understood, of course, that even though they were betrothed and married before the high priest, they would retain their virginity and live as brother and sister. This type of marriage may be entered into today. It is known as a Josephite marriage. The 

Blessed Virgin Mary and St. Joseph both retained their beautiful innocence.

One day while Mary knelt in prayer in her humble little home at Nazareth, the archangel Gabriel appeared to her and announced that she had been chosen to be the Mother of Jesus, the Son of God. Our divine Creator always respects the faculty of free will which He vouchsafes to rational man. He never crosses its threshold until 

He comes to serve sentence of death. Therefore, He did not command the Virgin Mary to be the Mother of Jesus Christ; but He sent the angel Gabriel to brief her on His divine plan and to ask her consent. Even though He offered her the most sublime dignity, He left her free. 

The Jewish maidens of that time knew about the promised Messias. Doubtless they envisioned our Saviour in some great palace. When the angel said, "Hail Mary, full of grace, the Lord is with thee," he explained to her that she had been chosen by Almighty God to be the Mother of Jesus the Messias. Immediately, the Virgin posed a difficulty: "How shall this be done, because I know not man?" (St. Luke 1:34); that is, since I have taken

THE BIRTH OF CHRIST 

93 the vow of virginity. The angel assured the Blessed Virgin Mary that she could be the mother of God and at the same time retain her vow of virginity: "The Holy Ghost shall come upon thee and the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee. And therefore also the Holy which shall be born of thee shall be called the Son of God." (St. Luke 1:35)

Once she understood that she could keep her vow of virginity and at the same time become a mother, Mary spoke the momentous words: "Behold the handmaid of the Lord: be it done to me according to thy word." (St. Luke 1:38)

At the instant Mary gave her consent, the Son of God, by the intervention of the Holy Spirit, assumed of her His human nature, which developed like that of any other child during nine months. The Blessed Virgin Mary is truly the Mother of God, because the same divine Person who is the Son of God is the Son of the Blessed Virgin Mary. This is the mystery of the two natures, the divine nature and the human nature in one Person, the Person of Jesus.

While the Incarnation of Jesus, in harmony with the external works of God, was activated by all three divine Persons of the Blessed Trinity, it is attributed to the Holy Spirit because it is an achievement of love and sanctification.

Chapter XI  

"But when the fullness of the time was come, God sent His Son, made of a woman.' Ster 

(Galatians 4:4)

IT. PAUL here teaches the eternal and temporal generation, the divine and the human nature of Jesus. 

This is the foundation and the substance of the Catholic religion.

The Son of God took on our human nature not merely in appearance, but in reality. During the first and second centuries a group of dissenters known as the Docetae held that Christ only seemed to have a body. They based their assertion on a false principle — namely, that all material, being the product of evil, is evil in itself. Other heretics taught that the Incarnation of Jesus was only apparent. 

St. Paul refuted these errors when he wrote: ee 

Christ Jesus, who being in the form of

God, thought it not robbery to be equal with 

God: but emptied Himself, taking the form of a servant, being made in the likeness of men... 

(Philippians 2:5-7)

The expressions "form of God" and "form of a servant," both attributed to Christ, stress the divine and the 94

human natures hypostatically united in the one Person of Jesus. The nature of God and the nature of man were united in the God-man, Jesus Christ. Our Lord left no doubt regarding the reality of His Sacred Person by the words He spoke to His disciples following His Resurrection: "See My hands and feet, that it is I Myself. 

Handle and see: for a spirit hath not flesh and bones, as you see Me to have.” (St. Luke 24:39)

These two natures were neither separated nor blended but hypostatically united in the one Person of Jesus Christ.

Therefore Jesus was true man as well as the Son of God by eternal generation. He always existed in the mind of 

God, just as an idea may exist. Since there could be no accident in God, the verbum, i. e., the idea in the mind of God, took on our human nature. The word "nature" here signifies the gifts and qualities that belong to a rational person. Because Christ had a twofold nature, a twofold knowledge, and a twofold will, He could act as God or man. Thus we refer to Him as the God-Man. 

The human nature of Jesus, perfect from the start of its existence, was divinely enriched by its elevation to union with the Person of the Word. In the Divine 

Praises we pray: "Blessed be Jesus Christ, true God and true man." Hence the human nature of our Saviour is possessed by the Person of the Word. "... The Word was made flesh, and dwelt among us" (St. John 1:14).

"FOR THEM. ALSO❞ 

No theologian pretends to know the way in which God brought about this union. It is, in very truth, the Mystery of the Incarnation. 

We speak of the "hypostatic union." Hypostasis, a term difficult to explain, means that which subsists or is independently. Hypostasis here indicates the same thing as person.* 

THE SON OF MAN 

Our Saviour often referred to Himself as the Son of Man because of His descent from David and because He was born of the Virgin Mary. As the Son of God, He had a divine nature; and as the Son of man, He had a human nature derived from the pure body of the Blessed Virgin Mary in a miraculous manner without the intervention of man: ... the power of the Most High shall overshadow thee. . . . ' 

(St. Luke 1:35)

Contrary to the false doctrine of the second-century Valentinians, Christ did not bring His body from heaven, but assumed it from His virgin mother. "Behold, thou shalt conceive in thy womb and shalt bring forth a son: and thou shalt call 

His name Jesus. He shall be great and shall be called the Son of the Most High." (St. Luke 1:31-32) *Cf. St. John Damascene, Dialectica, 43-44. Cf. also P. Galtier, S. J., De Incar. et Redemp., p. 171.

The Blessed Virgin Mary remained a virgin before, during and after the birth of Jesus: "Behold a virgin shall conceive and bear a son." (Isaias 7:14)

The only possible interpretation of these words is a virginal conception and a virginal birth. For that reason the term "Mary ever virgin" expresses our doctrine of 

Mary's perpetual virginity.

While the Bible calls Jesus the firstborn of Mary, this Hebrew idiom can apply to an only son as well as to the first of several children (Exodus 12:29). In the same way the "brethren" of Jesus named in the Bible are His nearest of kin. This was the custom of His people; often a wife would refer to her husband as "my son." Abraham called Lot his brother, although they were only cousins 

(Genesis 13:8). 

HUMAN AND DIVINE 

When we profess our belief that Jesus took to Himself an entire human nature, we mean a human body and a rational human soul with all its faculties, spiritual and sensitive. Therefore, the human nature of Jesus was capable of suffering. The divine and human natures hypostatically united in Jesus Christ remained unmixed, unconfused, unchanged. Jesus had two distinct wills, divine and human. Note our Saviour's words in the Garden of Gethsemani:

"My Father, if it be possible, let this chalice pass from Me. Nevertheless, not as I will but as Thou wilt." (St. Matthew 26:39)

Not the human will of Jesus but the divine will of the 

Father must be accomplished. 

1. Christ fulfilled all the Messianic prophecies made of Him in the Old Testament. His very life of holiness and the doctrine He taught reflected divinity. 

2. Christ proved His divinity and the divinity of His mission by the miracles He wrought, especially 

His own Resurrection, which He Himself had prophesied.

PERSECUTION MUST BE 

The Catholic Church, which has suffered cruel persecution in every age, still endures, in accord with the promise of Christ that He would be with her until the end of time. In fact, He is her vitalizing force and the channel of divine grace lavished on mankind through the sacraments and prayer. Since our divine Redeemer, having atoned for our sins by His Passion and death on the Cross, continues in heaven to be the chief source and medium of any merit we can achieve, we conclude our prayers by the expression: "Through Jesus Christ our 

Lord. Amen." We try to acknowledge that all good derives through Him and from Him. For Jesus not only founded the Catholic Church. He is the focal point of everything comprised within it.

You may hear the shallow appraisal that Jesus was undoubtedly the grandest character that ever lived on earth but not divine, "as Catholics assert." Jesus was what He claimed to be, or He could not have been the grandest character that ever lived. He claimed divinity for Himself: "I and the Father are One." (St. John 10:30)

His enemies understood very well what He meant because they took up stones to throw at Him. When Christ challenged them: "Many good works I have showed you from 

My Father. For which of those works do you stone Me? The Jews answered Him: 'For a good work we stone Thee not, but for blasphemy; and because that Thou, being a man, makest Thyself God.'' 

(St. John 10:32-33) "Let not your heart be troubled. You believe in God: believe also in Me," said our Saviour. "If you had known Me, you would without doubt have known My Father also: and from henceforth you shall know Him. And you have seen Him." 

St. Philip said to Him: (St. John 14:1, 7) "Lord, show us the Father; and it is enough for us."

"for them also" 

Jesus said to him: "Have I been so long a time with you and have you not known Me? Philip, he that seeth Me seeth the Father also." (St. John 14:8-9)

Here Jesus identifies Himself with God the Father in virtue of His divine nature. 

When St. Thomas the Apostle, following the Resurrection, saluted Jesus with the words "my Lord and my 

God," Christ did not correct him. Jesus unmistakably manifested His divine nature when He commissioned the Apostles: 

"Going therefore, teach ye all nations.... (St. Matthew 28:19) "HE HATH BlasphemeD" 

Caiphas the high priest summoned our Lord and demanded by an oath: ... tell us if Thou be the Christ the Son of 

God. Jesus saith to him: 'Thou hast said it.

Nevertheless I say to you hereafter you shall see the Son of man sitting on the right hand of the power of God and coming in the clouds of heaven.' Then the high priest rent his garments, saying: 'He hath blasphemed. 

What further need have we of witnesses? 

Behold, now you have heard the blasphemy.' (St. Matthew 26:63-65) 

The members of the Sanhedrin understood what Christ meant by the expression "Son of man."

MIRACLES CONFIRM 

When St. John the Baptist, detained in prison, sent two of his disciples to ask Jesus: "Art Thou He that art to come, or look we for another?" Jesus answered them: "Go and relate to John what you have heard and seen. The blind see, the lame walk, the lepers are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead rise again, the poor have the gospel preached to them." (St. Matthew 11:3-5)

In a word, Jesus proved His divinity by performing miracles that only a divine Person could perform. These very miracles were the credentials of His divinity and the divinity of His mission. The doctrines that our Saviour taught, His insistence on charity and forgiveness, the mercy that He exemplified to all men, are cogent proofs that He was in truth begotten of God the Father. Jesus gave ample testimony in support of His own divinity. 

When our Saviour was led to the house of Caiphas, where the chief priests and the council summoned false witnesses to testify against Him, He held His peace. These are definite and unmistakable avowals that Jesus claimed to be divine.

THE MERCY OF GOD 

An incident which occurred many years ago during a trip East may clarify the doctrine of the divinity of 

Christ. An eminent attorney seated next to me in the

smoking car, asked, "Are you a Catholic priest?" In response to an affirmative reply, he continued, "Father, if anyone could prove to me that Jesus Christ was divine, I would become a Catholic. Tell me, please, why is the divinity of Christ the central hinge of your religion?" 

My answer had to be pointed. "Because the Catholic

Church which Christ founded, the miracles He performed, and the sacraments He instituted required divine power." 

To convince him, I first cited a few of the one hundred and ten Messianic prophecies made centuries before the birth of Christ and fulfilled by Him. The counselor advised me that he could not accept these prophecies as a proof of the divinity of Christ. 

Then came my recital of several miracles performed by Christ, miracles which reflected divine power-e. g., Christ raising Lazarus from the dead to inspire faith in an unbelieving generation. Our Saviour declared: "For the works which the Father hath given Me to perfect, the works themselves which 

I do, give testimony of Me, that the Father hath sent Me.” (St. John 5:36)

On another occasion Jesus appealed to the miracles He performed and challenged His enemies to refute them: "If I do not the works of My Father, believe 

Me not. But if I do, though you will not believe Me, believe the works: that you may know and believe that the Father is in Me and I in the Father." (St. John 10:37-38)

The insidious enemies of our divine Lord, the scribes and the Pharisees, had to acknowledge the miracles which they had witnessed. The testimony of their senses left them no alternative.

At this point it might be well to forestall the same objection made to Christ by pagan leaders like Celsus, Porphyrius and Julian the Apostate. These false teachers, well versed in the deceptions of magic art, pretended that Jesus had worked miracles by Beelzebub, the prince of devils. They reasoned poorly. Christ had repeatedly driven out Satan and his cohorts who took possession of human bodies. It would be absurd to imagine that Satan plotted against himself.

Jesus proved Himself to be divine when He multiplied the loaves and fishes to feed five thousand men, and possibly ten thousand women and children, with five loaves and two fishes (St. Matthew 14:19-21). Jesus repeated this miracle on another occasion, when He fed four thousand men, and probably more than eight thousand women and children, with seven loaves and a few fishes (St. Matthew 15:34). This particular miracle foreshadows the institution of the Holy Eucharist. 

He manifested power over the forces of nature when He calmed the tempest. Those who witnessed this miracle exclaimed: "What manner of man is this, for the winds and the sea obey Him?" (St. Matthew 8:27)

"ARISE AND Walk"

You will also remember the account of the man stricken with paralysis who was carried to Christ. Before 

He cured the paralytic, Jesus absolved him from his sins because He discovered in him true repentance. He then questioned the bystanders, who included Pharisees and other critics: "Why do you think evil in your hearts? Which is easier, to say, 'Thy sins are forgiven thee': or to say, 'Arise, and walk'? But that you may know that the Son of man hath power on earth to forgive sins (then said He to the man sick of the palsy): 'Arise, take up thy bed and go into thy house."" (St. Matthew 9:4-6) 

To answer the question: "Which is easier, to say . . . ?" one is as easy as the other, because both require divine power. 

The attorney at my side, unconvinced, observed that other prophets in the history of mankind had worked miracles. True, some did work miracles but always in the name and by the power of God. Jesus, on the other hand, performed miracles in His own name. Among our Saviour's miracles, and not all are recorded, His Resurrection from the dead confirms the divinity of Jesus 

Christ. The Apostles cited the Resurrection of Christ as the basis of faith. St. Paul re-emphasized it again and again. "And if Christ be not risen again, then is our preaching vain: and your faith is also vain.

Yea, and we are found false witnesses of 

God: ... If in this life only we have hope in 

Christ, we are of all men most miserable. 

But now Christ is risen from the dead.... (1 Corinthians 15:14-15, 19-20) 

Inspired by the Holy Spirit, St. Peter's sermon on the 

Resurrection bore fruitful results. Three thousand converts to Christianity were baptized on the first Pentecost 

Sunday. The rational man, open to reason and admitting the testimony of his senses, admits the Resurrection of 

Jesus Christ. If He had appeared only once or twice, there might be some question of illusion; but during forty days He appeared many times. St. Peter met Him;

St. Mary Magdalene recognized Him; the Apostles on one occasion, without Thomas received Him for a visit. Then all the Apostles, including St. Thomas, saw 

Him. He was seen afterward by more than five hundred people at one time. St. Paul, writing to the Corinthians, referred to the fact that there were many persons still living among them who had seen the risen Christ (1 

Corinthians 15:6). It is not possible that the five hundred persons who saw Christ on one occasion could have been deceived. 

PROOF CONCLUSIVE

The Resurrection, duly recorded in the Bible, is also an historical fact to which many eyewitnesses have testified. The proof is irrefutable. 

The Resurrection of our divine Saviour stimulates the faith of every Christian. It confirms the promise of

Jesus that we shall rise from the dead. The entire framework of the Catholic faith rests on the fundamental that Jesus rose from the dead. Emerging from the tomb, our 

Saviour reaffirmed the truths that He had taught. By 

His Resurrection He established the divine character of His priesthood and of His mission. He thus made certain the efficacy of His Sacrifice on the Cross and our resurgence from the death of sin to the life of grace. 

"HE HAS RISEN AS HE SAID" 

Many who prefer darkness to light scoff at our Easter joy. They assert that Christ's Resurrection is a myth. 

They even blaspheme, as did those who hurled insults at Christ when He hung on the Cross. They wag their proud heads and ridicule the Easter hymn of peace because there is so much grief in the world. The reasons for sorrow and trial are shrouded in mystery. Physical suffering and mental anguish have converted countless souls to Christ. On that first Easter, fear crushed the spirit of Christ's faithful followers. They had expected 

Him to triumph over His worldly enemies. Instead of 

His victory, they had seen Him rejected, scourged, and crucified. This was called the "scandal of the Cross." "But I am a worm and no man: the reproach of men and the outcast of the people." 

(Psalm 21:7) 

Remember that God never lost faith in man; by the very glory of His Resurrection He fortifies that faith.

God never attempted to deprive man of the gift of free will with which He had endowed him. 

Those who still languish in the dark, blue void of unbelief may take hope from Him who is the eternal 

Light shining in the darkness. The heavens are ever bright, luminous, and triumphant. Christ, the Resurrection and the Life, has conquered sin and death. "My little children, these things I write to you, that you may not sin. But if any man sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the Just. And He is the propitiation for our sins: and not for ours only, but also for those of the whole world." (1 St. John 2:1-2) 

"THE LIGHT OF THE WORLD" 

The Resurrection of Christ strengthened the faith of

His disciples and of all generations who have lived since the first joyous Easter Sunday. It illumined the divine mission of Christ, the Light of the world. You can understand that Almighty God in His divine wisdom would not permit miracles in favor of one who falsely pretends to be a divine messenger and who appeals to such miracles as proof of His mission. Some of the scribes and 

Pharisees tried to challenge Jesus: "Master, we would see a sign from Thee. 

Who answering said to them: 'An evil and adulterous generation seeketh a sign: and a sign shall not be given it, but the sign of Jonas the prophet. For as Jonas was in the

whale's belly three days and three nights; so

Ishall the Son of man be in the heart of the earth three days and three nights." " (St. Matthew 12:38-40)

On another occasion, when the Pharisees demanded a proof of His divine authority, Jesus replied: "Destroy this temple; and in three days I will raise it up.... But He spoke of the temple of His body." (St. John 2:19, 21)

His enemies understood what Christ meant and they never forgot it. Hence they demanded a guard at the tomb, because they complained: "We have remembered that the seducer said, while He was yet alive: 'After three days I will rise again.' (St. Matthew 27:63) 

Evaluate the Resurrection, then, as the fulfillment of a prophecy and the summary proof of Christ's divinity. 

While briefing His disciples, in the presence of His enemies, Christ foretold His Resurrection. In fact, He often referred to it. Christ said that His Resurrection would take place on the third day. Only in His supernatural character could He foresee His Resurrection, since such a miracle depended altogether on divine omnipotence. 

"I AM THE RESURRECTION AND THe Life" 

Holy Scripture tells of a sermon which our divine Lord preached on the occasion of the death of a friend.

St. John records that sermon in the eleventh chapter of his Gospel. Solemnly repeated in Masses for the dead, it is a short meditation but deep in meaning and farreaching in effect. Millions of sorrowing hearts for nineteen hundred years have felt the warmth of its message. 

Martha and Mary and their brother Lazarus, of the town of Bethany, were devoted friends of Jesus Christ. 

Now it happened that Lazarus fell sick and his sisters sent for Christ, saying "Lord, behold, he whom Thou lovest is sick" (St. John 11:3).

Our Saviour received the message, but He purposely delayed His coming so that He might glorify His Father and confirm the faith of His disciples. So Lazarus died before Christ arrived. "Martha therefore, as soon as she heard that 

Jesus was come, went to meet Him.... 

Martha therefore said to Jesus: 'Lord, if 

Thou hadst been here, my brother had not died. But now also I know that whatsoever 

Thou wilt ask of God, God will give it 

Thee.' Jesus saith to her: "Thy brother shall rise again.' Martha saith to Him: 'I know that he shall rise again, in the resurrection at the last day.' Jesus said to her: 'I am the 

Resurrection and the Life: he that believeth in Me, although he be dead, shall live: and everyone that liveth and believeth in Me shall not die forever.' (St. John 11:20-26)

Jesus cited His Resurrection as sufficient evidence to stabilize the Church He came to establish. You may

logically conclude that the Catholic religion bears the seal of divine authority. 

"THE WORD Was Made Flesh" 

Other points of history, including the Ascension, the descent of the Holy Ghost upon the Apostles (Acts of the Apostles 2:2-3), the destruction of Jerusalem, the preaching of the faith to the Gentiles, throw more light on the divinity of Christ. But the beloved disciple crystallizes this doctrine in his brilliant Gospel which tells of "the Word...made flesh [that] dwelt among us" (St. John 1:14).

The word "flesh" signifies the entire nature of man. 

The sacred writer obviously used this expression to establish the reality of the Incarnation. In the Apostles' 

Creed we refer to the Incarnation: "born of the Virgin 

Mary"; and later, in another profession of faith, we recite that "for us men and for our salvation, He came down from heaven, took flesh of the Virgin Mary, and was made man." Therefore, the Son of God assumed human nature not merely in appearance but in reality. 

It is the teaching of the Church that two natures, divine and human, exist in Jesus Christ, for He was both God and man. He was God by His divine nature and man by His human nature.

Every being is referred to according to his nature. 

Jesus was the Son of God by virtue of His eternal generation from God the Father. He also called Himself the Son of man by virtue of His descent from David and His birth of the Blessed Virgin Mary. To be the Son

of God, He would have to receive His divine nature from God; and to be the Son of man, it was necessary for Him to receive a human nature from a woman. 

When the Blessed Virgin Mary consented to become the Mother of God, Jesus, the Son of God, by the divine power of the Holy Spirit and without the intervention of man assumed our human nature. If Jesus had not been God at the instant of His conception, the Blessed Virgin Mary would have conceived only a human being. 

HUMANITY ASSUMED 

Even though God the Father and God the Holy Ghost participated in the Incarnation of Jesus, the Son of God alone assumed our nature. The Bible relates that 

Christ, dying on the Cross, "gave up the ghost" (St. 

John 19:30). His soul was separated from His body by death. When St. Luke wrote: "Jesus advanced in wisdom and age and grace with God and men" (St. Luke 2:52), he referred to wisdom gained by experience. 

The Catholic Church teaches that our Saviour was a perfect man, possessing the same nature that we possess, like unto us in all things, sin excepted. In conformity with His dignity as the Son of God, Jesus had all the perfections of body and soul which essentially belong to a human nature. That the Son of God took a human nature capable of suffering, He Himself admitted: "My soul is sorrowful even unto death." (St. Matthew 26:38)

There is nothing inconsistent with the nature of Christ being capable of suffering, because the reality of Christ's human nature is shown by the sorrows and agony which 

He endured in making atonement for mankind. He not only redeemed us, but He gave us a powerful incentive to take up our cross and follow Him-to suffer with patience, courage and resignation.

Because of the hypostatic union (the union of the nature of God and the nature of man in a single divine Person), the human intellect of Jesus had a twofold supernatural knowledge. Incapable of sin, the soul of our Saviour had the fullness of grace. In accord with the teaching of theologians, the human intellect of Jesus had two kinds of supernatural knowledge, over and above the natural knowledge that He gained from experience. 

Rapt in the vision of God, Jesus had supernatural knowledge. Theologians also teach that Christ's human nature was adorned with sanctifying grace: "Because in Him, it hath well pleased the Father that all fullness should dwell." (Colossians 1:19)

In view of the above truths, Christ, being truly the Son of God, merits supreme adoration.

BLAMELESS CHARACTER OF CHRIST 

The attorney at my side interposed to say that he had been reading a book which stated that Jesus Christ was undoubtedly the grandest character that ever lived but was not divine, as Catholics assert.

I asked the attorney: "Do you accept this appraisal?" "Oh, yes," he replied, "the book is logical." I then inquired, "You accept the Bible as the word of God?" "No," he answered, "only as authentic history." Then

I countered, "If Christ was the grandest character that ever lived on earth, He spoke the truth on all occasions." "Most certainly," he replied. "Well, now, the Bible itself twenty-four times records the fact that Christ referred to Himself as divine, the Son of God. He was either that which He claimed to be, or He was the worst impostor who ever lived. You admit that He could not be the grandest character that ever lived and ignore fundamental truth so vital to mankind." Logic did not impress my traveling companion. "Not only the book you quote, my friend, but even the enemies of Christ recognized Him to have been a wise, upright and blameless character. Often they heard 

His claim to be the Son of God. "You are acquainted with The Book and will recall that when His enemies found fault with Him for performing miracles on the Sabbath, our Lord replied that as the Son of God He had the same power as His Father in heaven: "My Father worketh until now; and I work.'

Hereupon therefore the Jews sought the more to kill Him, because He did not only break the Sabbath but also said God was His Father, making Himself equal to God. Then 

Jesus answered and said to them: 'Amen, amen, I say unto you, the Son cannot do anything of Himself, but what He seeth the

Father doing: for what things soever He doth, these the Son also doth in like manner.... For as the Father raiseth up the dead and giveth life: so the Son also giveth life to whom He will.... For as the Father hath life in Himself, so He hath given to the Son also to have life in Himself.... You sent to 

John: and he gave testimony to the truth....

But I have a greater testimony than that of 

John: for the works which the Father hath given Me to perfect; the works themselves which I do, give testimony of Me, that the 

Father hath sent Me!" (St. John 5:17-19, 21, 26, 33, 36)

These words confirm the divinity of Christ. In fact, our 

Saviour's enemies took them in their literal sense namely, that Jesus referred to Himself as the Son of God. 

The text is clear beyond a reasonable doubt. 

You will remember St. Peter's response to our Lord's challenge: "Whom do men say that the Son of man is?' 

But they said: 'Some John the Baptist, and other some Elias, and others Jeremias or one of the prophets.' Jesus saith to them: 'But whom do you say that I am?' Simon Peter answered and said: 'Thou art Christ, the Son of the living God.' And Jesus answering, said to him: 'Blessed art thou, Simon Bar 

Jona [Simon, son of Jona]: because flesh and

blood hath not revealed it to thee, but My 

Father who is in heaven!'" (St. Matthew 16:13-17)

St. Peter was inspired by the Holy Spirit to profess his firm belief in the divinity of Christ. 

When the Sanhedrin attempted to cross-examine Jesus, He declared in a positive and unmistakable manner that He was the Son of God. The Pharisees, sitting in judgment on Him, rendered a verdict of death because "He made Himself the Son of God" (St. John 19:7). 

Jesus attributes to Himself the nature of God. There was no doubt in the minds of His enemies that Christ claimed divinity for Himself. Wherefore they took up stones to cast at Him.

THE APOSTLES ATTEST HIS DIVINITY 

When the Apostles were challenged by what authority they taught, they proclaimed the divinity of Christ. 

This divinely commissioned teaching authority may be considered both in a natural and a supernatural aspect, according to whether we regard it as resting on natural or supernatural grounds. Christ commissioned His Apostles to teach His doctrines to all nations. Then the fact that Christ gave His Apostles the identical mission which 

He Himself had received from God (St. John 20:21) showed that they participated in the magisterial character of Christ, their Head.

The miracles and prophecies which we have submitted to prove the divinity of Christ's teaching also confirm the teaching of the Apostles, because the teaching

of the Apostles was supported by the same divine evidence. In season and out of season the Apostles reemphasized the divinity of Jesus. Consult a few passages from their writings: "In the beginning was the Word; and the 

Word was with God; and the Word was 

God. The same was in the beginning with 

God. All things were made by Him: and without Him was made nothing that was made. . . . And the light shineth in darkness: and the darkness did not comprehend it." (St. John 1:1-3, 5) "And after eight days, again His disciples were within, and Thomas with them. Jesus cometh, the doors being shut, and stood in the midst and said: 'Peace be to you.'" (St. John 20:26) "Whose are the fathers and of whom is

Christ, according to the flesh, who is over all things, God blessed forever. Amen." (Romans 9:5) "Who being the brightness of His glory and the figure of His substance and upholding all things by the word of His power, making purgation of sins, sitteth on the right hand of the Majesty on high." (Hebrews 1:3)

The Bible frequently mentions the fact that Jesus, the Son of God, assumed our entire human nature. Hence 

He possessed a human body and a rational soul, for the soul which animates the body belongs to man. 

Neither questions from Holy Scripture nor theological arguments establishing the divinity of Christ convinced my new acquaintance. After all, faith is a divine gift not like the conclusion of a problem in mathematics. The interior supernatural grace of faith enlightens the mind and moves the will to believe the truths which God has revealed. 

"TRIUMPH OF FAILURE" 

Having failed to win any admission from this lawyer, I thought it practical to review an incident in the life of 

Christ. "Do you remember," I queried, "that account in the Bible about the young woman caught in sin?” “Oh, yes," he answered, "quite well." "You recall the mob bent on her death? To execute a death sentence, the Jews had to submit each case to the Roman governor, except for adultery. There was nothing said about the man who seduced the poor girl. Could it be that they had a dual morality? At any rate, they were taking no chances on the wellsprings of life. They insisted that the mothers of their children be pure. Singlehanded, Jesus halted the mob and demanded: 'What are you going to do with this girl?' They quoted the law: 'Now Moses in the law commanded us to stone such a one.'' (St. John 8:5)

"They had stones in their hands and were ready to throw them at their victim. The condemned girl knelt at the feet of Christ. He read her truly repentant heart. While these hypocrites quoted the law correctly, they ignored an important factor, which required the accuser to be without guilt. Only then did he have the right to throw the first stone. Christ said: "He that is without sin among you, let him first cast a stone at her.'' (St. John 8:7) "Cowardly, yellow with fear, they stood motionless.

They sensed the fact that Jesus tore the mask from their wicked hearts and found every man there a thousand times blacker than the captive. Then Jesus traced letters in the sand. I have never been able to find out in any commentary what He was writing. I like to think that 

He was writing the sainthood of the sinner and the doom of her accusers. Not one of the mob dared to raise a hand. Stunned, they looked at one another, dropped their stones and went sneaking away like whipped curs. The intended victim with bowed head, kneeling at the feet of Christ, hid her face in shame, covering it with strands of her golden hair that glistened in the rays of the morning sun. "Our Saviour addressed the penitent: 'Woman, where are they that accused thee? 

Hath no man condemned thee?' Who said: 'No man, Lord.' And Jesus said: 'Neither

will I condemn thee. Go, and now sin no more.'' (St. John 8:10-11) 

To the lawyer I said: "And that was divine."

He seemed deeply absorbed and said with a voice almost quivering: "Father, you are talking my language."

Even the well-trained mind of an astute attorney, unimpressed by the logical arguments of theology, could not resist the boundless mercy of God. He got some notion of how the love and mercy of God pursue the sinner. "The bruised reed He shall not break, and smoking flax He shall not quench." (Isaias 42:3) 

To my next question, "Have you read in the Sacred 

Scriptures about St. Mary Magdalene?" the lawyer assented. "Well, she was very beautiful, very wealthy and very bad-in fact, the town sinner. Having heard of the merciful Christ, known as the wonder-worker, she determined to seek Him out. You will recall that on a certain Sabbath Christ and His Apostles accepted a dinner invitation to the home of Simon the Pharisee, who had also invited to dinner some Jewish notables. Simon had not invited Christ because he loved Him and wished to honor Him, but because he hoped our Saviour might entertain his guests with miracles. "Let me first attempt to describe a custom that prevailed in those early days. When a guest arrived, the first ceremony consisted of providing water for the bathing of the feet. If the guest were some person of dis-

tinction, the host would bathe his feet for him. If he were an equal, such as one's own brother, or cousin, or friend, the host would let him bathe his own feet. "Remember how Christ at the Last Supper went the rounds bathing the feet of His Apostles. St. Peter protested and declared: ""Thou shalt never wash my feet.' Jesus answered him: 'If I wash thee not, thou shalt have no part with Me.' Simon Peter saith to 

Him: 'Lord, not only my feet, but also my hands and my head.' Jesus saith to him: 'He that is washed needeth not but to wash his feet, but is clean wholly. And you are clean, but not all.... For I have given you an example, that as I have done to you, so you do also.' (St. John 13:8-10, 15) "The second prevalent courtesy was the anointing with perfumed oil. Generally they broke a container with perfume and poured it over the head of the guest and let it fall down on the tunic and even to the ground. After this second requirement of protocol had been fulfilled, the host then gave the guest the kiss of welcome. "When Jesus entered the home of the Pharisee, they snubbed Him. They did not provide water with which to bathe His Sacred Feet. They did not anoint Him or give Him the kiss of welcome. Christ took the lowest place at the table. Soon an uninvited guest, Mary Magdalene, entered the dining hall. She walked right up to

Jesus and bathed His feet with her tears and dried them with her long brown, wavy hair. Tenderly she kissed His feet, and then broke a rich alabaster box of precious ointment and poured it over His body. The hypocrites looked on, pretended to be shocked, and said within themselves, 'Look at that! If this man, Christ, were the Prophet they say He is, He would not allow this bad woman to come near Him' (see St. Luke 7:39). "Simon and the others might as well have spoken aloud, because Christ read their thoughts. With great restraint our Saviour addressed the host: 'Simon, I have somewhat to say to thee' (St. Luke 7:40). Jesus continued: 'When I came in here you gave Me no water with which to bathe My feet. You knew of this customary courtesy. This woman, whom you condemn, has bathed them with her tears. You did not supply any perfumed ointment. This woman, by her act of anointing 

Me, has prepared My body for its burial' (cf. St. Luke 

7:44, 46; St. John 12:7). "The aroma of this perfume permeated the entire house of Simon the Pharisee. Ever since, the fragrance of the mercy of God has pervaded the House of God. "The mercies of the Lord I will sing forever' (Psalm 88:2). "Our Lord continued: 'You did not give Me the kiss of welcome; she has not stopped kissing My feet.' And then Christ added the words that at this moment are being fulfilled: 'As long as the world lasts, what she has done here today will be told in memory of her' (cf. St. 

Luke 7:45; St. Matthew 26:13).

"There is something to think about in the fact that Judas was invited to the Last Supper. In treacherous betrayal, he forsook the warmth and light of the Divine Presence and went out into darkness. Mary Magdalene, uninvited, left the outside world of darkness and came into the warmth and light of the Saviour's heart. Jesus lifted up Mary Magdalene, the sinner; lifted her up from the dust and the dirt and the dirty insinuations- lifted up Mary Magdalene the sinner, and made her Mary Magdalene the saint. And that was divine."

The lawyer at my side had given a hearing to truth. 

Now he made his profession of faith: "Jesus Christ was divine."

Chapter XII MIRACLES 

MIRACLE-from the Latin "mirari," to won A der is an extraordinary achievement accomplished by God over and above natural forces. A miracle may further be defined as a divinely performed, sensible effect beyond the common order of nature. Evidence of 

God's supreme dominion over all things is found in both the Old and New Testaments. Miracles give encouragement to follow the light.

When Voltaire plotted with his followers to destroy the Catholic Church in France, their first effort was directed to undermine the miraculous, which points to the supernatural. They gained a following by indoctrinating weaklings to disregard the restraint taught by religion. 

Thus these infidels degraded themselves and embarked on a career of vice, rapine, plunder and lust. Despising the truth and compromising justice, men embraced the line of least resistance: "Eat, drink and be merry because tomorrow we die."

The most compelling and important miracle was our 

Saviour's Resurrection from the dead. By His own inherent, divine power Jesus revived His body in the tomb and rose gloriously - - a conclusive proof of His divinity. 

The Apostles cited this fact to confirm the truths they taught. The omnipotence of God works miracles. No created cause can function without the concurrence of 123

divine power. The laws of nature depend on God, who controls every detail of the norms and rules He has made. 

Examples of miracles performed by our divine Lord include raising the dead to life, giving sight to the blind, instant cure of paralytics, cleansing of lepers. Divine 

Wisdom employed miracles to awaken faith and confidence in the people and to brief unbelievers on the supernatural. Jesus used miracles to prove His divinity and the divinity of His mission. 

The Author of creation in His all-wise designs can change the ordinary course of created things, modify, increase or extend them. The great philosopher St. Augustine taught that our Saviour's founding of Christianity without miracles is a greater miracle than those claimed for it.

SUSPENSION of Law 

Because the Almighty Creator still sustains the universe and everyone in it, because His divine providence reaches "from end to end mightily and ordereth all things sweetly," being the efficient cause of all things,

He alone can work miracles either directly or through those delegated by Him. These latter, however, are not to be considered the efficient causes or authors, but only the instrumental causes of the miracles. 

In analyzing the nature of a miracle, the question often arises: In performing a miracle, does God suspend the laws of nature? He could suspend whatever He has made, but such suspension is not necessary, because God, knowing future needs and emergencies, can foreordain

MIRACLES 

125 them from all eternity. For example, the Incarnation of 

Jesus was promised for centuries. It was planned by God before the prophets foretold the details of the miraculous conception and birth of our Saviour, His sacred Passion, and His atonement on Calvary. If the divine Teacher of truth finds it salutary to enlighten our minds through miraculous channels, He will dispose circumstances so that the miracle can be recognized with certainty. The devil and his cohorts often try to fake miracles. They may pretend or devise evil deceptions, but eventually they betray themselves. Evil spirits do not know the future.

They may surmise or guess from natural causes unknown to us.

GOD IS TRUTH 

The truthfulness of God cannot bear witness to deception of any kind. Therefore, God does not work miracles in favor of impostors. The sanctity of God cannot hurl individuals or nations into error by working miracles in favor of false teachers or pseudo-prophets.

The wisdom of God would employ the best means at His disposal to acquaint mankind with His divine will. 

The surest and swiftest means to achieve this is by miracles. Men must believe the testimony of their senses.

The five thousand men and possibly ten thousand women and children, who were well fed by the multiplication of the loaves and fishes did not have to be experts to know their hunger of several days had been satisfied. 

In fact, they witnessed the goodness and kindness of 

God, our Saviour.

Chapter XIII THE SACRED HEART OF JESUS 

TH HIS brief outline of Catholic doctrine should include a word about the widespread devotion to the 

Sacred Heart. 

But first of all, what is devotion? To check the radical meaning of the word, "to devote" comes from the Latin "devovere," to vow; to dedicate by a solemn act or to give up entirely e. g., to devote oneself to science. Those in military service devote themselves to the protection and defense of our country. Then there are acts of devotion which could be fervent prayers or ceremonies of worship. The offering of Holy Mass, meditations on the Way of the Cross, invoking the maternal intercession of the Blessed Virgin Mary, are all forms of devotion.

The Catholic Church, universal and all-embracing, encourages different kinds and degrees of devotion to suit a variety of people, young and old, learned and unlearned. The Church, guiding millions of the faithful for nearly two thousand years, knows human nature and what her children need. Constantly she implores the guiding help of the Holy Spirit.

DOCTRINAL FOUNDATION 

Devotion to the Sacred Heart rests primarily on the doctrines of the Incarnation and the redemption. 126

THE SACRED HEART OF JESUS 

127 Jesus Christ, being perfect God and perfect man, has one divine personality.

One divine Nature, hypostatically united to the humanity of Jesus, is also considered to be united to each part to His Sacred Heart, His Precious Blood, His

Hands, His Feet. Hence every one of these organs merits adoration in view of its union with the Godhead. Therefore, the Sacred Heart of Jesus deserves the same adoration as His divinity, assuming that it is worshiped conjointly with His divine Person.

When you refer, then, to the Sacred Heart, in reality you have in mind the love of God for man, because the heart considered as an organ is the symbol of love. The same can be predicated of the Immaculate Heart of Mary - which means the pure love of God's Blessed 

Mother for all mankind.

Mary has a claim on us and we have a claim on her, because, at a moment of supreme tenderness, Christ dying on the Cross bequeathed to us His Virgin Mother in these words: "Mother, this is your Son." He then said to the disciple (St. John, who in Sacred Scripture represents the people), "This is your Mother" (St. John 

19:27, Kleist-Lilly translation of the New Testament from the Greek).*

The picture of the Heart of our divine Redeemer, encircled with thorns of suffering and aflame with love, has a vivid human interest that words cannot convey. *The New Testament, rendered from the original Greek, translated by James A. Kleist, S. J., and Joseph L. Lilly, C. M. (Milwaukee: Bruce 

Publishing Co., 1954). Used with the permission of the publishers.

For them also” "་་ Indeed, millions of poor people brought low with grief, privation and illness, who cannot understand words, do find bright hope and warmth in this symbol of undying love—immortal and divine.

Our divine Lord in His appearances to St. Margaret 

Mary Alacoque expressed the yearning of His Sacred 

Heart for the love of mankind. He said: "Behold this 

Heart that has loved men so much."

Love is the desire to possess and to be possessed. 

Our Lord loves intensely all rational beings without exception - yes, even sinners who have rejected Him. His arm is stretched out to embrace them. The image of our 

Saviour showing His Heart open to receive everyone stimulates devotion, awakening in the human heart a response to divine love. We pray frequently, trying in our poor human way to express childlike confidence in this symbol of divine love: "Most Sacred Heart of Jesus, I trust in Thee.”

Chapter XIV  

IN ORDER to clarify the doctrine of the Redemption IN of mankind, these reflections start with an analogy. 

Visualize a great kingdom. The subjects are happy and prosperous. One comparatively small province, however, constantly rebels against the king and defies his authority. 

In an effort to enlighten these subjects, the king sends his ambassadors. Instead of receiving the ambassadors in the kindly spirit in which they were sent, the rebels seize the representatives of the throne, torture them, and put them to death. When His Highness hears of this, he determines to send armies to exterminate the province, which had become the source of scandal throughout the kingdom. But the king's son pleads with his father to give these wayward subjects one more chance to reform. 

He begs his royal father: "You love these wicked people. 

I love them too. Let me go in your name and teach them how the mercy of God reaches out to win back even those who defy every law human and divine." The king replies, "I do love these people even though they are evil. It would be heart-rending to destroy them. Go, then, in my name and brief them. Teach them that I ardently desire to win them back, not by fear or punishment, but by loving solicitude."

When the prince came among the ungrateful people, he explained to them that only the loving solicitude of 129

his father had sent him. The prince proved the sincerity of his mission by countless acts of kindness. A few disciples listened and believed in him; others, goaded by jealousy and corrupt lives, incited the majority of the people against him. Finally, the enemies of the prince trumped up false charges against him and in a travesty of justice condemned him to an ignominious death. The prince warned the people of the frightful guilt they would incur by putting him to death unjustly; but the mob that condemned him cried out that they would take the responsibility. Then the prince explained to them that he would suffer agony and death willingly to reconcile them to the king. His last message was: "Take this word to my father, the king, and tell him that I have offered my life's last drop of blood in reparation for all the crimes you have committed, and even for this very grave crime of putting me to death. My father will not ignore the life blood of his own son, offered for your pardon." 

You know, of course, that every comparison lacks something, but this might bring to your mind the general picture of our divine Saviour coming on earth for us and for our salvation. 

When our first parents disobeyed God, they lost original justice and its corresponding gifts, including sanctifying grace with which God had endowed them. 

They were driven forth from the Garden of Paradise.

They and their posterity incurred the punishment of suffering and death. But God in His mercy did not leave them without hope. He promised them a Redeemer.
