Church, He did not exempt her from suffering, sorrow and contradictions. Among these are the heresies and schisms promoted by false teachers who continue to afflict the Bride of Christ. A heretic rejects a doctrine of the 

Church. A schismatic declines to acknowledge the lawful authority of the Church. *Father Mateo Speaks to Priests on Priestly Perfection (Westminster, Md.: Newman Press, 1960), p. 49. Used with the permission of the publishers.

Prominent among these groups are the Greek Orthodox churches and the Protestant churches, which have divided and subdivided, reformed and revised their doctrines. At present, more than four hundred ninety-nine

Protestant sects throughout the world claim to represent Christ, and all contradict one another. The followers of 

Luther assumed the name "Protestant" because they vehemently protested against a decree of the German government which forbade further attempts at promoting new religions until a general council got under way. 

The particular protest from which the heretical sects derive their name was recorded at a Diet (i. e., a legislative session) which convened in the year 1529 at Spires, Germany. The history of Luther and the Protestant sect he founded shows, on the part of the majority of their leaders, not the good fruit of the charity of Christ, but hatred. "By their fruits you shall know them." (St. Matthew 7:16) 

Can an evil tree produce good fruit? In a word, the good fruit is love; the evil fruit is hatred. A glaring example of the evil fruit of an evil tree is Communism. 

We poor human beings have need of divine guidance every hour and every day. Jesus, having in mind our weaknesses and the corresponding remedies, sent the Holy Spirit to enlighten, guide, and teach us all things. 

Above all knowledge and worldly wisdom, the Third

Person of the Blessed Trinity teaches us to exemplify the first and greatest commandment - to love God and to love one another.

Chapter XIX THE ATTRIBUTES OF THE CHURCH 

NOR the Church to function effectively and carry FOR on the mission entrusted to her by Jesus Christ, she must exercise three necessary attributes: namely, authority, infallibility - i. e., freedom from error - and indefectibility.

AUTHORITY 

The success of any organization depends on the authority vested in and exercised by its leaders. In the 

Catholic Church, authority and corresponding obedience are the bond of union. The Pope and the bishops, as the successors of the Apostles, have the right and the solemn duty to teach and govern the members. The definite and firmly established authority exercised explicitly even during years of persecution throughout nineteen centuries reflects the stability of the Church, the visible rock on which her authority was built. 

The faithful recognize the Church's power to enact the legislation necessary to guard the faith, morals, discipline, and the liturgy. These regulations stem from the 

Bible and Tradition, both known as "the Deposit of Faith." Practical Catholics, instructed in their religion, reverence, respect and obey the directives of Holy Mother Church. In a word, they "think with the Church.” "He that heareth you heareth Me.” (St. Luke 10:16) 196

THE ATTRIBUTES OF THE CHURCH 

197 

INFALLIBILITY 

Infallibility, another essential attribute, assures the Church divine protection when teaching a doctrine of faith or morals. The Pope alone, or united with the 

Bishops in general council, is so guided by the Holy Spirit that de facto he makes no mistake in these matters. 

The Catholic Church teaches us the truths revealed by Jesus Christ; moral theology guides us in the practical application of the ten commandments. The solemn declaration of a dogma (an established truth) simply declares that a certain doctrine of faith and/or morals has been revealed by God. A dogma is learned from the 

Bible or Tradition. It should not be considered as a newly discovered truth but as a clarification of what

Christ and the Apostles actually taught, either explicitly or implicitly. What the Pope and bishops teach, the faithful have a corresponding obligation to learn. 

A period of more than nineteen hundred years is an impressive test for any truth or an article of faith to withstand the ravages of time. If the Church had ever erred in pronouncing a doctrine of faith or morals, you may be sure her enemies would have cited the error. Not one Pope in the long history of the Church ever taught a false doctrine. If the Church could err in the teaching of the truths revealed by Jesus Christ, our Lord's promise to be with the Church and to provide a Guiding Spirit and Teacher of truth in the Holy Ghost would not be fulfilled.

"for them also"

INDEFECTIBILITY 

The true Church, founded by Jesus Christ to serve mankind in every generation, must endure until the end of time. She could not fulfill her mission if she ever defaulted or suffered destruction. Jesus promised: "The gates of hell [i. e., the power of hell] shall not prevail against it." (St. Matthew 16:18) "And behold I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the world." (St. Matthew 28:20)

At the consummation of the world, the Church militant here on earth and the Church suffering in purgatory will be united with the Church triumphant in heaven.

As long as the world lasts and as long as mankind needs salvation, the Catholic Church will continue her divinely commissioned apostolate to apply, through the sacraments, the infinite merits of Christ's suffering and death for the salvation of souls.

Chapter XX  

URING the course of the centuries the Church has wisely legislated to solve problems, to stress and clarify passages in Holy Writ, to point out practical applications of her teachings that fit particular times, places, and conditions of the faithful throughout the world. At present a body of legislation called the code of canon law briefs and guides both clergy and laity. A canon is defined as a rule, a standard or measuring rod. Canon law is, therefore, the standard law of the Church. 

Most of the canon laws refer to bishops, the clergy, diocesan officials, the management of ecclesiastical property, and the regulations governing the sacrament of Matrimony. There are particular directives, six in number, which are called the precepts of the Church. 

Jesus Christ, before ascending into heaven, authorized the Apostles and their successors to formulate laws. "If he will not hear the Church, let him be to thee as the heathen and publican. Amen I say to you, whatever you shall bind upon earth shall be bound also in heaven." (St. Matthew 18:17-18) "He that heareth you heareth Me: and he that despiseth you despiseth Me.... 

(St. Luke 10:16) 199

To function efficiently and to fulfill her divine mission, the Church must teach and counsel the faithful. 

Catholics therefore are expected to obey the laws of the Church, which stem from the ten commandments. For example, the first precept of the Church teaches us how to carry out the third commandment of God, and so in like manner with the other precepts.

THE FIRST PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

Those who have reached the age of reason (generally interpreted as the age of seven) are obliged to hear Mass on Sundays and holydays of obligation. Man owes God both public and private worship. This obligation is of divine origin. Christ conferred fullness of power on 

His Church: "As the Father hath sent Me, I also send you" (St. John 20:21). The Council of Jerusalem exercised that power in 52 A. D. by revising the liturgical ceremonies to be observed by the churches. The dedication of Sunday to God by special acts of worship and by refraining from commercial, legal and manual pursuits, had its beginning with the Church. Certain feast days, such as Christmas and the octave day of the Nativity, are sanctified in the same way as Sunday. Regarding these, the Church has established uniformity within the various countries. In the United States the holydays to be observed are: Christmas, the Octave of Christmas, the Ascension, the Assumption, All Saints, and the Immaculate 

Conception. Under penalty of mortal sin, the faithful are obliged to assist in offering Holy Mass with the priest on Sundays and holydays of obligation.

Exemption 

Inability because of sickness or convalescence, the necessity of marketing perishable products, works of charity for instance, attending one seriously ill — are examples of exemptions from the law obliging Catholics to hear Mass on these days. 

To satisfy the obligation of Sunday and holyday Mass, a worshiper must be present and know that the adorable Sacrifice is in progress. Thus a person who was sleeping during Mass would not satisfy the precept. Because of the great influx of people, one might not be able to enter the church; but outside, even at a distance of two hundred feet, he would be morally present. The ringing of the church bell and other signs make known the progress of the various parts of the Mass. 

THE SECOND PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

"To fast and abstain on the days appointed." Fasting

Fasting pertains to limiting the amount of food taken during the day. Fast thus has to do with the quantity of food. The Church law admits but one full meal on a day of fast. A full meal is one in which we take as much as we wish, with due regard for temperance. Meat may be taken at the full meal on a day of fast, unless it is also a day of complete abstinence (see below, page 203).

In addition to the full meal, two other meatless meals may be taken on a fast day, sufficient to keep up one's

strength. The amount of food for these two meals should together not equal the amount of food consumed at the principal meal.

Our divine Lord does not impose penances that impair health or preclude normal work. If additional nourishment is indicated on a fast day, it may be taken. 

Drinks not having the nature of food; light beverages such as wine, fruit juice, tea, coffee, milk, and an orange are allowed between meals. Sugar and cream may be used in the drink.

Excused from the law of fasting are: (1) Those employed in strenuous work; (2) The infirm and the convalescent (the doctor's directions for diet should be followed); (3) Pregnant and nursing mothers; (4) Those in military service who cannot choose their food; (5) The poor and those on community welfare rations. 

Others in doubt will consult their pastor or confessor. 

The precept of fasting binds all Catholics who have completed their twenty-first year and who have not yet reached their fifty-ninth birthday. 

The days on which fasting is prescribed are: (1) all days of Lent except Sundays; (2) the ember days; (3) the vigils of Christmas,* Pentecost, and the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin Mary. *The fast and abstinence attached to the vigil of Christmas may be observed on December 23.

Abstinence

Abstinence is something quite different from fasting. 

The law of abstinence prohibits the use of flesh meat and of soup or gravy made from meat. Bacon drippings and lard may be used in the preparation of food-for instance, in frying eggs.

In recently promulgated legislation, the bishops of the United States, using the provisions of canon law as modified through special faculties granted by the Holy See, have made a distinction between days of partial abstinence and days of complete abstinence. On days of partial abstinence meat and soup or gravy made from meat may be taken at the principal meal. These days are 

Ember Wednesdays and Saturdays, and the vigil of

Pentecost. On days of complete abstinence, meat and soup or meat gravy may not be taken at all. The days of complete abstinence are all Fridays, Ash Wednesday, the vigil of the Immaculate Conception and of Christmas.* 

Vigils (watches) serve as preparation for the celebration of great feasts in the Church. It was the custom at one time to spend part of the night preceding a religious feast in church, fervently praying and meditating on the sufferings and death of Christ.

When Christmas falls on Friday the law of abstinence does not hold. Also, on certain other special occasions, the bishop may dispense those within his diocese from the law of abstinence, or from both fast and abstinence. 

The salutary custom of fast and abstinence on the days specified follows the example given us by our divine

Lord. The Friday abstinence commemorates the suffering and death of Jesus on the first Good Friday. The spiritual advantages of mortification are evident. Even the ancient pagans knew that it was good for man to deprive himself. The Bible in many passages commends fast and abstinence as a remedy for concupiscence. Selfdenial builds up the spiritual strength to overcome temptations. "I chastise my body and bring it into subjection; lest perhaps, when I have preached to others, I myself should become a castaway.” (1 Corinthians 9:27) 

Members of the medical profession, Catholic and non-Catholic, also recognize the physical advantages accruing from days and seasons of fast and abstinence. 

Ember Days

The ember days mentioned above are days of fast and abstinence the Church designates for the sanctification of the four seasons. There are twelve ember days: three each for the seasons of spring, summer, autumn and winter. They occur on the Wednesday, Friday, and 

Saturday after the first Sunday of Lent; after Pentecost; after the feast of the Holy Cross, September 14; after the feast of St. Lucy, December 13: Our acts of penance on these days serve as reparation for our sins, and are intended to beg of God the graces necessary to correct them. The ember days also are observed to obtain God's

special blessing on the clergy, whose ordination often takes place on an Ember Saturday.

THE THIRD PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

Catholics are solemnly obliged to confess their sins at least once a year. To obtain the graces necessary to lead a good life, we frequent this sacrament of Divine Mercy. The precept of annual confession binds all who have attained the use of reason. This generally includes children from the age of seven. 

The confession should be sincere, humble and entire.

The purpose of amendment is not an oath that we shall never sin again. It is, however, a firm resolution that, with the help of God's grace, we shall remove from our lives sin and occasions of sini. e., persons, places and things that lead us into sin. We are never tempted beyond our strength. God permits these trials to test our sincerity and to fortify our resistance to evil. "Let no temptation take hold on you, but such as is human [incident to man]. And God is faithful who will not suffer you to be tempted above that which you are able: but will make also with temptation issue, that you may be able to bear it." 1 Corinthians 10:13) 

THE FOURTH PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

This precept obliges the faithful to receive worthily Holy Communion during the Easter time. It binds under

pain of mortal sin, and obliges all Catholics who have the use of reason. In the United States, the Easter time is reckoned from the first Sunday of Lent up to and including Trinity Sunday.

Our personal sanctification is the will of God. Christ taught His Apostles: "Except you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink His blood, you shall not have life in you." (St. John 6:54)

THE FIFTH PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

"To contribute to the support of the Church." This, the fifth precept of the Church, obliges us in conscience to share a reasonable part of the expenses of our parish church and school, and of the financial burden of the diocese. St. Paul taught that those "who preach the Gospel should live by the Gospel" (1 Corinthians 

9:14). In the old dispensation, people considered it a serious duty to support the temple by contributing a tithe (ten per cent) of all their possessions. At other times a tax was levied to meet the related overhead. In the 

United States the custom prevails of accepting voluntary offerings in accord with the donor's income. Some of the faithful are generous in this regard. In fact, they carry the burden of those who default.

THE SIXTH PRECEPT OF THE CHURCH 

The sixth precept of the Church is to observe the laws of the Church concerning marriage.

A Catholic can contract a true marriage only in the presence of an authorized priest and two witnesses.

Catholics are also forbidden to marry non-Catholics, or those related to them within the third degree of blood relationship. That is, they may not marry their second cousin or any relative closer than a second cousin.

Examples of other marriages forbidden by the Church are: that of a boy who is not yet sixteen or a girl who is not yet fourteen; the marriage between a godparent and a godchild; marriage between a widow or a widower and the near relatives of a deceased spouse.

The Church also forbids the solemnization of marriage at forbidden times. The penitential seasons of Lent and Advent are designated as forbidden times. An urgent and valid reason may be presented to the bishop to obtain a dispensation for a solemn wedding (that is, one with a Nuptial Mass) during these times. Otherwise Catholics who wish to marry during Lent or Advent may do so, provided they are married without pomp and undue publicity. Regarding this and various other dispensations, the pastor should be consulted one month in advance of the scheduled marriage.

In accordance with the code of canon law, an attempted marriage by a Catholic before a justice of the peace or a Protestant minister is null and void. For a baptized person, marriage is a sacrament. The contract and the sacrament cannot be separated. Those who have entered an invalid marriage should promptly consult their pastor. 

Alert practical Catholics entering the married state, aware of eternal values, are married at a Nuptial Mass,

as this manifests their reverence for the sacredness of Matrimony and invokes special graces on their wedded lives. Our divine Lord accepted the invitation to the marriage at Cana. This was, in fact, His first public appearance. He should be invited to every Christian marriage.

Chapter XXI THE HOLY MASS THE MASS AS A SACRIFICE 

ROM the beginning of time man expressed his FR loyalty, love and gratitude to God by a sacrificial act. The most important liturgy in the Catholic Church centers around the Holy Mass. "Liturgy" means the rites prescribed for the public and official worship of the Church. The sublime character of this solemn ritual, typified from ancient times and foretold by the prophet Malachias, continues on our altars - the true, eternal oblation.

The Mass is divine. This explains why Catholics are privileged to offer the Mass with the celebrant. The 

Holy Sacrifice and the sacred priesthood are correlative terms. The celebrant at the altar pleads for all, acts for 

Christ, who perpetuates the Sacrifice wrought on Calvary. The reality of the redemptive immolation of Jesus remains in every Mass, even though the repetition of Calvary takes place in an unbloody manner. The consummation of the Sacrifice of the Cross had for its victim the body of Christ conceived, nourished and protected by the Blessed Virgin Mary. Her decisive fiat at the Annunciation made possible both Priest and Victim for this 

Sacrifice. Jesus then associated His Blessed Mother with 

Himself on Calvary, as He does in every Mass. 209

While we must admit many beautiful devotions approved by the Church, and practiced by the faithful, none is more important and fruitful than the offering of Holy Mass. It embraces the three mysteries of the Credo of our Catholic religion: (a) the Incarnation, the mystery of love; (b) the adorable Sacrifice of Jesus on the

Cross, the mystery of atonement and redemption; (c) the 

Holy Eucharist, the gift of love.

THE MASS VESTMENTS 

The priest wears a cassock or soutane, which indicates that he is a cleric dedicated to God and detached from the things of the world. Similarly, a judge on the bench wears a black robe which symbolizes his detachment from all worldly considerations in the administration of justice. We surround the Holy Sacrifice of the 

Mass with all possible reverence and respect. The faithful wish the sacred ceremonies to be performed with becoming dignity. You yourself would not want to see the priest walk in off the highway and up to the altar without the proper preparation. The priest, then, must prepare for Holy Mass, first by prayer and then by donning vestments, each one of which carries its own special significance. Before vesting, the celebrant washes his hands to indicate the great purity a priest must have before handling the sacred vestments, and also for the practical reason to remove any dust that may have accumulated on his fingers.

THE HOLY MASS 

211 

The Amice 

The first vestment placed around the shoulders is called an Amice, from the Latin word "amicire," which means to cover. The Amice, made of linen about twice the size of the ordinary handkerchief, is tucked around the neck, and protects the ornaments on the stole and chasuble. It has, however, the symbolism of a helmet, as indicated by the prayer recited when the priest puts on the Amice: "Place upon my head, O Lord, the helmet of salvation, that I may overcome the deceits of the devil."

The Alb 

The long white linen robe that completely covers the black cassock is called an Alb, from the Latin word “albus," signifying "white." The related prayer explains its significance: "Purify me, O Lord, and free my soul from every stain, that having been cleansed in the Blood of the Lamb, I may one day enjoy eternal happiness."

The Cincture 

To hold the Alb in place, a Cincture or double, heavy cord is placed around the waist. It reminds the priest of the self-restraint he must exercise and also recalls the ropes which tied our divine Lord to the pillar in His cruel scourging. The related prayer: "Bind me, O Lord, with the cincture of purity and extinguish in me the temptations of the flesh that the virtue of continence and chastity may abide in me."

The Maniple 

The Maniple, a band of cloth of the color of the day, is worn on the left arm. Formerly it was a linen cloth that served as a handkerchief. In the course of time it developed into a vestment. It now signifies the burdens of the office of the priesthood, which are to be borne courageously. The related prayer: "May I be worthy, O

Lord, to wear the maniple of weeping and sorrow, that with exultation I may receive the reward of my labors."

The Stole 

The Stole, a long band of material which is placed around the neck, is an ancient emblem of authority. In ancient times governors and judges wore it as a distinctive badge of their jurisdiction. The priest recites the following prayer in donning the Stole: "Restore unto me, O Lord, I beseech Thee, the stole of immortality which

I lost through the sin of my first parents; and, although unworthy to approach Thy sacred mysteries, may I nevertheless merit eternal joys." 

The Chasuble

The Chasuble, which is generally ornamented with a cross, is the chief outer garment of the priest. It represents the necessity of the priest's taking up his cross and following Christ. The celebrant recites this prayer while investing himself with the chasuble: "O Lord, who hast said, 'My yoke is sweet and My burden light,' grant that 

I may so carry my cross as to attain Thy grace."

THE HOLY MASS 

213 

COLORS OF THE VESTMENTS 

Regarding the colors, white or gold is used on feast days concerned with the joyous and glorious mysteries of our Lord's life, such as Christmas and Easter; on feasts of Our Lady; of the angels and of saints who were not martyred. It symbolizes joy. 

Red is used on feasts of our Lord commemorating 

His Passion; on feasts of the Holy Spirit and of martyrs for the faith.

Green, the emblem of hope, is worn on the Sundays after the Epiphany and Pentecost.

Black, the color of mourning, is used on Good Friday and in Masses for the Dead.

Violet symbolizes penance and reparation. It is used during Advent and Lent and on the Sundays immediately preceding Lent, as well as on ember days and vigils. 

THE ROBES OF SANCTIFYING GRACE 

The reader may have already concluded that the content of this book represents the talks addressed to the 

Dogma Seminar held weekly at the University of San

Diego, as well as television and radio broadcast programs. Following forty minutes of the explanation of a doctrine, questions were invited from the floor. One question, very often posed, deserves an answer. You are familiar with St. Matthew's account of the parable of the marriage feast, herewith quoted. "The kingdom of heaven is likened to a king who made a marriage for his son. And he

sent his servants to call them that were invited to the marriage; and they would not come. Again he sent other servants, saying: "Tell them that were invited, Behold, I have prepared my dinner; my beeves and fatlings are killed, and all things are ready. Come ye to the marriage.' But they neglected and went their ways, one to his farm and another to his merchandise. And the rest laid hands on his servants and, having treated them contumeliously, put them to death. But, when the king had heard of it, he was angry; and sending his armies, he destroyed those murderers and burnt their city. Then he saith to his servants: "The marriage indeed is ready; but they that were invited were not worthy. Go ye therefore into the highways; and as many as you shall find, call to the marriage.' And his servants, going forth into the ways, gathered together all that they found, both bad and good; and the marriage was filled with guests. And the king went in to see the guests; and he saw there a man who had not on a wedding garment. And he saith to him: 'Friend, how camest thou in hither not having on a wedding garment?' But he was silent. 

Then the king said to the waiters: 'Bind his hands and feet, and cast him into the exterior darkness. There shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth.' (St. Matthew 22:2-13) 

Both Catholics and non-Catholics queried: Inasmuch as the guests were quickly and indiscriminately assem-

THE HOLY MASS 

215 bled from highways and byways, how could anyone expect them to be properly attired? When the king surveyed the guests in the banquet hall he discovered a man without a wedding garment. When challenged, the culprit had no defense and offered none. The king's sentence of this individual could not be justified without a definite reason.* And there was a reason. According to the ancient protocol, guests invited by royalty were received by a master of ceremonies, who ushered them to the wardrobe room of the palace. These wardrobe rooms were elaborate and elegantly equipped. In fact, some kings measured their wealth by the wealth of their wardrobe. The master of the wardrobe decked out the guests to fit each one's rank and position. For example, members of royal households were clothed with ermine. 

Dukes and counts donned brocade, while others, of lower rank, received satin or silk tunics. Everyone found it easy to be properly garbed. The particular individual singled out, for example, spurned the wardrobe and thereby his royal host. Nevertheless, he boldly entered the dining room for the feast. Hence he was immediately discovered and ejected into exterior darkness. The darkness outside is mentioned in contrast to the brilliantly illuminated banquet hall. *Rev. Mark Kennedy, O. F. M., M. A., S. S. Lect. Glis., in A Catholic Commentary on the New Testament by the Catholic Biblical Association (Paterson, N J., St. Anthony Guild Press, 1942); cf. also, Rev.

Alexander Jones, S. T. L., L. S. S., Professor of Sacred Scripture and 

Hebrew, Upholland, Wigan, in A Catholic Commentary on Holy Scripture, edited by Dom Bernard Orchard and others (London, Thomas 

Nelson and Sons, 1957).

The parable unfolds itself when, by allegory, the wardrobe room is to be taken as the confessional, where the soul is cleansed in the Precious Blood of the Lamb of God and adorned with the rich robes of sanctifying grace. Thus the guests are worthy to appear at the banquet table of our Lord and receive in Holy Communion, the Body, Blood, Soul and Divinity of our Lord Jesus 

Christ under the appearance of bread.

Chapter XXII  

HE word "Mass" derives from the Latin "missio"

TH 

dismissal. In the early days of the Church, worshipers were dismissed: (a) unbelievers before the

Gloria: (b) catechumens before the Offertory: and 

(c) all the faithful at the end of Mass: "Ite, missa est 

Go, the Mass is ended."

Significant prayers and appropriate accompanying ceremonies lead us step by step to the twofold consecration, the essential of the eucharistic Sacrifice. "The unbloody immolation of the divine Victim is shown in a mystical manner by the separation of the sacred species and by the offering of them which is made to the Eternal Father. The august Sacrifice of the altar concludes with the Communion of the divine banquet. But the integrity of the Sacrifice, as everyone knows, requires only that the priest himself partake of the heavenly food." 

(Pius XII, Mediator Dei, 1947) Part One 

The first part of Holy Mass extends to the Offertory; it serves as a preparation or introduction to the Canon. 

More than merely being present or attending, the faith- 217

ful have the sublime privilege of offering the Holy Sacrifice, with the priest, to Almighty God. This is the most solemn act of worship that a creature can pay the Creator. 

That is why at the Offertory the priest turns towards the people and says: "Pray, brethren, that your sacrifice and mine may be acceptable to God the Father Almighty...."

PRAYERS AT THE FOOT OF THE ALTAR 

The Mass is introduced by the Sign of the Cross and the antiphon for Psalm 42. "I will go unto the altar of God; to God who giveth joy to my youth.'

THE CONFITEOR 

After the recitation of Psalm 42 (omitted in Masses for the Dead and those of Passiontime because of its cheerful character), the celebrant of the Mass recites the Confiteor, in which he accuses himself before God. He bows down in humble attitude, and, calling to mind our Saviour's anguish in the Garden of Gethsemani, he begs pardon for his sins and failures. The faithful also ask forgiveness, that they may worthily share the fruits of this unbloody repetition of the Sacrifice of Calvary. 

At the end of the Confiteor, the priest, with joined hands, recites a prayer calling upon Almighty God to absolve the faithful from their sins.

After concluding the prayers at the foot of the altar, the celebrant, extending and joining his hands, invites the prayers of those present, saying, "Let us pray," as he ascends the steps of the altar.

The priest venerates the sepulcher of the altar stone, which encloses relics of saints and martyrs. In the first ages of Christianity, altars were erected on the tombs of saints and martyrs.

In a high Mass the celebrant incenses the altar, praying that his prayer and the prayers of the faithful who offer the adorable Sacrifice will ascend to God like the sweet fragrance of incense. The smoke of the incense is symbolic of honor, of prayer, of zeal, and of the fragrance of virtue. "Let my prayer be directed as incense in 

Thy sight; the lifting up of my hands as evening sacrifice" 

(Psalm 140:2). 

On Sundays and major feasts of the Church, a solemn Mass is celebrated; hence the expression "high 

Mass," which means a sung Mass as distinguished from a "low Mass," which is read in a low tone of voice. 

THE INTROIT 

The Latin word "Introit" means "he enters." Formerly an entire Psalm, the Introit was sung as the priest entered the sanctuary. Today it consists of an antiphon, part of a Psalm and the Gloria Patri. The Introit gen erally gives the keynote of the Mass; for example, in 

Masses for the Dead, the Introit reads, "Eternal rest grant unto them, O Lord...." The Introits for Christmas and Easter herald notes of joy.

THE KYRIE 

To indicate the universality of the Church, two Greek phrases, "Kyrie, eleison," and "Christe, eleison," are

used here to invoke the mercy of God. (There are Hebrew words such as "amen," "alleluia," and "sabaoth," found in other parts of the Mass.)

THE GLORIA 

On Sundays and on feast days, following the Kyrie the priest recites or chants the Gloria in Excelsis Deo, the hymn of the angels at the birth of Christ. What follows in the Gloria sums up the purpose of life, and also the four ends for which the Holy Sacrifice is offered: adoration, thanksgiving, expiation, and petition.

At the conclusion of the Gloria, the priest reverences again the relics in the altar stone. He then salutes the people with the words, "The Lord be with you." They answer: "And with thy spirit." This reciprocal salutation, recurring five times during the Mass, stresses the union of priest and people in offering the Holy Sacrifice. 

THE ORATIONS 

The orations or prayers said at this part of the Mass are called the Collects, from the Latin "collecta" (assembly or gathering); they are a group of prayers related to the feast day or the intention for which the Mass is offered. (In some Masses there may be only one Collect.) In early Christian times the word "collect" designated the gathering of priest and faithful in a convenient nearby place, where they recited a prayer prior to the procession to the church.

THE EPISTLE 

The Epistle and the Gospel which occur in every Mass re-emphasize doctrines calculated to stir the faith and devotion of the people. To express grateful homage to the divine Benefactor for the advantages of these glad tidings, the priest and the people pray at the conclusion of the Epistle: "Thanks be to God."

THE GRADUAL, ALLELUIA, SEQUENCE AND TRACT 

After the Epistle a brief prayer called the Gradual is read. The Latin word "gradus" means "a step." The

Gradual takes its name from the fact that it was chanted or read from the lower step of a special pulpit called an "ambo." 

The Gradual renews the thought or message of the Introit. On solemn feasts the Alleluia (a Hebrew word meaning "Praise God") is added to the Introit and

Gradual. Masses for some feasts, such as Easter, Pentecost and Corpus Christi, include a hymn called the Sequence, which narrates the highlights of the feast or gives an instruction in the form of a canticle. In solemn funeral Masses the hymn consists of the Dies Irae. During the seasons of Lent and Advent, and other times of penance, the Church omits the Alleluia and substitutes a prayer called the Tract, which is a manifestation of sorrow and repentance. The Latin word "tractus" means "motion." The faithful so named it because it was once sung straight through without any response or interruption — i. e., “in uno tractu," in one motion. 

---

THE GOSPEL 

The priest, standing before the tabernacle, bows profoundly, and begs Almighty God to purify both his heart and his lips that he may worthily announce the holy

Gospel. 

At the conclusion of the Gospel occurs the prayer: "Praise be to Thee, O Christ." The celebrant then kisses the printed text of the holy Gospel, praying: "By the merits of this Gospel, may our sins be blotted out." In a gesture of reverence and humility the priest extends his hands, reciting the Nicene Creed (on Sundays and the principal feasts).

THE NICENE CREED 

"Creed" means a summary of the chief articles of faith. For public use the Catholic Church employs three versions, namely: (1) the Apostles' Creed; (2) the 

Athanasian Creed; and (3) the Nicene Creed. (1) The Apostles' Creed, composed by the Apostles, states twelve fundamental doctrines of our holy religion. 

In the early Church, the catechumens, or those under instruction, were required to learn and recite the Apostles' 

Creed before receiving the sacrament of Baptism. 

(2) The Athanasian Creed, attributed to St. Atha nasius, is accepted by many Protestant sects. It summarizes certain doctrines regarding the Trinity and the Incarnation. On Trinity Sunday priests recite the Athanasian Creed at Prime, one of the little Hours of the

Divine Office, the official prayer of the Church.

3) The Nicene Creed, which has been used in the Church since the fourth century, originated in the First Council of Nicaea, 325 A. D. It covers the principal doctrines of our religion and includes a profession of faith.

Part Two 

We now come to the second part of Holy Mass, which extends from the Offertory to the Communion. 

The preceding part of the Holy Sacrifice was known in ancient times as the Mass of the Catechumens, being named for those under instruction who were being prepared for baptism. At the beginning of the Offertory, the catechumens retired from the Church. 

This section of the Mass includes its three principal parts: the Offertory, the Consecration, and the Communion. Formerly these three constituted the Mass of the 

Faithful, because only those in the state of grace, those who had retained their baptismal innocence, remained for the sacred mysteries. This ancient custom points to the reverence and sanctity surrounding the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass.

Again reminding the faithful that they are united to the priest in offering the Holy Sacrifice, the celebrant kisses the reliquary of the altar, and turning to the people, extends his hands and says, "The Lord be with you." 

They answer: "And with thy spirit."

THE OFFERTORY VERSE 

In the early days of the Church, the Offertory Verse, made up of a Psalm from the holy Bible, was chanted while the faithful approached the sanctuary to offer suitable gifts of bread and wine for the sacrifice. The custom now obtains for the Church to provide special bread, made of pure whole wheat flour; and pure wine, made of grapes. At the Last Supper, Jesus offered bread and wine for the Sacrifice of the Mass.

THE OBLATION OF THE HOST 

Placing the bread, or host, as it is called, on the paten (a small gold plate which covers the chalice), the priest lifts it up, beseeching our heavenly Father to accept it from his hands on behalf of all the faithful, who should unite their intentions with the priest in offering themselves to God as a living host, so that in all things God may be glorified. During the above-described offering, the priest recites the prayer for the offering of the host. 

At the conclusion of this prayer, the celebrant makes the Sign of the Cross with the paten before he places the host on the corporal. Then, going to the Epistle side of the altar, the celebrant prepares the chalice by pouring in wine and a little water. As he does so, he says this prayer: "O God, who in a wonderful manner didst create and ennoble human nature, and still more wonderfully hast renewed it: grant that by the mystery of this water and wine we may be made partakers of His divinity, who

vouchsafed to become partaker of our humanity, Jesus Christ, Thy Son, our Lord. 

Who liveth and reigneth with Thee in the unity of the Holy Ghost, one God, world without end. Amen."

Following an ancient custom, Christ mixed a little water with the wine at the Last Supper. Three mystical reasons explain this ceremony in the Mass. (1) It indicates the union of the human with the divine nature in Jesus Christ; (2) It signifies the blood and water that issued from His Sacred Side on the Cross; (3) St. John says that wine represents our divine Lord, water represents the people. The mingling of wine with water for the sacrifice symbolizes the union of Christ with the faithful: "I am the Vine: you the branches. He that abideth in Me, and I in him, the same beareth much fruit: for without Me you can do nothing" (St. John 15:5).

The Vine vitalizes the branches; as members of the spiritual Body of Christ Jesus, we live and move and have our being. 

Raising the chalice, the priest says: "We offer unto

Thee, O Lord, the chalice of salvation, humbly begging of Thy mercy, that it may arise before Thy divine majesty with a pleasing fragrance for our salvation, and that of all the world. Amen." 

Before placing the chalice on the corporal, the celebrant makes the Sign of the Cross with it, then covers it with the pall (a square piece of cardboard covered with linen, and blessed for sacred use).

Bowing down before the center of the altar, the celebrant begs favor in the sight of the Almighty.

Raising his eyes to the crucifix over the altar, and extending his hands, the celebrant continues to pray. 

In solemn high Masses, the incensation takes place here. As the priest blesses the incense, he invokes the intercession of St. Michael, then incenses the "oblata" the bread and wine prepared for the sacrifice. In token of reverence, the altar, the clergy and the people are also incensed at a solemn high Mass. 

Returning to the Epistle side of the altar, the celebrant washes his hands to cleanse them, and also to indicate the purity of heart and soul with which we ought to approach these sacred mysteries. The faithful silently recite with the priest the prayer called the Lavabo. 

Glancing at the crucifix above the altar, then bowing in adoration, the priest joins his hands and prays to the 

Holy Trinity.

After this prayer, the celebrant reverences the sepulcher containing the relics of martyrs, and turns towards the people. Extending and joining his hands, he invites the faithful to pray with him. The first words of this prayer are recited aloud, "Orate, fratres Pray, brethren." 

THE SECRET PRAYERS 

The Secret Prayers are so called because they are said in a low voice. Here again the celebrant humbly invokes our eternal Father graciously to receive the offering of sacrifice meant to glorify God, and to call down divine assistance upon the people. These prayers vary according to the feast, and in some Masses there is only one Secret.

The number of Collects, Secrets, and Postcommunion prayers may vary in accord with the directions given in the Ordo-a liturgical calendar which guides the priest as to the prayers for the changeable parts of the Mass. 

THE PREFACE 

Concluding the Secret Prayers, the priest intones the Preface to the Canon of the Mass. In this sublime introduction, the celebrant invites the people to lift up their hearts to adore, to praise, to thank God. The Preface varies according to the feast or the liturgical season. 

THE SANCTUS 

The prophet Isaias, in a vision of heaven, heard the seraphim sing this canticle. The Church has added the 

Benedictus, a hymn of rejoicing which the children and their elders chanted when our Lord entered Jerusalem on the first Palm Sunday.

The Canon of the Mass The name "Canon" describes that part of the Mass from the Sanctus to the final ablution, when the priest takes wine and water into the chalice after Holy Communion. "Canon" means a rule or standard, or measuring rode. g., canon law, standard regulations. The Canon of the Mass follows a fixed rule, according to which the Holy Sacrifice of the New Testament is offered.

PRAYER FOR THE CHURCH 

In a prayer of supplication the priest extends, then elevates, and joins his hands. He then raises his eyes towards the crucifix above the altar to impress on his heart the memory of Christ's suffering and death. The petition that he now makes, one of the most sublimely worded in the liturgy, is for the Church on earth and her shepherds.

MEMENTO OF THE LIVING 

The celebrant may remember in the Mass the living as well as the dead. Here the faithful mention thè intentions for which they are offering the Holy Sacrifice; they unite their intentions with those of the priest, who recommends all these petitions to God.

VENERATION OF THE SAINTS 

The two previous prayers have been for the Church on earth the Church militant. The priest now recites a prayer commemorating the saints in heaven - the 

Church triumphant.

PRAYERS BEFORE THE CONSECRATION 

During the first of these two prayers the priest extends his hands over the host and chalice, signifying, according to an ancient tradition, that the one offering the sacrifice also offers his own life to glorify God. 

During the second prayer he makes the Sign of the 

Cross over the offerings.

THE CONSECRATION 

Note that the ritual of the Mass re-enacts the actions and the words of our divine Lord at the Last Supper. "Who, the day before He suffered, took bread into His holy and venerable hands, and with His eyes lifted up to heaven, unto Thee, O 

God, His almighty Father, giving thanks to

Thee, blessed it, broke it, and gave it to His disciples, saying: "Take and eat ye all of this.'

The priest takes the unconsecrated bread in both hands, then pronounces the words of consecration with deep-felt reverence and devotion: "For this is My Body."

The separate consecration of the bread and wine symbolizes the death of our divine Lord.

Following the consecration, the priest kneels in adoration, then elevates the Sacred Host for the people to adore. Again, after placing the Blessed Sacrament on the altar, the priest kneels in profound reverence. Preliminary to the consecration of the most Precious Blood of our Saviour, the celebrant says: "In like manner, after He had supped, taking also this excellent chalice into His holy and venerable hands, and giving thanks to Thee,

He blessed it and gave it to His disciples, saying: (Here the celebrant pronounces the words of consecration over the chalice, which he holds slightly elevated.)

"Take, and drink ye all of this, for this is the chalice of My Blood, of the new and eternal testament: the mystery of faith, which shall be shed for you and for many, unto the remission of sins."

The celebrant then replaces the chalice on the corporal, saying: "As often as ye shall do these things, ye shall do them in remembrance of Me."

He genuflects in adoration, then elevates the sacred chalice containing the Precious Blood. During this solemn ceremony we call to mind how our divine Saviour was elevated on the Cross; we also think of His very consoling promise: that He would lift up all mankind to Himself to share in the merits of His Passion and death. 

During both elevations of the Blessed Sacrament, the faithful may either bow their heads or gaze reverently while praying: "My Lord and my God."

FOLLOWING THE CONSECRATION 

The first of the prayers now said reminds the faithful of the main events connected with the Redemption. The second begs God to accept the Holy Sacrifice that is being offered, and the third is a solemn plea that the sacrificial gifts may be brought before the throne of God, and that those who receive the Body and Blood of our Lord may be granted eternal blessings.

MEMENTO FOR THE DEAD 

Here both priest and the faithful recommend to God their dear departed, especially those for whom they are bound to pray: parents, relatives, and benefactors. 

"NOBIS QUOQUE PECCATORIBUS" 

To invite the faithful to unite with him in praying for the people, the priest raises his voice a little and continues to pray, reciting the prayer that begins, “And to us sinners, also...."

The celebrant, before making a genuflection, uncovers the chalice, takes the Sacred Host in his right hand, and makes the Sign of the Cross three times over the chalice, saying: "Through Him, and with Him, and in Him, be unto Thee, O God the Father, Almighty, in the unity of the Holy Ghost, all honor and glory."

THE OUR FATHER 

This prayer, composed by our divine Lord, granted the request of the Apostles: "Lord, teach us how to pray." 

"LIBERA Nos" 

Taking the paten in his hand, the priest makes the Sign of the Cross with it, kisses it in a token of reverence, and prays in a low voice, "Libera nos, ... Domine - 

Deliver us, O Lord...."

The celebrant uncovers the chalice, genuflects, takes the Sacred Host, and breaks it in the middle, holding it over the chalice, saying:

"Through the same Jesus Christ Thy Son, our

Lord, who liveth and reigneth with Thee, in the unity of the Holy Ghost, God, world without end. Amen."

Placing half of the Sacred Host on the paten, the priest then breaks off a small part of the other half, with which he makes the Sign of the Cross over the chalice, saying: "May the peace of the Lord be always with you.” 

The acolyte and people answer: "And with thy spirit.”

The celebrant then drops the small particle of the Sacred Host into the chalice to represent the reunion of the Body and Blood of Jesus Christ at His Resurrection. 

"AGNUS DEI"

Before genuflecting, the priest covers the chalice; then, bowing down, he strikes his breast three times in compunction, saying the prayer "Agnus Dei - Lamb of 

God" each time.

After the Agnus Dei follows a prayer for the peace and unity of the Church. During the first centuries of 

Christianity, when the people exemplified the charity of Christ, at this part of the Mass they gave one another the kiss of peace. In fact, the first Catholics were known for their mutual love. To keep alive this cherished virtue, at solemn high Masses the clergy salute one another, saying, "Peace be with you," and replying, “And with thy spirit."

The celebrant then recites prayers appropriate for the devout reception of Holy Communion, in which the faithful may join. No prayers in preparation for Holy

Communion could be more efficacious than those from the Ordinary of the Mass.

The priest makes a genuflection and, taking the Sacred Host in his hands, says: "I will take the heavenly 

Bread, and call upon the name of the Lord."

Slightly inclining before the tabernacle, the priest takes both parts of the Sacred Host and, striking his breast, says three times: "Lord, I am not worthy that Thou shouldst enter under my roof; but only say the word and my soul shall be healed."

He then makes the Sign of the Cross with the Host, holding it in his right hand, and imploring: "May the Body of our Lord Jesus Christ keep my soul unto life everlasting. Amen."

After a brief meditation, the celebrant uncovers the chalice, genuflects, gathers whatever fragments may have fallen on the corporal into the chalice. Here a prayer of thanksgiving to our Lord is recited, which consists of part of Psalm 115. 

Taking the chalice in his right hand, and with it making the Sign of the Cross, he reverently receives the 

Precious Blood: "May the Blood of our Lord Jesus Christ keep my soul unto life everlasting. Amen."

THE COMMUNION OF THE FAITHFUL 

The priest now recites the prayer "Ecce, Agnus Dei,' then the "Domine, non sum dignus," the latter three times.

If one should be conscious of mortal sin, then the sacrament of Penance is necessary for the worthy reception of Holy Communion, the preparation for which should always include a sincere act of contrition. In case of doubt, make a good act of contrition, and receive Holy Communion. It is assumed, of course, that those approaching the altar have been fasting from solids and alcoholic beverages for three hours and from liquids for one hour except for the taking of water.

SPIRITUAL COMMUNION 

When one cannot receive Communion sacramentally, it is well to make a spiritual Communion, which consists in an ardent desire to receive Jesus in the Most Blessed 

Sacrament, and to express our love for Him.

THE ABLUTIONS 

The priest now purifies the chalice by pouring into it wine, and then a little water, while reciting prayers in thanksgiving. 

Part Three

THE COMMUNION VERSE 

The Communion Verse varies with the feast and renews the thought expressed in the Introit, Gradual, and Offertory. This special prayer carries the spirit of the feast, and in Masses for the Dead it intensifies our pleading for the departed souls.

Going to the middle of the altar, the celebrant again reminds the faithful by word and gesture, that both priest and faithful are still united in Holy Mass. The priest says: "The Lord be with you." The acolyte and people answer: "And with thy spirit."

Returning to the missal, the celebrant reads one or more prayers known as the Postcommunions.

THE DISMISSAL 

Afterwards, he salutes the people: "The Lord be with you." They reply: "And with thy spirit." The priest then says, "Go, the Mass is ended." The people respond: "Thanks be to God." 

In Masses for the Dead, instead of saying, "Go, the

Mass is ended," the priest turns towards the tabernacle and prays, "May they rest in peace. Amen."

Bowing down before the crucifix, the priest begs the Blessed Trinity to accept the sacrifice which he has offered, and graciously to extend the mercy of God on those for whom he has offered it.

THE BENEDICTION 

(omitted in Masses for the Dead)

The celebrant then kisses the reliquary of the altar, and turning towards the faithful, blesses them with these words: "May Almighty God bless you: the Father, the 

Son, and the Holy Ghost. Amen.”

THE LAST GOSPEL 

Concluding the adorable Sacrifice, the celebrant recites the beginning of the Gospel in which St. John outlines the divinity of Jesus Christ, the mercy and beauty of Divine Providence in the Incarnation and the Redemption. The people stand for both the first and last Gospel out of respect for the word of God. They make the Sign of the Cross on their forehead, lips and heart to indicate that they accept divine revelation with their minds; that they will profess the faith with their lips; and love it with their hearts.

Chapter XXIII THE SACRAMENTS IN GENERAL 

HE word "sacrament," a sacred action, signifies the

THE 

means at our disposal through which we attain sanctity and thereby please God by becoming holy. Our divine Lord used ceremonies in the countless miracles He performed. There were no printed books of instruction. 

By employing signs, symbols and parables, Jesus taught the fundamental doctrines of our holy religion. He made it clear that the sacraments are channels of divine grace. 

During the first centuries of Christianity, instead of "sacrament," the word "mystery" was used, because of the hidden significance underlying the outward ceremony. "A sacrament is an outward sign instituted by Christ to give grace." Note the three essentials: (1) an outward sign; (2) institution by Christ; (3) the conferring of divine grace.

AN OUTWARD SIGN 

In a wide sense a sign points out something or makes it known. There are three kinds of signs: a. An indicative sign is one that directs our attention to an object — e. g., the red and white pole or board placed outside a barber shop. It signifies the business conducted there. 237

b. Resultant signs which point to a causee. g., smoke results from fire or combustion. c. Effective signs which produce what they indicate-e. g., black clouds forecast rain.

A sacrament is an outward sign of inward grace. The outward sign indicates the spiritual effect of the sacrament.

Examples Pouring water on the head of the person to be baptized is an outward sign of inward grace; i. e., it signifies the cleansing of the soul from original sin when the accompanying words complete the sacrament. The minister of baptism, while pouring the water, declares: "(John), I baptize thee in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." The outward sign of pouring water, and the form- "I baptize thee," etc.— constitute the sacrament of Baptism. Each of the seven sacraments produces grace through the merits of 

Jesus "ex opere operato." This term, "ex opere operato," refers to the intrinsic, inherent efficacy of the sacrament. Its efficacy does not depend on the dispositions of the person baptizing or on the piety of the recipient, because in reality Jesus Christ is the principal minister. 

He alone sanctifies; He is the "causa efficiens" of the sacraments. He uses His priests, lovingly incorporated to 

Himself, as instruments - "causa instrumentalis." 

The seven sacraments channel the Precious Blood from the pierced Sacred Heart of Jesus on the Cross to the souls of those who receive them. The Author of the

THE SACRAMENTS IN GENERAL 

239 sacraments and the invisible Head of the Church is Christ, whose redemption on the Cross merited the sacramental graces now diffused from heaven. Every sacrament worthily received glorifies God and redounds to the well-being of the Church.

A sacrament is composed of matter and form; e. g., in baptism the remote material or matter is water; the pouring of the water is the proximate matter. The words "I baptize thee in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost" are the form of the sacrament. 

Once these two essentials take place, the cleansing with water and the reciting of the words, the effect signified results—that is, the cleansing of the soul. Briefly, a sacrament, validly conferred, produces grace. The expression "outward" means something that can be seen or heard.

In the sacrament of the Holy Eucharist, the outward sign or material required is the bread and wine. The form consists of the words of consecration during the Holy Mass. When our Saviour instituted the sacraments, He intended to confer sanctifying grace in general and also a special sacramental grace, that we might attain the end for which He instituted each sacrament. Any attempt to modify or substantially change the matter and form as defined by the Church invalidates the sacrament. 

An accurate survey of non-Catholic baptisms reveals defects of form and material or both such as, "I christen thee James"; or omitting water: "You are hereby initiated into the ordinance of baptism." Cases of invalid or doubtful baptism should be submitted to the Church. 

---

INSTITUTION BY CHRIST 

God alone can attach to human acts divine efficacy or make them sources of divine grace. The Catholic Church does not claim the power to institute a sacrament or to terminate one. To remove all doubt the Ecumenical

Council of Trent defined this truth. As far back as the third century, Pope St. Stephen I taught that the sacraments conferred by heretics are valid if no hindrance and no defect of form interferes. It is essential that the minister conferring the sacrament have the intention of carrying out the will of Christ. Otherwise he would not confer the sacrament. Civil judges are not ministers of the sacraments.

THE CONFERRING OF DIVINE GRACE 

Every sacrament, validly administered, produces grace through the merits of Jesus Christ. If the ceremonies lack any of the essentials, the sacrament is not conferred. 

Sacraments, in general effective "ex opere operato," are not to be confused with the pious acts called sacramentals. The efficacy of these prayers and other good works depends on the intention and fervor of those who perform them -i. e., "ex opere operantis." The Sign of the Cross, the recitation of the rosary, novenas, retreats, and other devotions, are classed as sacramentals. 

The Church has clearly defined seven sacraments which may be compared to the seven principal stages in life: (1) Baptism is a spiritual birth- "Unless a man be born again of water and the

THE SACRAMENTS IN GENERAL 

241 Holy Ghost, he cannot enter into the kingdom of God." (St. John 3:5) (2) Confirmation strengthens and enlightens the pilgrim to fight valiantly as a soldier of Christ, to persevere unto death. 

(3) Penance: The priest in the confessional is the physician of the soul. If we become spiritually ill, lag or fall along the road, we have need of a remedy and of the means of reviving. The worthy reception of this sacrament revives us spiritually.

Jesus provided the sacraments of Baptism and Penance, or confession, to remit sin. A sincere wish to receive baptism of water is called baptism of desire. This suffices if the regular reception of baptism of water is impossible. When circumstances prevent confession of sins, a perfect act of contrition may be recited. Perfect contrition implies sincere sorrow for sin, because sin offends our Chief Benefactor and Sovereign Good, who loves us. (4) The Holy Eucharist nourishes us with food required for the soul, enlightens us, and enriches us with the presence of 

Jesus Christ in our souls. (5) Matrimony sanctifies the marriage bond and provides special help-sacramental graces for performing the sacred duties of wedded life. 

---

(6) Holy Orders is a sacrament which perpetuates the priesthood in the Church. 

Ordained priests have the twofold power and sacramental grace to offer sacrifice and to sanctify the faithful. (7) Extreme Unction confers graces to ward off temptations, to be resigned, and to die a holy death. It restores health when 

God sees fit.

THE SACRAMENTS OF THE LIVING AND THE SACRAMENTS OF THe Dead 

Two of the seven are called sacraments of the deadnamely, Baptism and Penance, because they revive the soul that is spiritually dead by reason of mortal sin. The other five the sacraments of the living -are received in the state of grace- that is, freedom from mortal sin.

The sacraments of Baptism, Confirmation, and Holy 

Orders can be received but once. They imprint a character on the soul. This character is not a grace, but a spiritual mark of distinction. Baptism makes us children of 

God and confers on us a title to heaven. Confirmation makes us soldiers of Christ; Holy Orders confers the priesthood of Jesus Christ and, in the case of the consecration of a bishop, the plenitude of the priesthood. 

The character of these three sacraments remains forever, to the glory of those who die in the Lord; to the confusion and shame of the unrepentant. 

All the sacraments confer sanctifying grace or increase it. Moreover, each sacrament conveys a special sacramental grace, the particular help that is needed.

THE SACRAMENTS IN GENERAL 

243 For example, the purpose of Extreme Unction - to prepare one for death- differs from that of Matrimony; and so with all the other sacraments, each one of which carries out its own definite mission. Matrimony gives grace to the wedded couple to do God's will. Holy 

Orders confers special graces to enable the priest to ward off temptations and to perform his sacred duties. 

The unworthy reception of a sacrament in the state of mortal sin is a sacrilege, which deprives the soul of God's gift. A good confession revives the graces corresponding to the particular sacrament. While the recipients must receive the sacrament willingly, infants and those mentally deficient from birth can validly and licitly be baptized and confirmed. Also, those who have lost the use of reason may receive Baptism and Extreme Unction if previously they expressed or implied a desire for these means of salvation. In practice, the benefit of the doubt is given to a person in danger of death. The goodness and mercy of God devised these sacraments to help mankind. There is, of course, a limit to our powers, but there is no limit to the mercy of God.

Chapter XXIV BAPTISM 

HE word "baptism" stems from a Greek word which 

THE 

means "to wash." This sacrament is the most necessary because no other sacrament can be received without it. Baptism is the door to all the other sacraments. 

Baptism is a sacrament instituted by Jesus Christ to cleanse the soul from original sin, make us Christians, children of God, and to give us a title to heaven. It fulfills the three requisites for a sacrament: viz., (1) the outward sign of pouring the water onto the person to be baptized and at the same time completing the form by reciting the words; (2) institution by Christ, the Son of 

God, who proclaimed: "Going therefore, teach ye all nations: baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost" (St. Matthew 28:19); and (3) the infusion of sanctifying grace, which cleanses the soul from sin.

From the direct teaching of our divine Lord, no one can doubt that water is to be used as the matter of the sacrament: "Unless a man be born again of water and the Holy Ghost, he cannot enter into the kingdom of 

God" (St. John 3:5). Definitely, this makes baptism a necessity of means ("necessitas medii") for salvation.

Could the wisdom of God have devised other means for admitting members to His Mystical Body? Certainly He could have, but we must follow what He actually did. 244

BAPTISM 

245 The baptism of penance used by St. John the Baptist was not a sacrament but rather a sacramental which foreshadowed the sacrament of Baptism. Jesus Christ instituted the sacrament of Baptism when He was baptized in the river Jordan. Before ascending to heaven, Jesus directed the Apostles and their successors: "Going therefore, teach ye all nations: baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." (St. Matthew 28:19)

To make sure of the validity of this sacrament, the water must flow or be poured over the person to be baptized and the words must be spoken at the same time to complete the significance of washing. Baptismal water used by a priest in the solemn ceremony of baptism is natural water mixed with holy oil and chrism and blessed with special prayers. In case of emergency, however, ordinary water from a faucet may be employed. 

IMMERSION 

During the first fourteen centuries of Christianity, baptism was usually administered by immersion or sprinkling. It would seem evident that so long as water actually reaches the person to be baptized, whether by immersion or by sprinkling or pouring, the outward sign of the sacrament is carried out. 

WHO MAY BAPTIZE 

The priest is the ordinary minister of baptism. 

Anyone having the use of reason may, however, in

an emergency validly baptize, assuming two requirements: (a) that he or she pours the water while saying the words; (b) that he intends to do what Christ and

His Church intended by baptism. This general intention, although confused and not definitely expressed, would suffice. The person who pours the water in baptism must say the words at the same time and without any modification of them—namely: "I baptize you in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." 

By way of example: a great catastrophe occurred from which hundreds of injured people were carried into the streets - several dying persons begged for baptism. 

One man, realizing that the end was near, asked a young man nearby to come and baptize him. The youth replied: "I have no religion of any kind and do not know how to baptize." The victim countered: "Quick, get a handful of water. I'll tell you what to do." Having brought the water, the dying man directed the other to pour the water on his head and while pouring it, to say: “John,

I baptize you in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost. You want to do for me whatever God meant by baptism." "I surely do," said the youth. He poured the water, saying the words of the form. Even though he was a pagan, this lad baptized into the Catholic Church a man immediately afterwards summoned to heaven. It would seem that God had, through the inspiration of the Holy Ghost, disposed the dying man for baptism, employing an unbeliever as the human instrument. 

By virtue of the reception of baptism, we become members of Christ's body-branches of the vine that

BAPTISM 

247 is Christ: "I am the vine: you the branches" (St. John 

15:5). You know what happens to a branch when cut off from the vine. On the other hand, branches are vitalized by the vine and share in the fruit of the vine. It is consoling to know that, like the branches, we merit the fruits of the vine. Thus Christ Jesus stimulates our hope that we may one day enjoy the vision of God.

As members of Christ's Mystical Body, branches of the vine, we are enfolded in the redemptive Passion and death of Jesus — just as if we had suffered with Him.

This privilege is, indeed, our best security, assuming that we keep the commandments and frequently receive Holy 

Communion, the pledge of everlasting life.

EFFECTS OF THIS SACRAMENT 

The fruits or effects of baptism may be listed as follows: 

(1) Remission of sin, original and actual.

The Apostles, taught directly by Christ, baptized people for the remission of their sins. "Do penance: and be baptized every one of you in the name of 

Jesus Christ, for the remission of your sins. And you shall receive the gift of the Holy Ghost." 

(Acts of the Apostles 2:38) 

(2) Canceling of the temporal punishment due to sin.

(3) An indelible character imprinted on the soul. (4) Adoption as children of God; hence, the baptized become heirs to the kingdom of heaven, members of Christ's Mystical

Body, true members of the Catholic 

Church. Observe that "Mystical Body" signifies spiritual body.

BAPTISM OF DESIRE 

Baptism of desire, sometimes referred to as baptism of the spirit or baptism of fire, implies fervent love of God, regret for sins committed and a desire, implicit or explicit, to receive the sacrament in its ordinary form and to co-operate in every way to do the will of God for salvation. One with such a love desires to fulfill the divine will because of the infinite goodness and mercy of God. A concrete example can clarify this point. A man who had privately read books on Catholic doctrine and who was convinced of its truth fell seriously ill. He requested a non-Catholic member of his family to summon a priest because he wished to be baptized. This request was refused. The man died in peace, sanctified by baptism of desire: "... And he that loveth Me shall be loved of My Father" (St. John 14:21).

BAPTISM OF BLOOD 

Baptism of blood refers to martyrs (a Greek word meaning "witness") who suffer torment and death — or what would ordinarily cause death for their religion

BAPTISM 

249 or in defense of any Christian virtue. They are baptized in their own blood. "Everyone therefore that shall confess Me before men, I will also confess him before My 

Father who is in heaven" (St. Matthew 10:32). Surely

God provides for such as these. There is nothing repugnant in the possibility of the Almighty sending His angels (messengers) to baptize them invisibly. "He that shall lose his life for Me shall find it" (St. Matthew 

10:39). The Holy Innocents martyred by Herod received baptism of blood. In the event of complete or partial recovery,* baptism of blood should be followed by baptism of water, because baptism of water is the ordinary channel the others being substitutes whereby God accepts the will for the deed. The same principle holds true in the case of baptism of desire. 1 If they have kept the natural law, unbaptized souls go to a place of natural happiness in accord with their nature. They have no claim whatever to heaven, which is a supernatural reward for a supernaturally good life. 

On the other hand, a naturally good life merits a natural reward.

Catholics should renew the agreement they have made with God. They should never lose sight of their baptismal vows to renounce the devil with all his works and pomps. The baptism of infants should not be postponed or neglected. The parents or guardians must ordinarily give permission for the baptism of their infants and also agree to have them educated as Catholics. * Such might be the circumstances in times of bloody persecuone left for dead might later revive. tion - 

---

SPONSORS 

Practical Catholics are requested to serve as earthly advocates for the person baptized. In the case of infants they pledge fidelity on behalf of their godchild. They also obligate themselves by a spiritual relationship to look after the religious instruction of their godchild should the parents die or neglect this duty. Adults may not be baptized against their will. 

RITUAL OF Baptism 

The ceremonies of the sacrament of Baptism are significant: (1) Parents' choosing of a saint's name. (2) Placing of salt on the tongue to symbolize wisdom and preservation. 

(3) Prayers of exorcism. 

(4) Signing with the cross- -possession in the name of Christ. (5) Investing with stole: The priest lays the stole upon the child to indicate that the child from now on belongs in a special manner to God. This is an external sign of that inner and heavenly covenant which baptism establishes between the divine Creator and the creature now made a child of God. (6) Sponsors' recitation of the Apostles' 

Creed and the Our Father. (7) Symbolic opening of the ears and nostrils.

BAPTISM 

251 (8) Renunciation of the devil. (9) Anointing of chest and back with holy oil-a sign of strength to carry yoke of Christ. 

(10) Profession of faith. 

(11) Pouring of the water and reciting of the essential words: "I baptize thee in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost." (12) Anointing of head to show relationship with Christ the King. (13) Presentation of a white garment or linen cloth, symbolizing purity of soul. (14) Giving of a lighted candlesymbol of grace, good works, and union with 

Christ, the Light of the World.

Chapter XXV PENANCE THE HE Holy Spirit inspired the Royal Psalmist with an appropriate introduction to the sacrament of

Penance. 

"He hath not dealt with us according to our sins: nor rewarded us according to our iniquities.... As a father hath compassion on his children, so hath the Lord compassion on them that fear Him: for He knoweth our frame. He remembereth that we are dust...." 

(Psalm 102:10, 13) 

Penance is a sacrament instituted by Christ to forgive all sins committed after baptism through the absolution of the priest. It is the ordinary means established by our divine Saviour to secure the salvation of those who have fallen into mortal sin. The word "all" stresses the power of the priest, acting in the place of God, to forgive every mortal sin without exception. There could be a barrier on the part of the penitent, however, if he resists divine grace and perversely continues impenitent. This explains the oft-repeated question regarding the following text: ee ... the blasphemy of the Spirit shall not be forgiven." (St. Matthew 12:31) 252

PENANCE 

253 A sin against the Holy Ghost, in effect, means a persistent refusal to accept the grace of God and to confess one's sins. One who obstinately refuses to accept God's pardon cannot have his sins forgiven. The proud man, unyielding, afflicted with intellectual pride- referred to as a superiority complex - presents the real problem. 

DISPENSERS OF GOD'S MERCY 

The priest receives the power to forgive sins through the sacrament of Holy Orders. In the exercise of this power the priest is truly a judge. To render an intelligent decision, a judge must first hear the case; hence the necessity of confession of sins before a divinely commissioned judge who has faculties for a designated jurisdiction. 

The word "penance" means more than satisfaction for sin. As a sacrament instituted by Christ, it includes all the required acts by which sins committed after baptism are forgiven. Regarded as a virtue, the word "penance" signifies penitence, a sincere regret for all mortal sins committed, which implies the firm resolution to avoid sin and its occasion for the future else the sorrow could not be genuine. - - St. John records the institution of the sacrament of 

Penance. On the evening of the day after His Resurrection, Jesus Christ breathed upon His Apostles and said to them: "As the Father hath sent Me, I also send you.... Receive ye the Holy Ghost. Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven

them: and whose sins you shall retain, they are retained." (St. John 20:21-23)

In this definite charge our divine Saviour commissioned the Apostles and their successors to forgive sins. 

He gave them real power to reconcile man to God. The sacrament of Penance is not a prayer or a hope that sins may be forgiven but an actual pardon of sins. There are no halfway measures with God. Jesus assured the paralytic: "Thy sins are forgiven thee."

He thus made it clear that (St. Matthew 9:2) "the Son of man hath power on earth to forgive sins." (St. Luke 5:24)

While our divine Lord also empowered the Apostles to retain sins, in practice absolution is rarely denied. The prerogative to forgive sins assumes that the penitent is rightly disposed. If not, the prudent confessor guides the penitent to the right disposition.

MATTER AND FORM 

-the acts The proximate matter of this sacrament of the penitent-comprises confession of the number and kind of sins, satisfaction, and contrition, which includes the firm resolution to avoid the persons, places, or

PENANCE 

255 things that lead to sin, as well as all other occasions of sin.

Sins in themselves are the remote matter of this sacrament. The words of the confessor: "I absolve thee from thy sins" constitute the essential form of this sac rament (Council of Trent, Session XIV, Chapter III, canon 4). 

Before giving absolution, the confessor counsels the penitent how to avoid future relapses and imposes a penance a few prayers to be said in satisfaction for the temporal punishment due to sin. Our Saviour intended the power of forgiving sins to last as long as human beings had need of pardon for their transgressions. 

The conferring of this power is restated in the commission given by Christ to His Apostles: "As the Father hath sent Me, I also send you." (St. John 20:21) "Behold, I am with you all days...." (St. Matthew 28:20)

CONFERRING OF POWER 

In the sacrament of Holy Orders, when deacons are elevated to the sacred priesthood, the power to forgive sins is conferred. Wherefore priests are "the salt of the earth,... the light of the world" (St. Matthew 5:13, 14); and "the dispensers of the mysteries of God" (1 Corinthians 4:1). To validly exercise the power to forgive sins, jurisdiction is required. For the exercise of

the priesthood within a diocese, a permission called "faculties" is granted by the bishop. 

Every sin can be forgiven, given co-operation on the part of the penitent. "If your sins be as scarlet, they shall be made as white as snow: and if they be red as crimson, they shall be white as wool." (Isaias 1:18) "Whose sins you shall forgive, they are forgiven them.' (St. John 20:23)

Confession is the ordinary means of salvation for those who have committed grave sins after baptism. The absolution of the priest, who is divinely commissioned as judge, is ratified in heaven: ... and whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven." (St. Matthew 16:19)

If one has the misfortune to commit mortal sin and is forgiven directly by God through an act of perfect contrition, one must submit the sin in confession as soon as possible; just as a person who has been reconciled to God by baptism of desire must later receive baptism of water, if possible.

This sacrament, worthily received, remits the sins and the eternal punishment due to sin. Sins are remitted by a bestowal of sanctifying grace, which results in peace of

PENANCE 

257 mind and a strengthening of will power to avoid further relapses. Self-accusation is necessary on the part of the penitent; otherwise, it would be impossible to judge the worthiness for absolution or to give a penance proportionate to the offense. Sacramental confession is most beneficial to man who, by nature, craves to confide in someone.

SINCERE 

"Be not deceived: God is not mocked." (Galatians 6:7)

Contrition, confession and satisfaction must have certain qualities. Real contrition implies more than a deep regret. It connotes detestation of sin and the firm purpose to sin no more. This in turn implies the resolution to give up persons, places and things that lead to sine. g., indecent motion pictures, suggestive theatrical performances, improper television and radio programs, all obscene books, magazines and other literature, risqué jokes and association with people who conduct themselves improperly.

Let no one dare to trifle with God in withholding a firm purpose of amendment. This resolution must be supported by earnest prayer. All of us depend on the grace of God.
