The grace of the sacraments strengthens the resolution to exclude further offenses against our Sovereign Good. In a word, absolute sincerity should characterize a good confession.

Stimulated by actual grace, the well-disposed penitent puts forth his best efforts to restrain the motions of the

flesh so as promptly to banish temptations. He despises and shuns the evil which separates him from God, his 

Chief Benefactor. If one plays with fire, he will be burned. No intelligent creature can indulge in temptation and expect to remain sinless. To form a habit of regular confession once a week or once a month is indicated, and even more frequently if one relapses into grave sins. 

Recourse to the sacraments conquers sin with its mental anguish that often tortures the sinner into a state of melancholy fears and neuroses. After treating a homosexual for six weeks, an eminent non-Catholic psychiatrist came up with his final remedy: "Avoid the occasions of sin." Mortal sin very often stems from pride and vincible ignorance, with consequent revolt against God. By contrast, humility and obedience to God's laws go hand in hand, because the humble man realizes the need for direction and the corresponding grace to accept guidance. 

UNIVERSAL

There can be no question of compromise with any phase of sin, or of reserving little key weaknesses that open the door to evil. Therefore, contrition must be universal that is, it must extend to all sins, mortal and venial. Contrition demands a supernatural motive — such as the fear of losing sanctifying grace or recognition of one's ingratitude to his heavenly Father. Natural motives, such as fear of loss of health, reputation, or incarceration, do not constitute contrition. Neither an emotional disturbance nor the shedding of tears rates as true compunction.

PENANCE 

259 Contrition may be perfect or imperfect, depending on the motive. To abhor sin, for example, as the greatest of evils because it cuts us off from our divine Loverwho loved us first and who has watched over us with constant paternal solicitude — is perfect sorrow for sins. 

Fear of the punishment of hell is imperfect contrition. 

Imperfect contrition suffices for a valid confession. Listen to the pertinent warnings of the Holy Spirit: 

"Rend your hearts and not your garments.' (Joel 2:13) "Do penance... for the remission of your sins." 

(Acts of the Apostles 2:38) ENTIRE 

The confession must be entire; it must cover all mortal sins according to number and species. This calls for a diligent and honest examination of conscience. The confessor, to pass sound judgment, should know clearly the nature of the sin. The nature of the sin determines its species. The number of times it has been committed should be mentioned as accurately as possible.

THE SEAL OF CONFESSION 

The confessor is bound by the strictest secrecy to observe rigidly the seal of confession, directly or indirectly, or by inference. Not even to save his own life nor the life of another may he disclose a sin confessed to him.

Satisfaction-known as the penance enjoined by the confessor-consists of penitential works: generally prayers offered in payment of the temporal punishment due to sin and as a reminder that we must sincerely repent, to the point of avoiding the occasions of sin.

Often the dread of punishment deters people from relapsing into sin. Even though the penance given must be accepted, failure to perform it does not invalidate a confession.

Our Lord reminds us that we must atone for our sins: "Amen, I say to thee, thou shalt not go out from thence till thou repay the last farthing.' (St. Matthew 5:26) "Unless you shall do penance, you shall all likewise perish." (St. Luke 13:3) 

Venial sins may be forgiven by prayer and good works because venial sins do not void sanctifying grace.

It is understood that when sins are forgiven, the eternal punishment due them is always remitted. But the temporal punishment due to sins remains. Holy Scripture shows that Moses, Aaron, and King David, even though their grave sins had been forgiven, were still punished. "And David said to Nathan: 'I have sinned against the Lord.' And Nathan said to 

David: "The Lord also hath taken away thy sin. Thou shalt not die.'" (2 Kings 12:13)

PENANCE 

261 The temporal punishment due to sin may be remitted either through the penance imposed by the confessor, through indulgences, or by sufferings patiently borne and offered in union with the suffering Christ. "Having gathered together the multitude before the rock, he said to them: 'Hear, ye rebellious and incredulous: Can we bring you forth water out of this rock?" " (Numbers 20:10)

Chapter XXVI INDULGENCES 

NDULGENCES may be considered as something akin

IND to a parole in our civil courts. Indulgences do not remit mortal sin. Only the sacraments of Baptism and 

Penance and, in certain cases, baptism of desire and baptism of blood or an act of perfect contrition, can restore sanctifying grace, the life of the soul.

An indulgence is not a license to commit sin. No one who has lost sanctifying grace by mortal sin can gain an indulgence. Ignorance of the definite Catholic doctrine on this subject led Martin Luther to start the Protestant Revolt, the so-called Reformation, in 1517. Many people today are in error regarding the related facts.

As the term indicates, a temporal punishment lasts for a while, either in this life or in purgatory. An indulgence is the remission in whole or in part of the temporal punishment due to sin. The words "in whole or in part" are used because a plenary indulgence remits all the punishment due to sin whereas a partial indulgence takes away only a portion of the punishment due to sin. 

In a word, "plenary" means complete, whereas "partial” means a fraction of the whole. 

To understand the background of indulgences, note that every good work performed with the right intention has a double value; one of merit and of expiation. Hence the sufferings of our Saviour had a meritorious fruit as 262

INDULGENCES 

263 well as being expiatory for us. The Blessed Virgin Mary, having been without sin, did not have to make any kind of satisfaction. The same may be said of the saints, some of whom atoned for their mistakes in this life. But their works of merit and expiation far exceeded their personal needs. The superabundant merits of Christ, of the Blessed Virgin Mary and the saints and martyrs, supply the treasury of the Church. The Pope may draw on this treasury.

Does the Pope have the power to allocate indulgences from this storehouse of good works? He has: "Whatsoever thou shalt loose on earth, it shall be loosed also in heaven" (St. Matthew 16:19). The commutation, then, is ratified in heaven.

A partial indulgence remits before God such portions of the temporal punishment due to sin as would be satisfied by a public penance (e. g., three hundred days) in the first years of Christianity. In those days of fervor, penitents, covered with sackcloth and ashes, stood at the door of the church begging prayers from the passers-by. 

Such penances varied according to the gravity of the offense, but the early Christians zealously performed them to atone for their sins and win everlasting life.

Indulgences gained may be retained by the person who performs the good works required, or they may be applied to the souls in purgatory. They cannot be applied to the living because it is the mind of the Church that living people should gain their own indulgences. 

No one can measure the great spiritual advantages accruing from the good works done for indulgences. These promote devotion and lay up treasures in heaven.

Chapter XXVII CONFIRMATION 

HE name of this sacrament derives from its effect:

TH "confirmare" to make strong; to protect one's faith and morals and to enfold them with stability. The sacrament of Confirmation confers on those baptized sufficient sacramental grace to courageously profess their faith and to persevere in it till death. "And I will ask the Father: and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you forever: the Spirit of Truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it seeth 

Him not, nor knoweth Him. But you shall know Him; because He shall abide with you and shall be in you.” (St. John 14:16-17) 

Ten days after His Ascension to heaven, our divine

Lord sent the Holy Ghost. His coming was marked by miraculous manifestations, such as parted tongues of fire which descended on the heads of the one hundred and twenty disciples and the Blessed Virgin Mary, who, gathered in the Upper Room, received the sacrament of Confirmation and the seven gifts of the Holy Ghost. No longer afraid, the Apostles went forth valiantly to announce the glad tidings of salvation.

Unlettered fishermen, endowed with the gift of tongues after receiving the Holy Spirit, could speak and 264

CONFIRMATION 

265 be understood in many languages. The bestowal of these charismata, supernatural gifts, continued through the first centuries of Christianity.

SPIRITUAL STRENGTH While the Third Person of the Blessed Trinity enters your soul in baptism, He comes in a special way through the sacrament of Confirmation. This is a sacrament through which those who have been baptized receive the Holy Ghost, to confirm them in the faith as Christians and to induct them in the army of Christ. Just as a soldier must be trained and instructed before taking his place in the military ranks, the Christian should be adequately briefed before the reception of this sacrament in order to be able to give a reason for and to defend his faith. In the words of St. Peter, a Christian should be able to give "a reason of that hope which is in [him]." (1 St. Peter 3:15)

Our Lord instituted the sacrament of Confirmation on the first Pentecost Sunday. The Acts of the Apostles record the conferring of the sacrament subsequent to 

Pentecost: 

"Then they laid their hands upon them: and they received the Holy Ghost." 

(Acts of the Apostles 8:17) 

"And Ananias went his way and entered into the house. And laying his hands upon him,

he said: 'Brother Saul, the Lord Jesus hath sent me, He that appeared to thee in the way as thou camest, that thou mayest receive thy sight and be filled with the Holy Ghost.'" 

(Acts of the Apostles 9:17) 

The above texts prove clearly that St. Peter, and St. 

John confirmed a number of Samaritans. St. Paul employed this same sacrament to strengthen the faith of his converts.

THE RITE OF CONFIRMATION 

The bishop's extending his hands over the confirmands and anointing their foreheads with chrism accompanied by the words, "I sign thee with the Sign of the 

Cross and I confirm thee with the chrism of salvation, in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost," constitute the matter and form of 

Confirmation. 

The words "I sign thee with the Sign of the Cross" indicate enrollment of the confirmed person in the army of Christ. The words "I confirm thee with the chrism of salvation" refer to the strength imparted with the sacramental grace. The sacrament of Confirmation imprints an indelible character on the soul. 

You will observe, in reading the Bible, that oil symbolizes abundance and gladness; unction carries the thought of strength and activity. In ancient times combatants anointed themselves with oil before entering the arena. Balm (a product of the balsam tree) mixed with

CONFIRMATION 

267 olive oil indicates preservation of the soul from sin and the fragrance of virtue. The effects of this sacrament are an increase of sanctifying grace and special sacramental graces. These strengthen us in the faith, that we may fearlessly confess it and openly defend it even with our life, as the martyrs have done in every age of the Church. Thus being confirmed, we receive actual graces, in abundant measure to fight against temptation with all its insidious snares.

The imposition of hands by the bishop signifies the coming of the Holy Ghost as well as divine protection. 

The anointing with holy chrism—of its nature unctuous, fluid and fragrant — signifies the copious graces which flow from Jesus Christ. 

"Of His fullness we all have received." (St. John 1:16)

The use of signs and symbols, employed in everyday life, goes back to the beginning of time. Up to the fifteenth century, because of the lack of printing, books were available in only a few places, such as the monasteries. The ancient parchments, written by hand, were beyond the reach of the majority. Doctrines had to be taught by word of mouth and by signs and symbols. Wherefore 

St. Paul wrote: "Faith then cometh by hearing; and hearing by the word of Christ." (Romans 10:17)

Moreover, normal human beings do not jump into the spiritual; they must be led, step by step-e. g., the Sign of the Cross! (Read explanation on page 347.)

Our Saviour instituted the seven sacraments, and invested the worthy reception of the sacraments with sanctifying grace and sacramental graces. He used signs that were familiar to the masses of the people. Apropos of the matter of the sacrament of Confirmation, anointing brought about strength and healing. Anointing with oil signifies strength-the giving of power. Kings and rulers were anointed at their coronation ceremonies to beg of the Almighty the bestowal of power and strength for their reign. Bread always meant nourishment. Many other signs came into use by people of every race and clime.

DECREE OF 1947 

The bishop confers the sacrament of Confirmation. 

In special circumstances, priests may administer it. In virtue of a decree that became effective January 1, 1947, pastors and administrators of parishes were granted the faculty of confirming, as extraordinary ministers, those among their faithful and others in their territory who are in danger of death from accident, sickness or old age.

Priests, especially those in missionary lands, are sometimes delegated by the Holy Father to administer Confirmation. Most priests of the Oriental Church have this privilege. (Cf. Baltimore Catechism No. 3, Question and Answer 331 a and b.) 

Jesus founded the sacrament of Confirmation, which imparts special graces to fortify and preserve the faith.

CONFIRMATION 

269 Hence we have the obligation to receive it. This sacrament of the living imprints a lasting character on the soul and can be received by a person only once. All those baptized may be validly confirmed. But to receive this sacrament worthily one must be in the state of grace.

THE OUTWARD SIGN 

Holy chrism is a mixture of olive oil and balm consecrated by the bishop in the solemn ceremonies of Holy Thursday. The outward sign of anointing the forehead symbolizes what takes place in the soul-i. e., the strength-giving power of the Holy Spirit. At the time

Jesus instituted the sacraments, anointing was generally understood to stimulate, heal and build up resistance. 

The fact that the anointing is given in the form of a cross reminds the confirmed as followers of our crucified 

Saviour to profess his faith openly and fearlessly. The bishop gives a slight tap on the cheek to remind the confirmed person to be ready to suffer anything, even death, for the faith bestowed by Jesus Christ. 

At the conclusion of the ceremony of Confirmation, the bishop, making the Sign of the Cross over all assembled, prays: "May the Lord bless you from Sion, that you may see the prosperity of Jerusalem all the days of your life, and that you may have eternal life.”

Chapter XXVIII  THE HE effects of the sacrament of Confirmation include the seven gifts of the Holy Ghost-from which flow the twelve fruits and the eight beatitudes. 

Each of these divine gifts has its definite objective. 

The gifts of the Holy Ghost enlighten the mind and develop the theological virtues of faith, hope and charity received in baptism. Faith, hope and charity, intensified by the seven gifts of the Holy Ghost, produce good deeds known as the twelve fruits and the eight beatitudes.

The fruits outline, in part, the active life of our Saviour; the beatitudes touch on His inner life.

Consider the scope and purpose of the seven gifts. (1) Wisdom teaches us to evaluate things in their relation to our final end. It points out a way of life by which we can reach our goal. Wisdom endows us with the foresight to look to our last end by co-operating now with divine grace for the greater glory of God and in order to secure our eternal salvation. (2) Understanding deepens our grasp of eternal truths. This grace, acquired by meditation, differs from knowledge. It enlightens the Christian regarding the truths of religion. (3) Counsel prudently chooses the best way of pleasing God, enables us to discern God's will, and to detect the deceits of the devil. 270

(4) Fortitude alerts us to stand up fearlessly in opposition to evil and to resist the first approach of temptation by avoiding the occasion of sin. (5) Knowledge examines self regarding fidelity to

God's laws and to the duties of one's state in life. (6) Piety, the love of a child for his parents, inspires us to cherish our lovingly solicitous heavenly Father and to prefer Him to creatures. (7) Fear of the Lord is not servile fear of loss or punishment but the dread of hurting one whom we love.

God is more precious than all other beings. This gift enlightens us to shun the folly and ingratitude of turning away from God, our Chief Good and greatest Benefactor. 

Truly, then "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom." (Proverbs 1:7) 

THE FRUITS OF THE HOLY SPIRIT 

The twelve fruits stem from the seven gifts. 

St. Augustine de- 

The one doctrine (1) Charity: Both heroism and evil emerge from the human heart, the center of our life. Most of our moral problems can be traced to the heart. fines virtue as "the order of love." constantly taught by Christ is love. Charity — in reality, divine love fosters spiritual progress. In heaven, love reaches its climax of perfection in the possession of God.

In hell no one loves. St. Teresa of Avila refers to Satan as "the one who does not love."

"The charity of God is poured forth in our hearts, by the Holy Ghost who is given. to us." (Romans 5:5)

Thus, we receive the grace to love God above all things and our neighbor as ourselves. Ponder the divine promise made by Jesus: "If anyone love Me, he will keep My word. 

And My Father will love him: and We will come to him and will make Our abode with him." (St. John 14:23)

THE THRILL OF LOVE 

(2) Joy: This fruit is related to charity because it rejoices in the discovery of true love. "I found Him whom my soul loveth. I held 

Him: and I will not let Him go... 

(Canticle of Canticles 3:4) 

The soul that rises above earthly attractions and finds the love of Jesus Christ experiences a profound joy and a peace surpassing understanding. In the gamut of human affections, no human joy can equal the thrill of bringing a soul closer to God. This explains why zealous missionaries, deprived of earthly comforts, in which many of them were reared; confronted with suspicion, hatred, and persecution, are intensely happy in bringing the glad tidings of the Gospel to the millions of pagans who live in spiritual darkness and degradation.

Worldlings often wonder how priests and religious can be content living their dedicated lives self-bound by the three arms of the Cross: poverty, chastity and obedience. Unbelievers cannot fathom the reasons. Never having been briefed on the subject, they fail to grasp that the basis of the spiritual life is charity. To take up one's cross and follow Christ exemplifies love in action. 

When challenged: "Why do you bury yourselves in 

Africa?" missionaries reply: "Because we believe in the 

Resurrection." Once this fundamental has been thoroughly learned and practiced, there results a deep-felt joy, the delight of loving God and of stimulating others to love Him. Who can measure up to this type of charity? Every person baptized and living in the state of grace. When lost by mortal sin, both divine grace and charity are restored by a good confession. This explains the meaning of the title of the popular work Confession 

Is a Joy by the late Daniel A. Lord, S. J. When the

Christian grows up to spiritual maturity, he finds in meditation, fervent prayer, and the worthy reception of the sacraments the exquisite thrill of possessing God and being possessed by Him. In the ineffable joy of union with our divine Redeemer, our heart, purified and shorn of vanities, finds that His yoke is sweet and His burden light (cf. St. Matthew 11:30). (3) Peace: St. Thomas Aquinas teaches that peace is the perfection of joy. Evidently the Angelic Doctor had in mind the peace of Christ. When our Saviour assured His Apostles after the Last Supper: "These things 

I have spoken to you, that My joy may be in you, and your joy may be filled" (St. John 15:11), He had in mind a

joy that one holds in complete security. Peace comes from tasting this full cup of happiness. You know that peace is the tranquillity of order. St. Ignatius Loyola left in his memoirs a statement that he could think of no one capable of disturbing his peace.

NEITHER DEATH NOR LIFE 

The martyrs and many other holy souls, even when suffering persecution and torture, preserved a refreshing serenity. Again, we invoke St. Paul to confirm this truth. "I am sure that neither death, nor life, nor angels, nor principalities, nor powers, nor things present, nor things to come, nor might, nor height, nor depth, nor any other creature, shall be able to separate us from the love of 

God which is in Christ Jesus our Lord." (Romans 8:38-39) (4 and 7) Patience and Long-Suffering: Our divine 

Redeemer trod the winepress of pain and anguish of heart. He sanctified sorrow and taught mankind how to bear it. Besides the mystery of evil, there is the mystery of suffering, inevitable in our lives. In His wisdom, God did not plan to free rational creatures from suffering of body and anguish of mind. St. Augustine, one of the great doctors of the Church, thought that God prefers to draw good out of evil rather than to prevent evil.

ONLY ONE EVIL 

Strictly interpreting revealed religion, only one evil exists in the world—namely, sin. To be sure, illness, pain, sorrow, humiliation may be trials, but not evils.

On the other hand, they become steppingstones to perfection. Note how pain purifies love and rises above temptation. An analysis of the teaching of St. Augustine: "Virtue is order in love," leads us to the conclusion that perfection is order in pain. 

You are aware that the effort to live a good life means self-control and a struggle to maintain it. In our spiritual life there are times of darkness and dryness. when we could easily become discouraged. Many of the saints endured a bloodless martyrdom - torments of the desire to fully possess God - a goal not attainable here on earth. St. Paul suffered so much from causes within and without that one would think that after his weary body had been beaten, stoned, betrayed, his great spirit might have surrendered. How he longed "... to be dissolved and to be with Christ." (Philippians 1:23) "Unhappy man that I am, who shall deliver me from the body of this death?" he asked.

NO SURCEASE 

(Romans 7:24)

He never relaxed his zeal and solicitude for all the churches. He still longed to preach Christ crucified.

"They are the ministers of Christ (I speak as one less wise): I am more; in many more labors, in prisons more frequently, in stripes above measure, in deaths often. Of the Jews five times did I receive forty stripes save one. 

Thrice was I beaten with rods: once I was stoned: thrice I suffered shipwreck: a night and a day I was in the depth of the sea. In journeying often, in perils of waters, in perils of robbers, in perils from my own nation, in perils from the Gentiles, in perils in the city, in perils in the wilderness, in perils in the sea, in perils from false brethren: In labor and painfulness, in much watchings, in hunger and thirst, in fastings often, in cold and nakedness: Besides those things which are without: my daily instance, the solicitude for all the churches." (2 Corinthians 11:23-28) 

The Apostle of the Gentiles is a striking example of patience under trial. Patience is defined as serenity in pain. Often we become impatient because our progress in the spiritual life is slow. But the wheels of life grind slowly. The seasons of the year, rain, snow, ice, winds, cultivate plant life. While the Holy Spirit develops the powers of the soul, He pours into it consolations that help to form the virtues of patience and long-suffering, the fruit of hope. (5) Benignity: Benignity is the kindly disposition, the ardent desire, to help all mankind. First, considering, How can I serve best? and then doing it, constitutes

benignity in action-the joy of doing good to our neighbor. No wonder the selfish and self-centered are grim, unhappy, depressed and frustrated. 

"LOVE ONE ANOTHER" (6) Goodness: The late Pope Pius XII, referring to the terrifying threat of war, said that wars result from the fact that men forget the divine principles of Jesus Christ. All men need the guidance of the Holy Spirit to dispose their hearts and enlighten their minds to ensure a peace built on justice and charity. On the eve of His 

Passion, Jesus stressed the essentials of fraternal charity to the end that families and nations should exemplify benevolence towards one another: "A new commandment I give unto you: That you love one another, as I have loved you, that you also love one another. By this shall all men know that you are My disciples, if you have love one for another." (St. John 13:34-35) 

Fraternal charity manifests itself in goodness—not in a theoretical love, but in one that is active, operative, efficacious. Good people are cheerful. They serve the 

Lord in gladness. (8) Mildness: Mildness may be defined as the exercise of humility and meekness combined. In His Sermon on the Mount, our divine Lord declared:

"Blessed are the meek: for they shall possess the land" i. e., the land of the living, which is heaven. (St. Matthew 5:4)

Meekness, which is related to poverty, draws hearts to God. Even when others afflict us, meekness gives us self-control and an intelligent approach to problems. (9) Faith: To complete the right order in human relations, the Holy Spirit lavishes on us the gift of faith. 

This word may be understood in two ways. First, faith is the acceptance of truth on the authority of the one who teaches it. It also means the fidelity with which we treat others. Loyalty, sincerity in dealing with others, generosity in sharing our gifts, standing by our word, and remaining firm in our affection, all express this fruit of the Holy Spirit called faith.

Although the realization of these fruits requires effort and sacrifices on our part, we can always count on corresponding graces as they are needed. With these graces comes sweet consolation in doing the will of God. Note the comment of David: "According to the multitude of my sorrows in my heart, Thy comforts have given joy to my soul." (Psalm 93:19) 

Remember the words of Christ: "If any man will follow Me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow Me." (St. Mark 8:34)

Our Lord further assures us that the yoke will be sweet, the burden light.

UNDER CONTROL 

(10, 11, and 12) Modesty, Continency, and Chastity: 

Note the profound teaching of St. Paul that the Christian must have everything under control in his life. God wills that our physical being develop in dignity. Before his fall, Adam had a keen sense of order. But sin threw everything out of balance-e. g., the selfish, fearful miser is not master of his riches; he is a slave to all he owns. He has no order in his life because his wealth, set up as an idol, deprives him of the true God. For the same reason, the glutton has a dim view of life's objective, because the craving for food enslaves him. In the same manner, the sensual person loses the balance of order to wallow in unlawful pleasure. "The sensual man perceiveth not these things that are of the Spirit of God." (1 Corinthians 2:14) 

Jesus came to correct these disorders, to set free the slaves to sin, and to restore the "liberty of the glory of the children of God" (Romans 8:21). "Because the creature also itself shall be delivered from the servitude of corruption, into the liberty of the glory of the children of God." (Romans 8:21)

Original sin has left its weaknesses: "For all that is in the world is the concupiscence of the flesh and the concupiscence of the eyes and the pride of life, which is not of the Father but is of the world." (1 St. John 2:16)

These defects would destroy order in our lives. The 

Holy Spirit provides the remedy if we but respond to His inspirations. We exercise self-control and restore or retain order by the fruits of modesty, reserve in word and action; of continency, self-restraint; and of chastity, which moderates and bridles concupiscence. "Let your modesty be known to all men." (Philippians 4:5)

Modesty directs us in relation to exterior things; chastity and continency preserve order in our lower nature. 

In the fruits of the Spirit we enjoy the sources of real freedom, because they break the chains of slavery and protect our royal heritage. Thus the wisdom and goodness of God have foreseen and lovingly arranged for every need and emergency in our lives.

No wonder that the vessel of election, in his grasp of the wonderful designs of God, exclaims: "For all things are yours, whether it be Paul or Apollo or Cephas, or the world, or life, or death, or things present, or things to come. 

For all are yours. And you are Christ's. And 

Christ is God's." (1 Corinthians 3:22-23)

Our divine Lord summarized many of His doctrines in a few dynamic sentences which are very comforting to ponder in the midst of trials and world confusion: "For whosoever will save his life shall lose it: and whosoever shall lose his life for My sake and the Gospel shall save it. For what shall it profit a man, if he gain the whole world and suffer the loss of his soul? Or what shall a man give in exchange for his soul?" (St. Mark 8:35-37)

Chapter XXIX  

OD'S greatest gift to mankind is the precious gift of Himself. GOD "... My delights were to be with the children of men." (Proverbs 8:31) 

At the Last Supper Jesus gave thanks. The Holy Mass is a most solemn act of thanksgiving, performed to express our heart's loving gratitude to our divine Benefactor. The reception of Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament is called Holy Communion. When we receive this "Bread of Angels" we are one with Jesus Christ and also one with each other. When a dying person receives Holy 

Communion, it is called Viaticum, from the Latin "via te cum"; in other words, Jesus accompanies us on our final journey. Our divine Lord, in bequeathing to us His last will and testament, spoke clearly and decisively. At the Last Supper He instituted the Blessed Sacrament. 

The Apostles understood it so without any doubt. "And whilst they were at supper, Jesus took bread and blessed and broke and gave to His disciples and said: 'Take ye and eat. This is 

My Body.' And taking the chalice, He gave thanks and gave to them, saying: 'Drink ye all of this. For this is My Blood of the new 282

testament, which shall be shed for many unto remission of sins.' (St. Matthew 26:26-28)

St. Paul defines the Real Presence of Jesus in the 

Blessed Sacrament: 

"The chalice of benediction which we bless, is it not the communion of the Blood of 

Christ? And the bread which we break, is it not the partaking of the Body of the Lord?" (1 Corinthians 10:16) "Therefore, whosoever shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the Body and of the Blood of the Lord." (1 Corinthians 11:27) 

This wonderful miracle of love Christ accomplished by His own infinite power, just as He created all things. 

By meditation one comes to realize more and more the intense love of Christ expressed in His desire to be incorporated with us. The plan is divine. It is His way of possessing us and enriching us with His own divine life. In this eucharistic possession of Christ we have the surest pledge of everlasting happiness, because, dwelling in our hearts, Jesus enlightens us and fortifies us to resist the first approach of temptation. Wherefore Pope St. 

Pius X urged the faithful, including children, to pray the Mass, to live the Mass, and frequently to welcome Jesus to their hearts by the worthy reception of Holy Communion.

OUR LADY OF THE BLESSED SACRAMENT 

The divine plan of selecting the Blessed Virgin Mary to be the Mother of God and fittingly adorning her soul comes to light on Calvary. Jesus became the Redeemer of mankind by His sacrifice on the Cross. Mary became our mother in that solemn immolation: "Jesus said to His mother: 'Mother, this is your Son.' He then said to the disciple: "This is your mother. 

St. John 19:26-27, Kleist-Lilly translation)*

In these words addressed to St. John, who represents us, Jesus included all rational beings. To His Mother He said in effect: "These are your children." The Mass perpetuates this privileged adoption. Recall the solemn words at the consecration of the chalice: "the mystery of faith.... Here heaven and earth meet. Fervent faith, firm, unwavering — 

"... the victory which overcometh the world...." (1 St. John 5:4)

MIRACLE OF Miracles 

The many miracles performed by our divine Saviour during His stay on this earth led step by step to the miracle of miracles- the institution of the Blessed Sacrament. You may read in the Sacred Scriptures how Jesus 

*The New Testament Rendered from the Original Greek by James 

M. Kleist, S. J., and Joseph Lilly, C. M. (Milwaukee, Wis.: Bruce, 1954). 

Used with permission of the publisher.

prepared the people to accept this evidence of His divine love. The multiplication of the loaves and fishes prefigured the Bread of Angels which would nourish our souls and enrich them with Christ's divine life. "In those days again, when there was a great multitude and they had nothing to eat; calling His disciples together, He saith to them: 'I have compassion on the multitude, for behold they have now been with Me three days and have nothing to eat. And if I shall send them away fasting to their home, they will faint in the way; for some of them came from afar off.' And His disciples answered 

Him: 'From whence can anyone fill them here with bread in the wilderness?' And He asked them: 'How many loaves have ye?' 

Who said: 'Seven.' [And He commanded the multitude to sit down on the ground.] And taking the seven loaves, giving thanks, He broke and gave to His disciples for to set before them. And they set them before the people. And they had a few little fishes; and He blessed them and commanded them to be set before them. And they did eat and were filled: and they took up that which was left of the fragments, seven baskets. And they that had eaten were about four thousand. And He sent them away." (St. Mark 8:1-9)

Jesus declared clearly and unmistakably that in the Holy Eucharist He would bequeath to all mankind to

the end of the world His Body and Blood, His soul and divinity, under the appearance of bread and wine. Rationalists and other unbelievers have failed miserably in their attempts to distort this truth. 

St. Paul confirms our doctrine of the Real Presence. "For I have received of the Lord that which also I delivered unto you, that the Lord Jesus, the same night in which He was betrayed, took bread, and giving thanks, broke and said: "Take ye and eat: This is My Body, which shall be delivered for you. This do for the commemoration of Me.' In like manner also the chalice, after He had supped, saying: "This chalice is the new testament in 

My Blood. This do ye, as often as you shall drink, for the commemoration of Me. For as often as you shall eat this bread and drink. the chalice, you shall show the death of the

Lord, until He come.' Therefore, whosoever shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the 

Body and of the Blood of the Lord. But let a man prove himself: and so let him eat of that bread and drink of the chalice. For he that eateth and drinketh unworthily eateth and drinketh judgment to himself, not discerning the Body of the Lord." (1 Corinthians 11:23-29) "For My Flesh is meat indeed: and My Blood is drink indeed. He that eateth My Flesh and drinketh My Blood abideth in Me: and I in

him. As the living Father hath sent Me and 

I live by the Father: so he that eateth Me, the same also shall live by Me. This is the bread that came down from heaven. Not as your fathers did eat manna and are dead. 

He that eateth this bread shall live forever." (St. John 6:56-59) 

The word "eucharist" stems from two Greek words: "eu" and "charistia,” i. e., the giving of thanks. Our Lord designed the Holy Eucharist both as a sacrament and a sacrifice. It is a sacrament because it was instituted by Christ, has an outward sign of inward grace— matter and form; and because it functions "ex opere operato," of its own inherent power. Holy Communion nourishes and sanctifies the soul. It is a sacrifice, because it is the offering of a visible gift and the consuming of that gift to glorify God and to acknowledge His supremacy. 

The Holy Eucharist consists in a transient action in the adorable Sacrifice and in a permanent state, under the appearances of bread and wine, in the Blessed Sacrament reserved in our tabernacles. From ancient times the faithful have known the Holy Eucharist by way of excellence as the Last Supper, Holy Communion, the Body of the 

Lord. The expression "Sacrament of the Altar" indicates its sacrificial character. Regarded as an action, the Holy 

Eucharist is the Sacrifice of the Mass, wherein bread and wine are changed into the Body and Blood of Christ.

This transubstantiation constitutes the Sacrifice wherein our Saviour, as victim, makes the oblation of Himself to 

His heavenly Father.

The Eucharist, considered as a sacrament, means that Jesus Christ is really, truly, substantially present under the appearances of bread and wine. Really, objectively not, as Calvin and his followers asserted, by reason of our belief. Truly not, as Zwingli claimed, under a symbol. Substantially — not, as Calvin falsely taught, merely by the effects of the works of Christ. 

A year before the Last Supper, Jesus foretold the institution of the Blessed Sacrament. To prove that He did not speak in figures or symbols, He challenged the Apostles to make a profession of faith. Many disciples walked away when they heard this doctrine. Jesus asked the Apostles: 'Will you also go away?' And Simon Peter answered Him: 'Lord, to whom shall we go? 

Thou hast the words of eternal life. And we have believed and have known that Thou art the Christ, the Son of God.'" (St. John 6:68-70) 

Can the Real Presence of Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament be proved from reason? No! But it can be proved to be reasonable. Sacred Scripture affords abundant proof. Note the promise of Christ: “Amen, amen, I say unto you: ... He that eateth My Flesh and drinketh My Blood hath everlasting life: and I will raise him up in the last day." (St. John 6:54, 55)

"EMMANUEL - GOD WITH US" 

Our Saviour apparently did not intend a figurative meaning, for among the Jews to eat the flesh of another signified to inflict injury. This, Jesus did not teach. Jesus meant what He said in a literal sense, and it was taken as such by His audience. In fact, Christ confirmed this meaning of His words and at the same time stressed His divinity. He deplored the lack of faith, as well as the worldly slant given to His words. The people were slow to grasp the difference between the human and divinethe material and spiritual. Moreover, had Christ not intended to give us His Body and Blood to nourish our souls, the words: "This is My Body;... this is My 

Blood," would be meaningless and false. The bread and wine are changed into the Body and Blood of Christ; therefore, a real change takes place. Christ, in truth, did not offer bread and wine on the Cross. He offered His real Body and Blood. The whole substance of bread and wine is changed into the substance of the Body and Blood of Christ-transubstantiation; only the appearances of bread and wine remain. You may conclude, therefore, that the integral, as well as the essential parts of the bread and wine, are likewise changed into the Body and 

Blood of Christ; because these integral and essential parts either contain or constitute the substance of the bread and wine, so that nothing of the former substance remains. What does remain the same are the external appearances, or the accidents, which are accessory to the substance.

Were His Presence not real, our Lord would be the cause of idolatry for centuries throughout the world. 

The Israelites were fed manna in the desert. To eat this in the state of mortal sin was not a sin, although the manna served as a figure of Christ. If the Blessed Eucharist in the New Law were only a figure, those who partook of it unworthily would not incur sin. The most ancient liturgies, the Doctors, the Tradition of the Church, the universal belief on the part of Catholics, from the institution of the Holy Eucharist at the Last Supper down to the present, all confirm the explicit doctrine of the Real Presence. Those of the Eastern Churches which are still separated from the Mother Church retain this belief even today.

The presence of God in the Old Law (veiled by a cloud) foreshadowed His Real Presence in the New Law. "I will appear in a cloud over the oracle."

REALITY 

(Leviticus 16:2) 

The Old Law foreshadowed the habitation of God among men in the New. Moreover, the yearning of the immortal soul for God's presence is satisfied in a measure even in this world and gives man a foretaste of heaven, which consists essentially in the possession of God. The 

Real Presence of Jesus continues under the species of bread and wine as long as the species themselves continue to exist. When our divine Lord gave Himself to all of us at the Last Supper with the words: "This is My Body;...

this is My Blood," He gave what existed when He spoke these words.

Once you establish that Christ determined to be present by virtue of the words of consecration, you will conclude logically that He continues His Real Presence among us. The prevailing custom of reserving the 

Blessed Sacrament in churches, and in private homes during times of persecution, proves the conviction of the Church that Jesus Christ continues His loving Presence under the sacramental appearance of bread and wine. 

In the Blessed Sacrament our Saviour gives us the same Body He possessed on earth. Christ is then entirely present under the appearance of bread and under the appearance of wine. Unless the bread is changed into the same living Body now glorified in heaven, the words "This is My Body" could not be verified. The same is true of the wine changed into the Precious Blood of the living Christ. In the Western Church people do not receive under both species because this is not necessary. 

Where the Body of the living Christ is present, there also are His Precious Blood, soul and divinity. Likewise, where His Precious Blood is, there also are His Body, soul and divinity the whole Christ. 

Because of the inseparable union between them, His Blood is present also under the species of bread and His Body under the species of wine - and His soul under both species by concomitance. 

In consequence of the hypostatic union of the human nature of Christ with the Second Person of the Blessed

Trinity, His divinity always accompanies His humanity

under the appearance of bread and under the appearance of wine. Christ is entirely present in each particle of either species before and after its division. 

Only bishops and priests have the power to consecrate to change the bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ, because to them only does Christ confide this sublime prerogative. Tradition confirms this fact, proving that from the Last Supper priests and bishops were the ministers of this great sacrament. 

MATTER AND FORM 

Wheat bread, leavened or unleavened, and the wine of the grape constitute the material of this sacrament. 

Leavened bread is used in Eastern-rite Churches, unleavened in the Latin Church. What bread did Jesus use?

He consecrated unleavened bread because it was the day of the Pasch. Now, just as food and drink provide a meal, so bread and wine were standard food. The same holds true in some countries today. Bread and wine nourish the body. These elements are appropriate, therefore, to indicate the nourishing of the soul. In fact, the food we assimilate is changed into the substance of our bodies. 

There is no reason, then, why the power of God cannot change bread and wine into the substance of His Body and Blood.

The form of the Holy Eucharist consists in the very words employed by Christ at the Last Supper: "This is 

My Body; ... this is My Blood." These words make the action efficacious. The priest standing at the altar assumes the Person of Christ, renews the same ceremonies,

and pronounces the same words. The expression "This is My Body;... this is My Blood," indicates two truths.

First, that the bread and wine are changed into the Body and Blood of Christ; and, secondly, the presence of the 

Body and Blood of Christ. In fact, only by a change of substance could the Body and Blood of Christ be present on our altars. Luther admitted the Real Presence only in the act of receiving Holy Communion. Moreover, he tried to pervert transubstantiation by teaching "consubstantiation" — i. e., the "coexistence" of the Body and 

Blood of Christ with the substance of bread and wine.

To be consistent, the result of partaking of this sublime sacrament must be to the soul the same as the natural effects of food and drink upon the body — nourishment. 

Specifically, the worthy reception of Holy Communion brings an increase of sanctifying grace - the basis of the spiritual life. It confers actual graces, remits venial sins, fortifies us against falling into mortal sins, and serves as a pledge of eternal happiness. In this sacrament Christ 

Himself, the source of all graces, coming to our souls imparts the graces we need. He enriches us by His own divine life.

Holy Scripture records the reception of Holy Communion under one kind; other passages indicate the reception of Christ's Body and Blood. To both, our Lord attaches the same grace. "He that eateth this bread shall live forever." (St. John 6:59)

Because Christ is entirely present under either species, it is not necessary to partake of Holy Communion under both species. Both species- -or either-signify the effect of the sacrament namely, the nourishment of the soul. Usage from the earliest centuries of Christianity confirms this practice of giving Communion under one species, especially in times of persecution. Only under the species of bread was the Blessed Sacrament preserved in private homes, in prisons, and other shelters to protect it from desecration. Many cogent reasons exist for the reception of Holy Communion under the form of bread. 

To receive Holy Communion worthily, one must be in the state of grace, because this is a sacrament of the living. St. Paul teaches: "But let a man prove himself: and so let him eat of that bread and drink of the chalice." (1 Corinthians 11:28) 

The sanctity of the sacrament demands preparation of the soul. To receive our Lord unworthily is a serious sin, a sacrilege. "Therefore, whosoever shall eat this bread, or drink the chalice of the Lord unworthily, shall be guilty of the Body and of the Blood of the Lord.... For he that eateth and drinketh unworthily eateth and drinketh judgment to himself, not discerning the Body of the Lord." (1 Corinthians 11:27, 29)

Except in the case of those seriously ill, a fast from solid food for three hours, and from liquids (except water) for one hour is required. Water does not break the fast. Respect demands that the body be cared for and properly attired. Outward appearance can well express inward reverence. The faithful welcome the divine 

Guest with acts of faith, hope and charity.

Chapter XXX  AS A SACRIFICE 

ROM the beginning of time man understood the some formal act of worship. The best way he could express himself was by sacrifice. Even though he was misguided and in error as to the correct ritual, nevertheless, the sense of his obligation remained. 

Sacrifice is a visible offering made to God and the consuming of that gift to acknowledge God's supreme dominion over us.

THE OLD DISPENSATION 

Sacrifices in the Old Law served as a visible expression of the worship of God on the part of the people. 

They brought to the temple the first fruits of the field and the first of the flock, to be burnt on their altars. 

Thus sacrifice publicly acknowledged their debt to God. 

A religion, to be real and effective, needs a sacrifice. In the Old Law, God directed, through His representatives, many sacrifices as is recorded in the Mosaic Law. These liturgies prefigure the Sacrifice of the New Law. That our Saviour offered Himself, a Victim on the Cross, shedding His Precious Blood for us and for our salvation, is made clear by St. Paul's reference: "Christ... offered Himself unspotted unto God" (Hebrews 9:14). 296

AS A SACRIFICE 

297 Moreover, this sacrifice took the place of the many ritualistic sacrifices of the Old Law. "[Christ] offering one sacrifice for sins, forever sitteth on the right hand of 

God" (Hebrews 10:12).

In the capacity of Priest and Victim, Jesus immolated 

Himself, shedding the last drop of His Blood. He suffered and died in expiation for all sins, and at the same time rendered homage to the infinite majesty of God.

SACRIFICE 

To rightly reflect its divine Founder, the Catholic religion had to possess a real and perpetual form of worship superior to that of the Old Law. No other formal act among Christians has the conditions necessary for a perpetual sacrifice except the Holy Mass. Through Malachias the prophet, God foretold: "For from the rising of the sun even to the going down, My name is great among the Gentiles: and in every place there is sacrifice and there is offered to My name a clean oblation" (Mala chias 1:11). This refers to the Holy Mass. 

In the Mass, as on Calvary, one and the same Victim, Jesus Christ, is offered and sacrificed. In the Mass, as on 

Calvary, one and the same High Priest, Jesus Christ, offers the Sacrifice, through the officiating priest, His representative. "Let a man so account of us as of the ministers of Christ, and the dispensers of the mysteries of God." (1 Corinthians 4:1)

Therefore, at the consecration of the Mass, the priest does not use the form: "This is the Body of Christ,” but assuming the person of Christ, he solemnly declares: "This is My Body." The difference between the Sacrifice on Calvary and the Sacrifice of the Mass is in the manner of offering; the objectives are the same. 

REPETITION OF CALVARY 

Jesus actually died on the Cross, after really shedding the last drop of His Precious Blood. In the Mass no actual shedding of blood takes place no real death but only a mystical shedding of blood and a mystical death. The three principal parts of the Mass are: the 

Offertory, the Consecration and the consuming of the Victim in Holy Communion. The separate consecration of the bread and wine represents the death of Christ on the

Cross. The essential of the Mass consists in the twofold consecration of the bread and the wine. Although the offering of Holy Mass does not in itself redeem men, it does bring to them the fruits of the Redemption. 

The sacred rites of the Old Testament, with their pre- scription that the people partake of the offerings after they were sacrificed - the Paschal Lamb or the first fruits of the field, for example - foreshadowed the close union with God that was to be perfected by the reception of Holy Communion in the New Law. 

To re-enact the Sacrifice of the Cross in the Mass, the celebrant offers it under both species and consumes both species in Holy Communion. In the first days of Christianity, the rites covering the Offertory, the Consecration and the Communion were called the Mass of the

AS A SACRIFICE 

299 Faithful. The ceremonies in preparation and before the Offertory came to be known as the Mass of the Catechumens.

RITUAL SIGNIFICANT 

The sacred ceremonies surrounding the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass and the vestments worn have their meaning and are intended to express and focus the attention of worshipers on this sublime rite. The use of Latin in the liturgy has many advantages-among them, the fact that it does not change and that it is understood by priests throughout the world as a common medium of expression.

Keep in mind the fundamental fact that a sacrifice consists in the offering of an object by a priest to God and the consuming of it primarily to acknowledge God as the Creator of all things. On the Cross, Jesus offered 

Himself as a sacrifice to God alone.

The Mass, the unbloody repetition and the continuation of the Sacrifice of the Cross, has the same four objectives for which Christ died on Calvary — namely: 

(1) To glorify God: Praise. 

(2) To thank Him for all His gifts and favors and for His loving paternal solicitude: Thanksgiving. (3) To atone for the sins of mankind: Atonement. (4) To invoke the graces we need - Petition (often referred to as impetration).

The Canon of the Mass expresses this fourfold purpose. 

CHANNELS of Grace

The merits our Saviour obtained for us by His Redemption on the Cross are channeled to the soul through the sacraments. God's plan specifies that the Holy Sacrifice be constantly offered for the needs of mankind. The acquisition or the application of related graces depends, to a great extent, on the proper disposition, the intention of those who offer the Mass, or of those for whom it may be offered. The prayers recited in the Canon of the 

Mass, in behalf of the whole Church, for "all who profess the Catholic and apostolic faith...," and for those "who have gone before us with the sign of faith, and who sleep in peace," clearly indicate that both the Church militant here on earth and the Church suffering in purgatory share in the general fruits of the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass. 

The faithful, however, who offer the Mass with the priest - for "all who are present"; and those for whom the celebrant applies the Mass by a direct intention, receive special graces — for example, the deceased person for whom the funeral Mass is celebrated; the bride and groom for whom the Nuptial Mass is offered.

Clearly, then, the Sacrifice of the Mass, instituted at the Last Supper and continued on Calvary, cannot be considered a mere prayer because it is the death of Christ, mystically —that is, spiritually — renewed.

Chapter XXXI EXTREME UNCTION 

HE words "Extreme Unction" mean the last anoint 

THE 

ing. The first anointing comes with baptism. The sacrament of Confirmation marks the second anointing. 

The conferring of Holy Orders marks the third unction. 

Extreme Unction is a sacrament instituted by Jesus Christ in which, by virtue of the anointing and prayer of the priest, a sick person receives special sacramental graces to strengthen his soul and sometimes his body when he is in danger of death from sickness, accident or old age. The authority for this sacrament rests on the revealed word of God as proclaimed by St. James the Apostle: "Is any man sick among you? Let him bring in the priests of the Church and let them pray over him, anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord. And the prayer of faith shall save the sick man. And the Lord shall raise him up: and if he be in sins, they shall be forgiven him." (St. James 5:14-15) 

Only God could attach to an outward sign the forgiveness of sins indicated in the above text. In the sacrament of Extreme Unction, Christ intended a permanent 301

institution. Tradition confirms this doctrine. The sacrament of Extreme Unction: (1) Increases sanctifying grace. (2) Gives comfort in sickness and strength against temptation. (3) Prepares us for entrance into heaven by the remission of venial sins and the cleansing of our souls from the remains of sin. (4) Gives health of body when it is good for the soul.

Inasmuch as mortal sins are remitted through the sacraments of Baptism and Penance, the sins referred to by St. James are generally understood to be venial sins. 

When, however, the sick person, even though rightly disposed, is unable to receive the sacrament of Penance, Extreme Unction can remit mortal sin. The words of 

St. James are universal and therefore extend to such an emergency.

The anointing with holy oil signifies healing power: "The Lord will raise him up" - that is, the grace of the sacrament strengthens one to accept suffering patiently and to resist temptation. 

Extreme Unction does not always restore the sick to health, nor does it effect miraculous cures. But just as 

God produces spiritual effects through the sacraments, so He often causes the bodily effect of better health through 

Extreme Unction.

EXTREME UNCTION 

303 

IN DANGER OF DEATH 

Any Christian who has attained the use of reason and who is seriously ill may receive this sacrament validly. 

To receive Extreme Unction worthily one should be in the state of grace. Those in probable or certain proximity of death from an external cause- for instance, execution or war― are not generally subjects for Extreme Unction.

Consider the objectives of this sacrament—namely, to fortify us in our last agony, to remit venial sins and their effects, and to prepare us for our final journey. Some danger must already exist to warrant the reception of this sacrament, which ordinarily may be received but once during the same illness. The effects of the sacrament continue as long as the danger of death continues. 

Catholics are admonished to promptly call for a priest when a member of the family becomes ill. Physicians often find it difficult to determine whether or not the malady is terminal. The grace of the sacraments always helps the patient. 

The remote matter of Extreme Unction is the oil of the infirm, consecrated by the bishop on Holy Thursday. 

Anointing with this holy oil constitutes the proximate matter of this sacrament. The prayer accompanying the unction constitutes the form of the sacrament. When the priest, the ordinary minister of Extreme Unction, anoints each of the five senses: eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, hands and feet, he recites the following prayer: "By this holy anointing and by His most bountiful mercy, may 

God pardon thee whatever wrong thou hast done by the use of (this sense)," i. e., sight, hearing, etc.

Chapter XXXII HOLY ORDERS 

NDER the legislation decreed by God, religion in UND the Old Testament included basic elements of the priesthood, sacrifices, and custody of the tabernacle. In the New Law the priesthood is bestowed in a sacrament- Holy Orders - the reception of which results in Sacred Orders or elevation to the sacred ranks or grades in the Church. Holy Orders imprints a character on the soul. 

The priest is known as an "alter Christus" or another Christ, because by offering the adorable Sacrifice of the Mass and administering the sacraments he carries on the work of our Saviour.

In Holy Orders, bishops, priests, and other ministers of the Church receive from God the power and grace to perform their sacred duties. "... they chose Stephen, a man full of faith and of the Holy Ghost, and Philip and Prochorus and Nicanor, and Timon and Parmenas and Nicolas, a proselyte of Antioch. 

These they set before the Apostles: and they praying, imposed hands upon them." 

(Acts of the Apostles 6:5-6) 

By the institution of Holy Orders, God intended that the priesthood, the Holy Mass, and the sacraments should continue as the means of sanctifying mankind. 304

HOLY ORDERS 

305 At the Last Supper, Jesus conferred a real priesthood on the Apostles and at the same time commissioned them to do the same for their successors. "And taking bread, He gave thanks and brake and gave to them, saying: 'This is My Body, which is given for you. Do this for a commemoration of Me.' In like manner, the chalice also, after He had supped, saying: "This is the chalice, the new testament in My 

Blood, which shall be shed for you.' (St. Luke 22:19-20)

On this solemn occasion Christ empowered the Apostles to change the substance of bread and wine into the substance of His Body and Blood, soul and divinity. Besides giving the Apostles the power of offering Holy Mass, He commissioned them and their successors to forgive sins. The Apostles, therefore, were ordained true priests. 

Our divine Saviour intended that a permanent institution of the sacred priesthood should be continued by means of the sacrament of Holy Orders and should last forever.

The words of Christ at the Last Supper, "Do this for a commemoration of Me," were spoken not only to the Apostles but to their successors. The same holds true for the power of forgiving sins - it is to continue for all time. "For every high priest taken from among men is ordained for men in the things that appertain to God, that he may offer up gifts and sacrifices for sins: who can have compassion

on them that are ignorant and that err: because he himself also is compassed with infirmity. And therefore he ought, as for the people, so also for himself, to offer for sins. 

Neither doth any man take the honor to himself, but he that is called by God, as Aaron was." (Hebrews 5:1-4) 

The word "priest" derives from the Greek "presbyteros," which means elder or chief and thus came to be applied to one ordained to offer the eucharistic Sacrifice and to perform other sacerdotal functions. The Latin word for priest, "sacerdos," means "an offerer of holy things." The word "priest" also denoted a ruler — a title of dignity irrespective of age. 

TO SANCTIFY 

The term for the priesthood has a plural form, Holy Orders, because of various grades and different ranks among the ministers of the altar. In the sacrament of 

Holy Orders, priests receive the power to offer sacrifice, to sanctify the faithful, to reconcile sinners to God in the sacrament of Penance, and to nourish their souls in Holy Communion. The twofold power conferred by Jesus 

Christ when He ordained the Apostles at the Last Supperi. e., the power to consecrate bread and wine and the power to forgive sins- implies also the commission to administer the other five sacraments: all instituted to sanctify and save souls.

HOLY ORDERS 

307 A distinction exists between the divine commission to teach, legislate and direct, known as the power of jurisdiction; and the power of orders. "Take heed to yourselves and to the whole flock, wherein the Holy Ghost hath placed you bishops, to rule the Church of God which 

He hath purchased with His own Blood." 

(Acts of the Apostles 20:28) 

Addressing the Apostles and their successors in the priesthood, Jesus said: "Do this for a commemoration of 

Me" (St. Luke 22:19); that is, continue to exercise this sublime privilege as long as there are people on earth who are in need of the sacraments. "Neglect not the grace that is in thee: which was given thee by prophecy, with imposition of the hands of the priesthood. Meditate upon these things: be wholly in these things: that thy profiting may be manifest to all. 

Take heed to thyself and to doctrine: be earnest in them. For in doing this thou shalt both save thyself and them that hear thee.” (1 Timothy 4:14-16) 

St. Ignatius Martyr, who lived from the year 50 to some time near the beginning of the second century A. D., and who was appointed by St. Peter as Bishop of Antioch, taught that Jesus lives in His Church through its bishops, priests and deacons.

The priesthood continues in the Church by virtue of the sacrament of Holy Orders, which confers the priestly office together with special graces to perform it faithfully. "For every high priest is appointed to offer gifts and sacrifices: wherefore it is necessary that he also should have something to offer." (Hebrews 8:3)

A SUBLIME Calling 

God alone can grant interior gifts signified by outward signs. Being a sacrament of the living, Holy Orders must be received in the state of grace. St. Paul wrote to Timothy: "...I admonish thee that thou stir up the grace of God which is in thee by the imposition of my hands." (2 Timothy 1:6) 

Following graduation from a collegiate course or its equivalent, six or more years of training and self-discipline, of study of the sacred sciences, are required before a seminarian can qualify for elevation to the priesthood. 

A divine invitation or call is a vocation. A true vocation can be determined by study, prayer, and the prudent direction of one's confessor, who weighs the talents, disposition, and related strength of character. Certain signs, positive and negative, especially a definite desire, predicate a vocation.

HOLY ORDERS 

309 In each step of Holy Orders, the ordinand receives sanctifying grace and special sacramental graces to perform faithfully the duties of his office. Once a priest always a priest, because the character of the priesthood is permanent. "Thou art a priest forever according to the order of Melchisedech." 

(Psalm 109:4 and Hebrews 5:6) MINOR ORDERS 

Minor orders preparatory to the priesthood are preceded by tonsure, not itself an order but a ceremony of admission to the clerical ranks. The minor orderswhich constitute sacramentals—are those of (a) porter; (b) lector; (c) exorcist; and (d) acolyte.

The major orders include (a) the subdiaconate; (b) the diaconate; (c) the priesthood. 

Parents may pray for and encourage but not impose a vocation on their children. 

While the powers are the same, priests belonging to religious orders-e. g., Benedictines, Franciscans, Jesuits, etc. are called "regular" because their priestly life follows a rule adopted and approved by the Pope for each Society or Congregation in the Church. Diocesan priests, who function within a given jurisdiction, are directed by their bishop. The bishops alone confer the sacrament of Holy Orders. 

The faithful reverence priests as the ambassadors of 

Christ (cf. 2 Corinthians 5:20); the "dispensers of the

mysteries of God" (1 Corinthians 4:1); "the salt of the earth" (St. Matthew 5:13). "The ancients therefore that are among you, I beseech who am myself also an ancient and a witness of the sufferings of Christ, as also a partaker of that glory which is to be revealed in time to come: Feed the flock of

God which is among you, taking care of it, not by constraint but willingly, according to

God: not for filthy lucre's sake but voluntarily: neither as lording it over the clergy but being made a pattern of the flock from the heart. And when the Prince of Pastors shall appear, you shall receive a never-fading crown of glory. In like manner, ye young men, be subject to the ancients. And do you all insinuate humility one to another: for 

God resisteth the proud, but to the humble 

He giveth grace. 

(1 St. Peter 5:1-5)

Chapter XXXIII MATRIMONY 

MAT 

ATRIMONY was instituted by God in Paradise and later raised to the dignity of a sacrament by Jesus Christ. It is a bilateral contract between one man and one woman to "live with each other in permanent wedlock." The sacrament of Matrimony "ex opere operato," of its own inherent virtue, bestows God's special sacramental grace on this holy union. 

The laws of the Church require Catholics who contemplate entering the married state to take a course of instructions relative to the obligations and rights of married life; also to answer truthfully the questions proposed in the canonical prenuptial investigation. The wisdom of Mother Church in these precautions is obvious. For a valid marriage, both contracting parties should be qualified re age; each must be free to marry and must express free consent. Boys under sixteen years of age and girls under fourteen may not validly marry.

Civil marriages may be valid contracts — binding as far as the civil effects are concerned - but they are not sacraments. Matrimony is a sacrament which unites a 

Christian man and woman in lawful permanent marriage. The Book of Genesis records how, in the institution of marriage in Paradise, God specified its unity. "It is not good for man to be alone; let us make him a help like unto himself." (Genesis 2:18) 311

ee 

.. God created man to His own image: . male and female He created them. And God blessed them, saying: 'Increase and multiply, and fill the earth.' (Genesis 1:27, 28) 

THREE ESSENTIALS 

The primary end of Matrimony is the procreation of children. Parents have the sublime privilege of peopling heaven with saints; they therefore co-operate with the divine Creator in extending the work of creation. Entrusted with immortal souls, parents assume the obligation to nourish their children's bodies; to clothe and shelter them; and, by means of a well-balanced religious education, to develop their intellects; likewise, to give their children good example. The second objective of marriage is the fostering of mutual love. The third objective, to help each other and the children attain heaven.

The fundamental attributes of the sacrament of Matrimony are unity and indissolubility—both implied in its divine institution. The marriage vows bind "until death do us part." Christ taught explicitly: "What therefore God hath joined together, let no man put asunder." (St. Mark 10:9)

ST. PAUL'S INSTRUCTION 

When Christ restored Matrimony to its original purity, He also elevated it to the dignity of a sacrament.

MATRIMONY 

313 

Wherefore St. Paul observes: "This is a great sacrament; but I speak in Christ and in the Church" (Ephesians 5:32). When entered into by the unbaptized, marriage may be valid but it is not a sacrament. Between 

Christians, however, marriage is a sacrament. For them it produces an increase of sanctifying grace and sacramental graces to assist married couples to fulfill their sacred duties to each other and to their children. 

In the marriage of Christians the contract itself cannot be separated from the sacrament of Matrimony. It fol lows, then, that the bride and groom are the ministers of this sacrament. Their individual persons supply the matter of the sacrament; the form consists in the expression of their mutual consent. The officiating priest serves as a witness. Two other witnesses are required. 

Sacramental marriage has a unique spiritual significance, because it represents the union of Christ and His 

Church. Hence St. Paul teaches: "The husband is the head of the wife, as 

Christ is the head of the Church.... Therefore, as the Church is subject to Christ, so also let wives be subject to their husbands in all things. Husbands, love your wives, as 

Christ also loved the Church, and delivered 

Himself up for it." (Ephesians 5:23, 24-25)

SPIRITUAL EFFECTS 

When the Apostle writes "subject to their husbands in all things," it is understood in all things, sin excepted.

Matrimony is a sacrament of the living and must be received in the state of grace.

The State has the right to legislate regarding the civil effects of Matrimony; the Church, to whom has been entrusted the sacraments, has both the right and the duty to legislate regarding the spiritual effects of Matrimony. 

Separation, without dissolution of the marriage bond, may be granted by the bishop for grave reasons known as canonical causes. This permission does not allow either party to remarry.

The Council of Trent teaches: "If anyone asserts that the Church has not the right to establish impediments invalidating marriage, or that it has erred in establishing them, let him be anathema" (Session XXIV, canon 4).

The reason for the right to legislate in these matters rests on the fundamental that Christian marriage is a sacrament. Christ committed the administration of the sacraments to His Church. Therefore, the Church alone acts within her rights when she prescribes forbidding and invalidating impediments. Forbidding (or impeding) impediments are those which render the marriage illicit. 

Invalidating (or diriment) impediments render the marriage null and void.

DIVORCE 

The divorce decrees of a state are wrong when contrary to the laws of God.

The Catholic Church does not grant a divorce. Its tribunal of justice investigates a particular marriage to determine whether or not a true marriage existed from the beginning. Therefore, the decree of nullity states

MATRIMONY 

315 whether the marriage in question was or was not invalid from the start.

INVALIDATING IMPEDIMENTS 

(1) Blood relationship in the direct line of descente. g., between father and daughter, between grandfather and granddaughter, etc., indefinitely; or collaterally to the third degree, as between children of the same parents; between first and second cousins. 

(2) Spiritual relationship. The only spirit ual relationship which is a diriment impediment of marriage is that which arises from baptism between the person baptized and the person baptizing and between the one baptized and the sponsor. (3) Affinity — kinship contracted with blood relatives of one's spouse. This applies in the direct line, and to the second degree of the collateral line. A. An example of the direct line would be: John marries Bertha. Bertha dies, leaving three children. John wishes to marry 

Margaret, Bertha's mother. This would be affinity in the first degree, direct line. 

B. An example of affinity in the second degree of the collateral line, would be: 

John wishes to marry Julia, first cousin of his deceased wife, Bertha. This

would be affinity in the second degree, collateral line. (4) Solemn profession in a religious Order or the reception of one of the Sacred Orders, beginning with the subdiaconate. (6) Certain crimes - (5) Disparity of cult- the marriage of a Catholic with an unbaptized person. adultery coupled with a promise to marry or with an attempted (civil) marriage; adultery coupled with the murder of the spouse of one of the adulterers; or the murder of the consort alone. (7) Violent abduction and detention of a woman with intent to marry her. (This impediment exists as long as the woman remains in the power of the abductor; in other words, as long as her will is not free.) (8) Lack of proper age- for a male, before the age of sixteen; for a female, before the age of fourteen. (9) Impotence -any physical condition or defect that makes the marriage relationship between two persons impossible.

Since the first purpose of marriage is the procreation of children, such an impeding condition or defect prevents the parties from entering into a true marriage.

MATRIMONY 

317 (10) The bond of a previous marriage. One who is still bound by the tie of marriage cannot marry another. The exceptions to this are: (1) A couple who have been married but never lived together as husband and wife can, for a good reason, be dispensed from the marriage bond by the Pope and be permitted to enter another marriage. (2) Also, if one of two parties makes solemn profession in a religious Order, the Pope may declare the marriage bond dissolved if the marriage was not consummated. (3) The third instance is known as the Pauline 

Privilege: If two non-baptized persons are married and one of them later is baptized a Catholic, as long as the other remains unbaptized and refuses to live peaceably with the marriage partner, the bishop may allow the Catholic party to contract marriage with another Catholic provided the required conditions have been fulfilled. (11) Public decency. This impediment prevents one who has lived in sinful union with another from marrying any of that person's relatives to the second degree in the direct line.

FORBIDDING IMPEDIMENTS 

(1) Legal relationship. This pertains to adoption. In this matter the Church fol-

lows the law. If the civil law of a country says that such a marriage is illicit, the Church then follows that law. (It should be noted here that where civil law declares such a marriage null and void, the Church also would regard it as invalid and the legal relationship in that case would constitute an invalidating impediment.) (2) A simple vow of chastity, of perpetual virginity, of celibacy, or a vow to enter the religious state or receive Holy Orders. (3) Mixed religion -the marriage of two baptized persons, one a Catholic, the other a member of a non-Catholic or schismatic sect.

If two parties married despite any of the above forbidding impediments, it would be wrong for them to do so, but theirs would be a true marriage. In the case of the invalidating impediments, the marriage would be null and void.

Those contemplating Matrimony should notify their pastor at least a month before the date set for the wedding.

Men and women embarking on this life contract are directed to consult the prudent advice of their parents and confessor.

Chapter XXXIV  

N A broad sense, grace is the loving solicitude of our IN 

Father in heaven. The intense love of our divine Lord enfolds every creature and pursues even the sinner who fled Him "down the nights and down the days,... down the arches of the years" (Francis Thompson, The Hound of Heaven). Indeed, God is more anxious to forgive than we are to be forgiven. The story of the prodigal son in The Book exemplifies the grace of God penetrating the dark places of the soul and bringing warmth and light, as it did to the ungrateful prodigal reduced to misery. Note the affectionate welcome, the honors bestowed upon him by his father. Holy Scripture records this: "I say to you that even so there shall be joy in heaven upon one sinner that doth penance, more than upon ninety-nine just who need not penance. 

(St. Luke 15:7) 

Remember the consoling picture of the Good Shepherd leaving the flock to seek the sheep that was lost: "What man of you that hath a hundred sheep, and if he shall lose one of them, doth he not leave the ninety-nine in the desert and go after that which was lost, until he find it?" (St. Luke 15:4) 319

The Bible often compares the soul to an orchard: "I am come into My garden, O My sister, My spouse. I have gathered My myrrh, with My aromatical spices.... 

(Canticle of Canticles 5:1) 

St. John calls this wonderful seed, destined to fructify, "the grace of God."

DOCTRINAL FOUNDATION 

Theologians hold that the tremendous beauty of divine grace makes us poor human beings resemble God. 

Grace, according to St. Paul, puts us in the divine Family- a bold statement: "For in Him we live and move and are; as some also of your own poets said: 'For we are also His offspring.'' (Acts of the Apostles 17:28)

When does the grace of God, planted in the soul, cultivate real fruit? When it reaches maturity, depending on our acceptance, because God does not force His gifts on anyone. These divine seeds take root and develop under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit who supplies active principles to meet our timely co-operation. 

Jesus sent the Holy Spirit to enlighten and direct us:

The Holy Spirit is to the Church what the soul is to the body, the animating principle. "Know you not that you are the temple of 

God, and that the Spirit of God dwelleth in

you? But if any man violate the temple of

God, him shall God destroy. For the temple of God is holy, which you are." (1 Corinthians 3:16-17)
