The infinite fruit of our Saviour's redemption was to be lavished on souls by the Holy Spirit. He bestows on the Church the grace of permanence, infallibility, unity, holiness, universality and apostolicity. The same Spirit of love sanctifies souls by dwelling in them and disposing them to shun evil and do good so as to win heaven. 

Theologians refer to this inspiration and influence on our souls which disposes us to persevere unto the goal of eternal happiness, as divine grace. Grace-from the 

Latin "gratia"- -means "a gift"; something bestowed gratis without being earned by us. This gift of grace, this aid to salvation, is not due to our human nature but is supernatural, because it exceeds what belongs to our nature. Unaided, our natural powers could not win heaven. This spiritual uplift is interior when it comes directly from God; or it may be exterior, arising from some external source perhaps the influence of a sermon or seeing good examples of faith in action.

Many meanings may be applied to the word "grace"; for example, someone may say, "Her actions revealed grace and charm" - that is, attractiveness. Grace also means benevolence, as in "I hoped for his good graces in my behalf." Grace may signify gratitude, as in the phrase "grace after meals." But in our study, grace means a gift from God. We have many gifts from God,

such as life itself. That God not only created us but continues to sustain us is a great natural gift. The natural gifts are those that belong to the integrity of man, such as vision, the power to speak, to hear, etc. Strictly speaking, the words "the grace of God" mean a gift above the natural — for the most part invisible to us. 

There are two kinds of grace — actual and sanctifying grace. 

A PATTERN TO FOLLOW 

There are many erroneous ideas about grace which have led to confusion on the topic. Our purpose is to present a pattern of positive constructive doctrine. Reflect briefly on actual grace. (We use the word "reflect" often, because conviction comes from thinking.) We have seen that the word "grace" may be used to denote a certain poise and charm. But in a theological sense, grace signifies the supernatural gift merited by Jesus. 

Christ for our salvation.

The natural and preternatural blessings bestowed by 

God are not graces proper. Besides the natural gifts, such as vision, there are certain gifts which are classed as preternatural because they extend a natural gift- for instance, the preservation of a human body years after death. A supernatural gift, such as conversion from sin, exceeds the natural.

ACTUAL GRACE 

Actual grace is divine help which enlightens the mind and moves the will here and now to avoid evil and do

good. Bestowed on us when we need it, actual grace is not permanent but transient. Remember the truth that grace does not deprive us of free will. We can accept or reject God's gifts. Persistent rejection of God's goodness, love and mercy all crystallized in divine gracewould preclude pardon, because a stubborn refusal to be reconciled to God by refusing His grace contradicts repentance or sorrow for sin. This explains the so-called "unforgivable sin" against the Holy Ghost. Strictly speaking, there is no sin that cannot be forgiven.

Actual grace functions through the influence of the 

Holy Spirit not blindly but by a flash of light to the intellect — stirring up grace. An appeal to the will follows. When the will responds freely; when the rational being shows even a modicum of good will, he receives as a free gift the actual grace distributed by our divine Benefactor. For proof that the intellect needs grace, read in Sacred Scripture: 

"At that time, Jesus answered and said: 'I confess to Thee, O Father, Lord of heaven and earth, because Thou hast hid these things from the wise and prudent and hast revealed them to little ones.'' (St. Matthew 11:25)

The royal psalmist sang of the fact that the will of man needs to be stimulated by grace: "Blessed are the undefiled in the way: who walk in the law of the Lord. Blessed are they

that search His testimonies: that seek Him with their whole heart." (Psalm 118:1-2) 

Even the first stir of interest on the part of an unbeliever, at least his willingness to inquire, required divine grace a manifestation of the loving solicitude of our Father in heaven. "For who distinguished thee? Or what hast thou that thou hast not received, and if thou hast received, why dost thou glory, as if thou hadst not received it?" 

St. Paul reminds us: (1 Corinthians 4:7) "Not that we are sufficient to think anything of ourselves, as of ourselves: but our sufficiency is from God." (2 Corinthians 3:5) 

The very fact that your interest alerted you to read this book is in itself a grace from God. St. Paul further teaches that "faith comes by hearing" the truth. Divine 

Wisdom never expects the impossible of us. He does supply our deficiencies by assisting us to achieve what He expects us to do. To reap a supernatural reward we all need the grace of God for every assignment. "No man can come to Me, except the Father, who hath sent Me, draw him. And I will raise him up in the last day." (St. John 6:44)

This is an interior help. Jesus says: "I am the vine, you the branches" (St. John 15:5). Unless the branch clings to the vine, it cannot bear fruit, "For it is God who worketh in you, both to will and to accomplish, according to His good will." (Philippians 2:13) 

To be justified or sanctified, to persevere and to be resolute in trampling on temptations and thus avoid sin, we need constantly the grace of God. "For in many things we all offend. If any man offend not in word, the same is a perfect man.

He is able also with a bridle to lead about the whole body." (St. James 3:2)

FINAL PERSEVERANCE 

The determination of people leading consistently good lives, free from mortal sin, wins the special grace of final perseverance: "Blessed are those servants whom the Lord, when He cometh, shall find watching." (St. Luke 12:37) 

Perseverance implies our co-operation or prompt response to God's inspiration to shun evil and do good. No one

is deprived of God's bountiful help, because His antecedent will is that all be saved. "[Jesus] will have all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth. For there is one God: and one mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus who gave Himself a redemption for all, a testimony in due times." (1 Timothy 2:4-6) 

Therefore, the grace of God embraces all mankind.

Just why and how our Saviour distributes His gifts and the reasons why some respond and others reject these gifts, we cannot pretend to know. Nature, indeed many things we can see and touch, involve mysteries. The supernatural is even more of a mystery. 

Here a word about temptations is in order. All human beings must be tested in the crucible of temptation. "Son, when thou comest to the service of God, stand in justice and in fear: and prepare thy soul for temptation.. For gold and silver are tried in the fire, but acceptable men in the furnace of humiliation." ... 

(Ecclesiasticus 2:1, 5) 

As we have observed, there are good and bad angels. 

The good angels, and especially our guardian angel, aid us to reach the goal of eternal happiness.

"For He hath given His angels charge over thee, to keep thee in all thy ways.” 

(Psalm 90:11) 

The bad angels, those who defied God and lost, are permitted to tempt us but never beyond our strength. "Be sober and watch: because your adversary the devil, as a roaring lion, goeth about seeking whom he may devour." (1 St. Peter 5:8)

SANCTIFYING GRACE 

As distinguished from a temporary or transient gift, sanctifying grace, which makes the soul holy and pleasing to God, is a permanent state which can be lost only by mortal sin. It therefore merits the title of habitual grace. St. Paul describes it as the "charity of God... poured forth in our hearts by the Holy Ghost" (Romans 5:5). The Apostle of the Gentiles further illustrates this particular doctrine: "Not by the works of justice which we have done, but according to His mercy, He saved us, by the laver of regeneration and renovation of the Holy Ghost. Whom He hath poured forth upon us abundantly, through 

Jesus Christ our Saviour: that, being justified by His grace, we may be heirs according to hope of life everlasting." (Titus 3:5-7)

When one turns away from God by serious sin, he cuts off the source of spiritual life which stimulates the soul. He deprives himself of sanctifying grace. The first effect of sanctifying grace channeled to the soul through the sacrament of Penance removes sins committed after

Baptism. This sublime bestowal elevates us to the privilege of being "partakers of the divine nature.” "By whom He hath given us most great and precious promises: that by these you may be made partakers of the divine nature: flying the corruption of that concupiscence which is in the world.” (2 St. Peter 1:4)

Certain texts in Holy Scripture clearly indicate that grace can be increased. "For the lawgiver shall give a blessing; they shall go from virtue to virtue: the God of gods shall be seen in Sion." 

(Psalm 83:8) 

"But grow in grace and in the knowledge of our Lord and Saviour Jesus Christ. To Him be glory both now and unto the day of eternity." (2 St. Peter 3:18) "He that hath My commandments and keepeth them; he it is that loveth Me." (St. John 14:21)

"By this shall all men know that you are My disciples, if you have love one for another. (St. John 13:35)

THE JUST MAN 

While mortal sin robs the soul of sanctifying grace, venial sin does not. But venial sin, which dims the love of God within us, should be avoided.

When the just man lives in the state of grace, he abhors sin and all its occasions. Being enlightened by the Holy Spirit of wisdom, he gradually comes to realize that not of himself but from the mercy, goodness, and loving solicitude of our heavenly Father, he derives his sanctification. "But by the grace of God, I am what I am. 

And His grace in me hath not been void: but

I have labored more abundantly than all they. 

Yet not I, but the grace of God with me." (1 Corinthians 15:10) "Not to us, O Lord, not to us: but to Thy name give glory." (Psalm 113:1) 

Theologians make a distinction between condign and congruous merit. Condign merit means what justice demands. Congruous merit lays no claim to justice but depends on the generosity of another. Man, of course, cannot make any claims on God's gifts. "So you also, when you shall have done all these things that are commanded you, say:

"We are unprofitable servants; we have done that which we ought to do.' (St. Luke 17:10)

God in His goodness has promised to reward us for good works performed with the right intention. Therefore,

God bestows supernatural merit for our supernaturally good acts. "As to the rest, there is laid up for me a crown of justice which the Lord the just Judge will render to me in that day: and not only to me, but to them also that love His coming. Make haste to come to me quickly." (2 Timothy 4:8) 

SOURCE OF STRENGTH 

Merely natural acts cannot claim a supernatural reward, because the effect cannot be greater than the cause. 

Our divine Lord lavishes His graces on our souls; His grace pursues the sinner. He is the source of strength in our spiritual lives just as the sap from the trunk vitalizes the tree. We know what happens to the branch when it is cut off from the tree.

No one really merits the initial grace of baptism, but with our heavenly Father's loving solicitude both the just and sinners may congruously merit additional actual grace. Moreover, the just can thus win final perseverance and condignly merit an increase of sanctifying grace.

Theologians agree that when the sinner is reconciled to God through the sacrament of Penance, the merit of former graces is revived. This opinion is based on Holy 

"For God is not unjust, that He should forget your work and the love which you have shown in His name, you who have ministered and do minister to the saints." (Hebrews 6:10)

GRACE AND THE PRODIGAL SON 

The disposition to accept faith-indeed, the very first impulse or desire to know, love and serve God requires supernatural grace. For one to keep the "Golden

Rule" and the natural laws also requires divine help. 

The question may be posed: Do unbelievers who perform works of charity and who are careful to respect the moral order depend on the grace of God? The answer is yes. To constantly conquer temptation and persevere unto death implies a special grace, as does total preservation from every venial sin. Those who have fallen into mortal sin, as well as infidels and heretics, have the duty to repent and avoid further sin. Hence they receive sufficient grace for their conversion, which on account of the sinner's weakness would otherwise be impossible. "The Lord... dealeth patiently for your sake, not willing that any should perish, but that all should return to penance." (2 St. Peter 3:9)

While our divine Lord "dealeth patiently" with the sinner, He has not agreed to give him unlimited years or old age in which to repent, or normal use of his faculties to the end of his life. The grace of God is powerful - a tremendous urge to amend one's life; nevertheless, it cannot compel or stifle man's free will. On the contrary, the sinner may resist divine help and suppress the voice of conscience. Our divine Lord confirmed this truth when He envisioned the future catastrophe of 

Jerusalem: 

"Jerusalem, Jerusalem, thou that killest the prophets and stonest them that are sent unto thee, how often would I have gathered together thy children, as the hen doth gather her chickens under her wings, and thou wouldst not?" (St. Matthew 23:37)

In a strict interpretation, grace, communicated through the sacraments ("gratia gratum faciens"), is a divine gift which makes the soul holy and pleasing to God. 

This interior grace is given for our own salvation. Grace ("gratia gratis data") i. e., granted gratuitously, includes special gifts, such as miracles, prophecy, bestowed to aid others. 

At the weekly Dogma Seminar, held at the University of San Diego, the author's efforts to explain the doctrine of grace resulted in failure.

The audience, composed of 274 adults, represented the professions, state college professors, students, and a sprinkling of Catholic "intellectuals." Many requests

came asking me to clarify the doctrine of grace. Complaints read: "Why don't you explain your theory of grace?" "We don't understand the Catholic teaching on grace."

LOVING SOLICITUDE 

After treating the subject from different angles, with no success, and invoking the Holy Spirit, the thought came to me to make an entirely new approach. To this end, the account of the Prodigal Son, with its heartwarming ideal of paternal mercy, gave me the cue. When this parable, verse by verse, was unfolded to my class how the father never lost interest in the son, how his heart, in sympathy and understanding followed the ungrateful boy some progress seemed indicated. Although the text does not mention it, surely the father poured out his soul in fervent supplication to God that his son might someday return. Every morning and evening the father scanned the horizon, the hills and the valleys, hoping to get a glimpse of his wayward boy.

One morning, he looked down the valley and lo, the outline of the returning prodigal loomed before him. He not only forgave the errant son, but honored him by putting a ring on his finger and a robe on his back and sandals on his feet. He called in his friends and provided a feast to make merry. But let St. Luke describe what happened: "So I say to you, there shall be joy before the angels of God upon one sinner doing penance. And He said: 'A certain man had two

sons. And the younger of them said to his father: "Father, give me the portion of substance that falleth to me." And he divided unto them his substance. And not many days after, the younger son, gathering all together, went abroad into a far country; and there wasted his substance, living riotously. And after he had spent all, there came a mighty famine in that country; and he began to be in want. And he went and cleaved to one of the citizens of that country. And he sent him into his farm to feed swine. And he would fain have filled his belly with the husks the swine did eat: and no man gave unto him. "And returning to himself, he said: "How many hired servants in my father's house abound with bread, and I here perish with hunger? I will arise, and will go to my father, and say to him: 'Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee. I am not worthy to be called thy son; make me as one of thy hired servants.'" And rising up he came to his father. And when he was yet a great way off, his father saw him, and was moved with compassion, and running to him fell upon his neck, and kissed him. And the son said to him: "Father, I have sinned against heaven, and before thee. I am not now worthy to be called thy son." And the father said to his servants: "Bring forth quickly the first robe, and put it on him; and put a ring on his hand, and shoes on his feet.

And bring hither the fatted calf, and kill it, and let us eat and make merry; because this my son was dead, and is come to life again, was lost, and is found." And they began to be merry. ""Now his elder son was in the field; and when he came and drew nigh to the house, he heard music and dancing. And he called one of the servants, and asked what these things meant. 

And he said to him: "Thy brother is come, and thy father hath killed the fatted calf, because he hath received him safe." And he was angry and would not go in. His father therefore coming out began to entreat him. 

And he answering said to his father: "Behold, for so many years do I serve thee, and I have never transgressed thy commandment; and yet thou hast never given me a kid to make merry with my friends. But as soon as this thy son is come, who hath devoured his substance with harlots, thou hast killed for him the fatted calf." "But he said to him: "Son, thou art always with me; and all I have is thine." But it was fit that we should make merry and be glad, for this thy brother was dead, and is come to life again; he was lost and is found.'" (St. Luke 15:10-32) 

Note how the enlightening grace of the Holy Spirit penetrated the darkest recesses of the boy's soul.

The above text portrays, by allegory, the rejoicing in heaven upon one sinner doing penance. With this beautiful parable deeply impressed on our minds, may we conclude that grace is the loving solicitude of our heav enly Father. Further requests for clarification by all attending the Dogma Seminar terminated. Whether or not rigorous theologians would approve this definition, the fact remains: it solved problems and answered the questions of inquirers.

THE THEOLOGICAL VIRTUES 

While we can acquire certain virtues by spiritual direction and self-discipline, God must dispose us to perform works that are in themselves supernaturally good. 

Such works are performed through the supernatural virtues. Chief among these are faith, hope and charity.

These virtues are called theological because they relate to 

God. We believe in God; we hope in Him; we love Him.

These three theological virtues are infused into our souls at the reception of the sacrament of Baptism, at which time the Holy Ghost takes up His abode within us. 

There are four other virtues and accompanying graces infused into the soul at baptism: namely, the cardinal virtues of prudence, justice, fortitude, and temperance. As their names indicate, they govern our relationship to God as it concerns our conduct towards our neighbor. These four moral virtues are called "cardinal" because all other moral virtues hinge on and derive from these four. The word "cardinal" derives from the Latin "cardo," which means a hinge.

We can accurately say that grace is an interior, supernatural gift of God bestowed on us through the merits of Jesus Christ for our salvation. We do not merit such gifts as graces. Jesus Christ, our divine Redeemer, merited these graces for us.

Chapter XXXV OUR LADY OF GUADALUPE 

"He hath not done in like manner to every nation." 

DE EVOTION to Our Lady under the title of Gua dalupe started at dawn, December 9, 1531, on

Tepeyac Hill, some twelve miles distant from Mexico City. The story has been told around the world for centuries. It continues to inspire and uplift souls. Time cannot dim the luster of divine grace channeled through the Immaculate Heart of Mary. 

Juan Diego, an Indian convert, en route to Holy Mass in the village of Tlaltelolco, passed the foot of Tepeyac Hill. Rays of an unearthly light shone from the summit. 

Strains of exquisite music brought the lone traveler to a standstill. Then a sweet voice of loving compassion invited Juan Diego to mount the hill. There he saw the 

Blessed Virgin Mary, enveloped by brilliant light.

In Juan's Indian tongue, Our Lady spoke reassuringly to him. She clarified for him doctrines that he had not understood. To this humble Indian she confided heaven's plan that a shrine should be built on the spot, to the end that her maternal interest and loving care be known to the masses of people. "In order that my may be fulfilled," she added, "you must go to the palace wish 338

OUR LADY OF GUADALUPE 

339 of the Bishop of Mexico, and tell him that I sent you; that it is my desire to have a church built for me here." 

The bishop, at first unimpressed by the poor Indian's account, deferred action. In the manner of apparitions, or what seems miraculous, the Church exercises the utmost prudence and reserve. She takes under advisement even apparently reliable accounts of the supernatural. 

Juan Diego returned to Tepeyac Hill, where our Blessed Lady awaited the account of his failure. She bade him return to the bishop and restate his mission. The bishop requested some proof of the Virgin Mother's apparition. Juan reported this to Our Lady, who promised a sign on the following morning. The critical illness of 

Juan Diego's uncle, Juan Bernardino, prevented him from keeping this appointment with the Blessed Virgin. 

When, on December 12, 1531, Juan Diego hurried to the rectory of Tlaltelolco to get a priest for his dying uncle, the Blessed Virgin met him below the hill of Tepeyac. 

She heard with kindly understanding Juan's excuse for failing to come the day before.

UNDER HER PROTECTION 

Our Lady's sympathetic response reassures us all. "It is well, littlest of my sons; only listen to what I tell you now. Do not let anything afflict you and do not be afraid of any illness or accident or pain. Am I not here who am your mother? Are you not under my shadow and protection? Do you need anything else? Your uncle will not die; be sure he is now well." Whereupon Juan

Diego, comforted and encouraged, begged a proof to

satisfy the bishop's demand. Although flowers never bloomed here during the winter, Our Lady directed Juan Diego to cut an armful of roses from bushes he would find blooming on a rocky, barren hillside. She arranged these flowers in Juan's mantle and directed him to take them to the bishop.

Bishop Juan de Zumárraga received the Immaculata's radiant messenger and learned from him of Our Lady's fourth apparition. Unfolding his mantle, Juan Diego disclosed the precious flowers. What further amazed the bishop and his curia was the Virgin Mother's image in exquisite colors, painted on the Indian's tilma. 

Although the material of the mantle on which the delicate and colorful image of Our Lady appears, is handwoven from cactus fibers calculated to last not more than twenty years, it has remained intact during the past four centuries. This tilma, resplendent with her lovely image, now occupies the central panel above the tabernacle on the main altar of Our Lady of Guadalupe's basilica in Tepeyac. 

"SHE WHO CRUSHED THE SERPENT" 

Our Lady of Guadalupe also appeared to Juan Bernardino, uncle of Juan Diego, and to him she made known her wish to be called "Coatlaxope," a Nahuatl (Aztec) word which means "She who crushed the serpent": 

Coat from "coatl," meaning "serpent" la - relative pronoun "(she) who"

OUR LADY OF GUADALUPE 

341 xope- past perfect tense of the verb "xopeua," "to crush with the foot"*

When Bishop Zumárraga and his curia heard the Indian say the word in his habitual language, they concluded, from the resemblance of the sound, that the Spanish word "Guadalupe" was meant.

Regardless of their many languages and dialects, the ancient tribes of Mexico discerned the message of this marvelous portrait. Devotion to Our Lady of Guada lupe spread throughout the world, especially in the Latin- American countries. Her benign image, as miraculously portrayed on Juan Diego's tilma, has thrilled the Catholic world and has intensified its faith. In the brief span of seven years following her apparition, eight million

Indians renounced their paganism to embrace the Catholic faith.

More than four centuries of filial devotion have culminated in the proclamation of Our Lady of Guadalupe as Empress of all America. Chapels and churches erected in her honor dot the hills and valleys and the long desert stretches of the world. Her sweet influence, under this significant title, penetrates the hardest hearts. The very name of Our Lady of Guadalupe has converted millions, many on their deathbed. *From Nican Mopohua (Ten Years After), a book on the apparitions of Our Lady of Guadalupe, which took place ten years after the conquest of Mexico by the Spanish in 1521. Written by a native Aztec 

Indian, Antonio Valeriano, translated into Spanish by Primo Feliciano

Velázquez, it was published by Lasso de la Vega. This book rates as an authority on the subject.

Today the Virgin Mother of Guadalupe is the heart and soul of Mexico. Her love for mankind is reflected in the gentle, refined and noble-minded Mexican people.

MESSAGE OF LOVE 

Her affectionate words touch the human heart: "My dear son, whom I love tenderly, I wish you to know that

I am the Virgin Mary, Mother of the true God, Giver and Maintainer of life, Creator of all things, Lord of heaven and earth, who is in all places. It is my ardent wish that a church be erected to me here, where I can manifest the compassion that I have for the natives, and for ALL who solicit my help in their work and their affliction; where I shall see their tears and listen to their pleadings, to give them comfort and ease."

INFINITE MERCY 

Every human being, by prayer and fidelity to God's laws, can erect a temple in his heart to our beloved Lady of Guadalupe. "Wonderful is the Lord on high." Even when men in their confused thinking try to frustrate His perfect designs, the boundless mercy of our heavenly Father "reaches from end to end mightily" (Wisdom 8:1).

The insults of the creature hurled at his Creator only intensify the mercy of God. 

Ponder well and be grateful for the goodness and kindness of God our Saviour, so beautifully portrayed in

Our Lady of Guadalupe. The life, sufferings and death of Jesus Christ, Son of God, established the reign of divine mercy above all His works. The Blessed Virgin

OUR LADY OF GUADALUPE 

343 Mary, chosen by our heavenly Father to conceive and give birth to Jesus, is the sacred Ark of the mysteries of the Incarnation. She taught the Child Jesus and also learned from Him. His loving compassion reacted on the heart of the mother who watched over Him so tenderly and became the counterpart of His mercy. Our Lord adorned 

His Virgin Mother with the dazzling splendor of mercy, and we have reason to salute her gratefully as "Mother of mercy." 

From the depths of grateful hearts we think of our

Blessed Lady as Co-redemptrix of the Human Race. 

Having provided the Victim for the Sacrifice, the Queen of Martyrs endured the agonies of His Passion and death with Him, especially anguish of mind and heart. 

"REFUGE OF SINNERS" 

The first to "take up her Cross and follow Him," the 

Mother of Sorrows suffered valiantly and with sweetest resignation, because the crucifixion of her divine Son was offered for the redemption of mankind. Our Blessed

Lady for she is ours when standing at the foot of the Cross became the mother of mankind and, therefore, the refuge of sinners. 

God's divine wisdom "orders all things sweetly" (Wisdom 8:1). When He elected the Blessed Virgin 

Mary to enfold mankind with His tender love, she invoked the Holy Spirit to touch the hearts of sinners.

Thus men and women, adorned with grace, have the urge to help their neighbors, because charity is not static it must reach out to others.

PRAYER TO OUR LADY OF GUADAlupe 

Dearest Lady of Guadalupe, fruitful Mother of holiness, teach me thy ways of gentleness and strength. A lowly suppliant at thy Shrine of Tepeyac, I plead: Hear my humble prayer, offered with deep-felt confidence to beg this favor: 

O Mary, conceived without sin, I come to thy throne of grace to share the fervent devotion of thy faithful 

Mexican children who call to thee under the glorious title of "Guadalupe," which we love to repeat in its Aztec form: "She who crushed the serpent."

Queen of Martyrs, whose Immaculate Heart was pierced by seven swords of grief, help me to walk valiantly amid the sharp thorns strewn across my pathway. 

Invoke the Holy Spirit of wisdom to fortify my will that I may frequent the sacraments; thus enlightened and nourished, may I prefer God to all creatures and shun every occasion of sin.

Help me, a living branch of the Vine that is Jesus Christ, to exemplify His divine charity, always seeking the good of others. Queen of Apostles, aid me to win souls for the Sacred Heart of my Saviour. Keep my apostolate fearless, dynamic, and articulate, to proclaim the loving solicitude of our Father in Heaven, that the wayward may heed His pleading and obtain pardon, through the merits of thy merciful Son, our Lord Jesus 

Christ. Amen. (The above prayer written in her basilica, Tepeyac, Mexico, October 12, 1957, expresses the deep-felt gratitude and fervent love of the author for the constant protection of Our Lady of Guadalupe.)

Chapter XXXVI  

O DEFINE the subject in a brief popular style, To subject brief prayer is thinking of God and speaking to Him. 

Let us hark back to the definition we learned in childhood: "Prayer is the lifting up of our minds and hearts to God to adore Him, to thank Him for His benefits, to ask His forgiveness, and to beg of Him all the graces we need, whether for soul or body." 

The four objectives of prayer: thanksgiving, pardon, petition, and adoration, conform to the four reasons why our Saviour suffered and died on the Cross. The Holy 

Mass, then, is the greatest of all prayers, because we unite with Jesus in the offering He makes daily to His heavenly Father in every adorable Sacrifice. The Mass, therefore, is divine, and the sacraments are the chief means of salvation. To pray, then, is necessary, as we learn from the revealed word of God: "Watch ye, and pray" (St. Matthew 26:41). The first commandment of God demands prayer, because this is the normal channel whereby man expresses his love and adoration for God. Moreover, prayer corresponds to our Saviour's plan for granting to us an abundance of divine gifts: “Ask, and it shall be given to you" (St. Matthew 7:7).

While we are all distracted in prayer, external acts help us to pray with attention, devotion, humility, confidence and perseverance. The very attitude of kneeling in our 345

supplications bespeaks humility and acknowledgment of our dependence on God and our reliance on His promises. The folding of the hands denotes reverence and childlike helplessness. When striking our breast three times during the Confiteor - "through my fault" — we express our sense of guilt. Signs and symbols direct and hold our attention. When Jesus exhorted us "always to pray" (St. Luke 18:1), He had in mind a habit of prayer, at stated intervals and the morning offering which sanctifies and makes meritorious every act of the day, including actions in themselves indifferent.

For our guidance the Church commends the Our Father; the Hail Mary; the Apostles' Creed; acts of faith, hope, love, and contrition. Aspirations are brief acts of love which are very effective because they can be easily said without distraction.

MENTAL PRAYER 

Another salutary form of prayer is meditation, in which we ponder truths of our holy religion. Thinking promotes conviction. 

The Holy Spirit warns us: "With desolation is all the land made desolate, because there is none that considereth in the heart." (Jeremias 12:11)

We might learn truths, and even memorize them; but meditation helps us to realize them.

This form of silent prayer consists of an introduction or prelude and composition of place, the recalling that

we are in the presence of God. This is followed by one or more points of some truth to be mentally analyzed. 

The conclusion, called a colloquy, expresses acts of love, resolutions of amendment, intimate converse with our divine Creator. A saint once described meditation as "preaching to oneself."

FIRST INTENTION 

When you begin a prayer with the right intention, then, even though your mind wanders, that intention carries through, so that the entire prayer is good. Notwithstanding involuntary distractions, the first good thought or word perseveres through to the end of the prayer. 

THE SIGN OF THE CROSS 

When our divine Lord founded His Church over nineteen hundred years ago, printing had not yet been invented. Hence the truths of religion had to be taught by word of mouth, preaching, by signs and symbols.

The unlettered grasped quickly this method. They understood the Sign of the Cross, which was more than a symbol; it was a profession of faith that embraced many doctrines. 

On awaking in the morning let us make our first thought and act of the day the Sign of the Cross. "In the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy 

Spirit. Amen."

Say the words slowly. The depth of our devotion while reciting a prayer means more than the length of

the prayer. Remember that faith is an act of the intellect and the will not blind emotion. 

In making the Sign of the Cross, you touch the forehead to indicate that you recognize God; you accept God with your mind. From the mere reasoning of cause and effect, as a rational creature, you conclude that God exists. 

The created world around us is an effect. It demands a mighty, all-powerful, intelligent cause. 

You touch the breast to show that you love God with your heart. You touch each shoulder to signify willingness to take up your cross and follow Christ.

The Sign of the Cross, packed with meaning, is an open profession of faith. Make it reverently. 

In this sacramental, you express your belief in the unity, in the Trinity of God; also by the gesture of forming the Cross you recall to mind the Incarnation, the sufferings and death of Jesus for the redemption of mankind.

THOUGHTS ON THE OUR FATHER 

It will grip our attention and intensify our devotion if we reflect for a moment after each phrase as we recite the Our Father. 

Our Father: He does not demand that you address 

Him as the Almighty Creator of heaven and earth - but as "Our Father," with all the tender solicitude and paternal love the word implies. He is your loving Father, because He created you and sustains you. In baptism you were born again to a new life of grace. The worthy reception of the sacraments increases this grace- - fortifies you with perseverance and security.

Our Father in heaven has sent us His divine Son. 

From both Father and Son proceeds the Holy Spirit of love, to enlighten, guide and strengthen us along the way. Our Father in heaven watches over us with tender solicitude and understanding. All of us, His children, are united to Him as branches to the Vine. Our Father expects us to love one another and to live in harmony. 

Who art in heaven: "Who art" means an eternal now — every moment of our gladness or of sorrow, of suffering in youth, in old age. 

"In heaven" denotes His power and unveiled majesty the Sovereign Good. "In heaven" - the true home our Father has prepared for us—the reward for being faithful over a few things. "In heaven" - thither our hearts should tend and yearn to possess Him and to be possessed by Him. 

Hallowed be Thy name: Holy, reverenced, respected be Thy name the Infant Jesus loved to lisp this holy name. The victorious angels chanting their battle cry, "Who is like to God?" defeated the rebel angels who would not serve. Note how the Blessed Mother Mary announced her obedience to the divine will: "Behold the handmaid of the Lord" (St. Luke 1:38). Often pronounce the holy name in reverence, never in vain.

Thy Kingdom come means the right ordering of all things in my life so as to glorify God; the extension of 

God's kingdom on earth by the propagation of the faith. 

Pray for our zealous missionaries at home and abroad.

Thy will be done on earth as it is in heaven: Thy will, infinitely perfect, which knows what is best for me. I should never hesitate to accept Thy will because Thy judgments are just. The angels and saints in heaven rejoice in doing God's will. 

Give us this day our daily bread: Give us actual and sanctifying graces. Give us - for we are poor and needy - food for the body, food for the soul, right knowledge for the mind. This day, because, "Lord, for tomorrow and its needs, I do not pray; keep me, my God, from stain of sin just for today" (Sibyl F. Partridge- 

Sister Mary Xavier). Today, because in this world of shadows we cannot claim tomorrow. 

And forgive us our trespasses: Because, dearest Lord, "in many things we all offend" (St. James 3:2), we must beg Your kind pardon. Forgive us our mortal sins the sins and ignorance of our youth; forgive us our venial sins even our little infidelities, when we played fast and loose with Your paternal love. If only our repentance comes from the heart, if we are humble and sincere, our Father will condone us. "O God, be merciful to me, a sinner." 

As we forgive those who trespass against us: Dare we say "forgive us" according to the measure by which we forgive: (a) harsh words hurled at us; (b) the mean insinuations that dug deep; (c) the jealous spite of others;

(d) the shallow reasoning of our critics; (e) the minor faults of those who "get on our nerves"?

Even if our hearts have been crushed by those near and dear give us the grace to be big enough to forgive.

Our heavenly Father, who has sent the Holy Spirit to enlighten us, expects us to be noble-minded, great of soul, never petty, or bitter. 

And lead us not into temptation: Even though You have endowed us with the gift of free will, never permit us to deceive ourselves by listening to Satan's cunning and insidious persuasion. Shield us from being led by our weak selves along devious pathways, by evil companions, or by false maxims. If temptation should assail us in some weak spot, let us turn promptly to You in prayer and "make also with temptation issue" (1 Corinthians 10:13) by saying, "My Jesus, mercy.' 

But deliver us from evil: From the evil of proud selfsufficiency; self-deceit; from the evil of lust that outsmarts the wise and tricks the unwary. Amen - So be it. 

THOUGHTS ON THE HAIL MARY 

The first part of this prayer was composed in heaven. 

Hail Mary: The name "Mary" signifies "the beautiful one." "Thou art all fair, O My love, and there is not a spot in thee" (Canticle of Canticles 4:7). Beautiful to the Blessed Trinity because she is heaven's masterpiece. 

Beautiful to the choirs of angels because "full of grace."

Full of grace: Destined to bring forth the Author of all grace, Mary corresponded in every way with the inspiration of divine grace and made the best use of God's precious gifts-perfect correspondence to the will of 

God in all things. 

The Lord is with thee: The Blessed Trinity is with thee, anticipating thy consent to become the Mother of our divine Redeemer, even though this entails the acute suffering symbolized by seven swords piercing thy Immaculate Heart. 

Blessed art thou among women: Blessed by God. 

Blessed, indeed, art thou in thy Immaculate Conception, preparing thee in a marvelous way for the virginal motherhood of Jesus, for whom thy pure body provided His first tabernacle. Mankind, too, through all the centuries has fulfilled thy prophecy and calls thee blessed in every generation. Among women: Hitherto, women had been toys and playthings for the caprice of men. 

Note the contrast wrought through Mary, who ennobled women, made them respected and reverenced. 

And blessed is the fruit of thy womb: When Mary came to visit her cousin, St. Elizabeth repeated the angelic salutation, "Blessed is the fruit of thy womb."

This blessed Fruit is the Christ Child, the beautiful Gift of the triune God. Blessed divinely because Jesus comes from the eternal Father to us, for our salvation. 

Holy Mary, Mother of God...: This conclusion of the Hail Mary, added by the Church, invokes Mary's loving maternal interest in all of us children, sinners and

ungrateful though we be. It begs her affectionate and powerful intercession, "now and at the hour of our death." Thou, who hast looked on death, obtain for us the grace we need when death is nigh. O Mary, stand very near us in that supremely important last moment of life.

Chapter XXXVII CHRISTIAN VIRTUES 

VIRT IRTUE has two classifications-theological and moral. These are fundamental and the fonts of many subdivisions. Briefly, virtue can be defined as a uniform quality or habit which inclines one to do the will of God consistently. One good act does not make a man virtuous. Inasmuch as there are natural and supernatural good acts, so there are corresponding natural and supernatural virtues.

The theological virtues, as the term implies, refer to God and consist of faith, hope and charity. We believe in God because He is truth. We hope in God with firm reliance on His divine promises and because of His infinite goodness. We adore God because adoration is love in action. These three divine virtues, being identified with God, have Him for their immediate object and motive. In a word, we love God for His own sake. Failure to guard and cherish the divine virtues reacts in causing defection from our religion.

The moral virtues which cultivate good conduct center on the morality of an act and have God for their final objective.

THE CARDINAL VIRTUES 

The moral virtues, also called cardinal, are: (a) Prudence-by which we discern what is good, and therefore pleasing to God. 354

CHRISTIAN VIRTUES 

355 

(b) Justice which directs us to give everyone his due. (c) Temperance - which controls and restrains carnal desires. (d) Fortitude-which enables us to promptly and valiantly trample on all temptation, to develop stability of character, to be articulate in defense of truth. "(Wisdom) teaches temperance and prudence and justice and fortitude, which are such things as men can have nothing more profitable in life.” (Wisdom 8:7)

While the moral virtues are not necessarily related, still it becomes evident that no one moral virtue can stand up perfectly unless the other three are present. If we succeed, with the aid of divine grace, in developing self-control, then the moral virtues can take deep roots. 

Under each cardinal virtue, St. Thomas Aquinas shows a sub-classification, of subordinate virtues which serve to develop or foster the cardinal virtues themselves. 

Thus he teaches that deliberation, judiciousness and discretion subserve prudence. Justice embraces religion, piety, reverence, prudence, truthfulness, gratitude, friendship—all of which regulate our duties to God and man. 

Temperance includes continence, sobriety, chastity, modesty. 

To fortitude belong magnanimity, constancy, patience, perseverance.

Other classifications point out the virtues that pertain to the intellect (e. g., prudence); and those that pertain to the will (e. g., fortitude). With reference to their origin, there are infused virtues (divine gifts accompanying sanctifying grace) and acquired virtues (which are obtained with God's help, by constant practice). 

Keep in mind that the duty of knowing God, loving and serving Him in this life—in fact, the objective of your reading and pondering the doctrines set forth in this book is preparation for the future life, the Beatific Vision in heaven. The Christian life, then, is primarily an unremitting preparation. The three theological virtues of faith, hope and charity point the way— they assist us to attain this supernatural end. Our motive for believing is the highest and most certain-namely, the truthfulness of God, who cannot deceive nor be deceived.

One seeking the true faith must be willing to accept it on God's terms. 

Even though faith requires understanding — since knowledge and assent stem from the intellect - nevertheless, we depend on the will to move the mind to accept the object — i. e., all the truths revealed by God. However, neither intellect nor will suffices. Genuine progress in the practice of virtue requires the grace of God. Hence faith is the virtue by which we firmly assent, on divine authority, to all the truths revealed by God. St. Paul writes: "Faith is the substance of things to be hoped for, the evidence of things that appear not." (Hebrews 11:1)

CHRISTIAN VIRTUES 

357 "Faith... cometh by hearing, and hearing by the word of Christ." (Romans 10:17)

Faith is not like the answer to a problem in mathematics. Faith is not merely knowledge which results from the grasp of truth in the object itself. The difference lies in the fact that faith demands the authority of another. 

God has revealed all that the Catholic Church proposes for our belief. Our acceptance of all that the 

Church teaches is motivated by our perfect trust in the Holy Spirit, who was promised by Christ and sent by God to guide His vicar on earth to teach all truth. 

Loyal Catholics, worthy of the name, accept all those truths or articles of faith that God has revealed and that the Church proposes for our belief. 

FAITH NECESSARY FOR SALVATION 

To believe the truths revealed by God is a divine command and also a necessary means of salvation. For example, you do not believe that two plus two makes four. You know that from intellectual perception. But you do not know from reason the existence of the Blessed Trinity. This article of faith you accept on the testimony of Jesus Christ, Son of God. It is true that the subject matter of knowledge may also be the subject matter of faith-e. g., the existence of God. Habitual faith is a divinely infused virtue whereby we assent to all that God has revealed. In view of the fact that Christ commissioned the Apostles and their successors to teach the

Gospel to every living creature, all have the corresponding obligation to actually and explicitly accept it as the word of God. St. Paul taught that without faith it is impossible to please 

God. For he that cometh to God MUST BE 

LIEVE that He is; and is a rewarder to them that seek Him." (Hebrews 11:6)

The rational man depends on faith to know his last end, which is supernatural, and to know how to attain it. The word "faith" is to be taken in its strict sense- namely, the assent of the mind on the authority of God. Because 

God wills "all men to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth" (1 Timothy 2:4), He gives to all, including pagans, heathens, and the enemies of the Church, sufficient grace to enable them to find the true faith and to embrace it. God offers this grace to every rational creature, but He does not force it on anyone. Our own free will must co-operate with the gifts of our divine Benefactor. Faith is active, universal, firm and vitalized. 

It extends to all revealed truths. "Not everyone that saith to Me, 'Lord, Lord,' shall enter into the kingdom of heaven; but he that doth the will of My Father who is in. heaven, he shall enter into the kingdom of heaven." (St. Matthew 7:21)

Active, living faith is efficacious when associated with good works, such as the performance of religious duties

CHRISTIAN VIRTUES 

359 and acts of charity, which reflect the inspiration of the 

Holy Spirit. "For even as the body without the spirit is dead; so also faith without works is dead." (St. James 2:26)

In fact, faith would weaken without some alerting influence. To intensify faith, one must exercise it. This is accomplished by fervent prayer that expresses confidence in God.

Christians have the duty to strive for at least the perfection that results from keeping the commandments of God and the precepts of the Church. Souls endowed with special graces from God follow the evangelical counsels of poverty, chastity and obedience. They reach higher and higher forms of moral beauty. "Be you therefore perfect, as also your heavenly Father is perfect." (St. Matthew 5:48)

A Christian will either advance in virtue or deteriorate. There is no standing still in this warfare of life. 

We have the stirring example and the divine assurance that those who courageously follow Christ find the yoke sweet and the burden light. "And He said to all: 'If any man will come after Me, let him deny himself and take up his cross daily and follow Me.'' (St. Luke 9:23)

HOPE 

Hope relates to the future and rests on confidence in the goodness of God, His omnipotence and faithfulness.

It is a divinely infused virtue which inspires us to have complete trust in the promises of Christ. Hope looks to the everlasting happiness of heaven and to divine help to attain it. 

Sins against hope are despair and presumption. Diffidence which doubts and despair which abandons hope reveal a lack of trust in God. Despair fails to reckon with the infinite mercy of our lovingly solicitous heavenly Father, who is more anxious to forgive us our sins than we are to be forgiven. 

Presumption sins by a false conscience which imposes on the goodness and mercy of God. The malice of this sin consists in frustrating the grace of God bestowed for our salvation.

CHARITY 

Charity is a divine virtue infused by the Holy Spirit who teaches us to place the highest value on God (love of appreciation) because of His infinite attributes, and to love our neighbor as ourselves (love of benevolence) for the sake of God. 

The love of benevolence ("bene volens" - wishing well) prompts us to hold all rational creatures in respect and affection because they are children of God. The love of benevolence, when mutual, is friendship. The infinite perfections of God motivate us to love Him above all creatures.

CHRISTIAN VIRTUES 

361 

Our love for God reaches perfection when we love

Him not only because He loved us first and lavished us with divine gifts, but for His own sake. Imperfect charity, although related to the divine perfection, has for its objective benefits in this life and the reward of heaven. 

Mortal sin, being in effect a rejection of God, offends charity, because it prefers creatures to the Creator. 

Mortal sin drives out of the heart the Holy Spirit of charity. Man has the solemn obligation to perform acts of faith, hope and charity. Failure to do so is a sin of omission. The prayer called the Morning Offering provides the supernatural motive for all acts of the day and renders good and meritorious acts that are in themselves indifferent.

WHAT DID GOD SAY?

Chapter XXXVIII INTRODUCTION TO THE COMMANDMENTS 

UR divine Lord made it very clear that

OUR 

"Not everyone that saith to Me, 'Lord, Lord,' ...but he that doth the will of My Father who is in heaven, he shall enter into the kingdom of heaven." (St. Matthew 7:21)

The will of God is definitely expressed in the ten commandments. In the darkest intervals of history, pagans living in the jungle were aware of the evil of certain acts. They knew this from the natural law written on their hearts. Why, then, did God give Moses ten commandments engraved on two stones; three on one stone, outlining our duties to God; seven commandments on the other stone, listing our duties to our fellow man?

Because sin, through the course of centuries, had so dimmed men's minds to right and wrong that the divine Legislator found it necessary, through Moses, and later through the teaching of Jesus, to re-emphasize and clarify these laws.

BACKGROUND 

In reality, both state and ecclesiastical laws stem from the law of God as applied to our exterior actions. A sincere inquirer might well pose the question: "How is the 365

unlettered man to determine the difference between wrong and right?" Conscience serves as his guide. It belongs to the integrity of nature. But to remove every vestige of doubt, God made His wishes explicit by giving mankind the essentials of right living, proclaimed from Mount Sinai.

During His mission on earth Christ stressed the importance of the commandments of God and promised a reward in this life for their faithful observance. 

GOOD OR BAD 

Human conduct is good or bad according to its conformity or difformity to the divine will. In a word, the rational creature, possessing free will, may fulfill or violate the commandments of God. "If thou wilt enter into life, keep the commandments." (St. Matthew 19:17)

The goal as specified for human beings is eternal happiness. The attainment of this end depends on how faithfully we keep the commandments.

God has made known to mankind both natural and positive law; these are also respectively called objective and subjective. The natural law written on the human heart may be termed the light of reason, pointing out good and evil. This is also known as subjective morality. 

St. Paul writes of the heathens, that they "are a law to themselves" and "show the work of the law written in their hearts, their conscience bearing witness to them"

INTRODUCTION TO THE COMMANDMENTS 

367 (Romans 2:14, 15). These heathens, then, could reason out what was in accordance with the will of God and what opposed it.

While the history of nations confirms these principles of morality, their application differs widely. The positive law of God depends on revelation; moreover, what natural law commands may also be revealede. g., keep holy the Sabbath. In fact, all of the ten commandments, which refer to man's duties to God, to his neighbor, and to himself, in their content include natural law; but the way they become known to us, through revelation, places them also in the positive law. 

What can become known to us by our conscience, which in reality is the voice of God, may be called subjective morality. What has been revealed, however, and what the Church commands, constitute objective morality. 

Atheists, rationalists, and other unbelievers reject objective morality altogether. They falsely contend that no act is wrong unless it opposes their standards of morality. 

By virtue of both his reason and his will, man freely exercises human acts, which may be good or bad. 

THE MORNING OFFERING 

Actions which fulfill the divine will as expressed in the commandments are morally good. Actions which violate God's will are morally bad. In some of his acts, man's conduct may be neither good nor bad, but indifferent. The Morning Offering in which we dedicate to 

God all the thoughts and acts of the day renders indifferent actions good and meritorious. Indifferent acts in

themselves can become good or evil if the doer performs them with a good or bad intention.

There is a positive and a negative side — a "do" and a "don't" to every commandment. A negative law admits no exception; that is, it binds our actions at all timesfor example, "Thou shalt not steal." In His goodness, mercy and love, God gave us the ten commandments to serve as directives or signposts along the highway of life. 

To increase our knowledge and efficiency, we use our intellects to study the regulations provided for our protection. We cannot ignore the danger signals if we wish to reach the goal. Actual graces the inspiration to avoid evil and do good-enlighten us. Conscience guides us when we are enveloped by the darkness. In a word, our Chief Benefactor and Sovereign Good has left nothing undone to lead us to heaven.

Chapter XXXIX  

"I am the Lord thy God, who brought thee out of the land of Egypt, out of the house of bondage. Thou shalt not have strange gods before Me. Thou shalt not make to thyself a graven thing, nor the likeness of any thing that is in heaven above, or in the earth beneath, nor of those things that are in the waters under the earth. Thou shalt not adore them, nor serve them.... 

(Exodus 20:2-5) "And hope confoundeth not: because the charity of God is poured forth in our hearts, by the Holy Ghost who is given to us." HE (Romans 5:5) 

ERE it may be helpful to our readers to define three different kinds of love. 

(1) LOVE OF CONCUPISCENCE is a self-interested love one that is based on the good that accrues to the lover. The word "concupiscence" stems from two Latin words ("con" and "cupere"), which mean to desire ardently. 

(2) Love of BENEVOLENCE ("bene volens"). This is a higher level of love which inspires us to wish well 369

to all mankind. We should pray for and desire good to everyone, our enemies included. Neither scoundrels nor hypocrites, not even our worst critics, may be excluded from our love of benevolence. When the first commandment bids us love God above all things and our neighbor as ourselves, the little word "as" refers not to the quantity but to the quality of love. The love we have for ourselves is the love of benevolence; in a word, we wish ourselves and everyone else well. 

(3) The highest form of love is LOVE OF APPRE- 

CIATION. The word "appreciation" comes from the Latin "appretiare"-to place a value on someone or something; to appraise. This is the kind of love we should have for God. We place the highest value on Him. So much so that He has the first place in our life—to the point that we would not offend God to please ourselves or any creature. We can be heartened by what Almighty 

God said to Abram: "Fear not, Abram; I am thy protector and thy reward exceeding great" (Genesis 15:1).

In His mission on earth, Jesus explained the commandments. Our Lord taught that, on the first and second commandments that is, on love of God and man "dependeth the whole law and the prophets" (St. Matthew 22:40); because all the other commandments refer directly or indirectly to love of God, love of self, and love of neighbor. We prove our love for God by keeping 

His commandments. "If you love Me, keep My commandments" (St. John 14:15). Love of self suggests a pattern for love of our neighbor. The word "neighbor" includes friends, strangers, enemies, everyone. While the

love of enemies does not require association with them, it does demand that we wish them well, pray for them, and do them no harm in word or in deed. We could not pretend to be followers of Christ and leave our neighbor in dire need. We must divide the loaf, even at great personal inconvenience. The first commandment stresses our fundamental obligation to know, love and serve God. 

It also brings into focus the three theological virtues of faith, hope and charity. They are termed "theological” because they refer to God, "supernatural" because they transcend our human nature.

THE PURPOSE OF LIFE 

They also point to our final objective -supreme happiness. Faith in the true God excludes false worship and gives to our divine Creator the homage due Him. By offering an eternal reward, God inspires the virtue of hope through the merits of Jesus Christ. By the virtue of charity God teaches man his obligation to love the Sovereign Good. Adoration, obedience to His laws, are, in reality, love of God in action.

Faith is the assent of the mind to all that God has revealed. Hope is the full reliance upon God's promise of reward, merited by Jesus Christ. Love puts God first in every consideration of our lives. It fosters appreciation for our divine Creator, our Chief Benefactor and our 

Everlasting Reward. 

Faith requires two things: a rational mind and the proper disposition to accept the truths which God has revealed. Once these two are present, faith enters the

soul and registers there, just as material objects register on the eye. A well-balanced education enables us to realize more and more the tremendous love of God for man. 

His love pursues man even when man has rejected Him by mortal sin. God does not will sin, but permits it because

He has endowed man with free will, "Before man is life and death, good and evil: that which he shall choose shall be given him" (Ecclesiasticus 15:18). God knows how to turn evil into good. To glorify His Father in heaven and to awaken faith in the people, our Saviour drove the devil out of a man possessed. Now freed from this terrifying experience, the man "went his way and began to publish in Decapolis how great things Jesus had done for him; and all men wondered" (St. Mark 5:20). 

CREATIVE LOVE 

God loved us first and chose us to be rational creatures "a little less than the angels." Our soul was not born but was created individually by God and sent to inform this clay element. He has every hair of our head numbered. He assures us that the mother will not forget her child; but even if she should, He will not forget us. "Know ye that the Lord He is God: He made us, and not we ourselves. We are His people and the sheep of His pasture." (Psalm 99:3) 

Life and all our faculties of mind and body come from God. Good health, vision, power of speech, artistic talents, craftsmanship, are natural gifts, because they belong to the framework of human nature. The word

"grace" means a supernatural gift of God-above and beyond the natural. 

Just as children owe their parents love, respect, obedience, so we owe the same to God. We must make acts of love such as: "My God, I love Thee!" God rules the mineral, the vegetable and the animal divisions of the world by fixed laws. Instinct and appetite govern brute animals. God would rule man by love. Rational man is expected to respond to God's inspiration to shun evil and do good. If the rational creature earnestly and daily invokes the Holy Spirit for enlightenment, guidance and courage, he will fulfill his duties to his Creator.

THE MYSTERY OF SUFFERING 

If God loves us so intensely, why does He permit us to suffer? God permits trials and pain so that we may learn to take up our cross and follow Him valiantly; often sinners are converted by suffering and disappointment. The good may sanctify their trials by offering them up with the sufferings of Christ.

Who can fathom the inscrutable designs of God, whose compassion, wisdom and goodness enfold and strengthen us, no matter how heavy the cross we are asked to carry? His grace is sufficient for us. In bearing up patiently under trial we gain heroic stature, "Gladly therefore will I glory in my infirmities, that the power of Christ may dwell in me." (2 Corinthians 12:9)

Firm belief in God's existence and in the rewards He has promised are necessary for salvation. The doctrines of the Blessed Trinity and the Incarnation of Jesus Christ; the Apostles' Creed, the decalogue, the chief precepts of the Church; and the sacraments may also be included as explicit articles of faith. For a dying man who requests baptism, belief in God and His promise to reward the faithful suffice.

DEFECTIONS 

We sin against faith by: (1) Infidelity, or refusal to accept the truths of faith. (2) Heresy, or the rejection of an article of faith, or holding as true an error condemned by the Church. 

If the heresy results from invincible ignorance, it may not be culpable. It is sinful to stubbornly persist in a false doctrine without any effort to clarify the related conclusion. (3) Apostasy, or the abandonment of the Church and her teaching authority by one who has been baptized. 

While no one is bound to proclaim the faith on every occasion, there are times when Christians have the solemn obligation to do so. "For, with the heart, we believe unto justice; but, with the mouth, confession is made unto salvation." (Romans 10:10)

This duty binds us whenever the honor of God is at stake, or if our own spiritual good or that of our neighbor re-

quires it. Neglecting to practice our religion for any considerable time is a sin against the virtue of faith. 

ILLOGICAL 

(4) Indifferentism, or the error of asserting that it makes no difference what religion you practice "so long as you are good."

Indifferentism further holds that all churches are true and pleasing to God; that man may accept or reject any or all creeds.

Those who lean on this kind of weak sophistry fail to reason logically. A little reflection would compel them to admit that the more than four hundred and ninetynine false religions all contradict one another. Contradictory beliefs cannot all be true at the same time. Moreover, common experience shows that those who pretend that it makes no difference what you believe, also live the false doctrine that it makes no difference what you do. 

The expression "so long as you are good" also falls apart when one considers that Jesus did not organize religion for good people only, but to help people become good and to stay good.

SINS AGAINST HOPE AND CHARITY 

The goodness of God, the fact that He loves us, should inspire hope. The objective of hope embraces all that God has promised us: adequate grace in this life, and the guidance of the Holy Spirit to obtain eternal happiness.

(1) Despair is the loss of hope in the mercy of God.

In effect, it denies the goodness of God and/or His fidelity to keep His promises. (2) Presumption, taking salvation for granted, sins against hope because it implies that we expect to obtain eternal happiness without applying the means necessary. 

To offend against charity is also a sin against the first commandment. Included among the sins against charity are hatred of God and of man, envy, sloth, scandal, and bad example.

HONORING THE SAINTS 

We do not worship the Blessed Virgin Mary, the Mother of God, nor the saints. We do beg their help because they are close to God in heaven. Intelligent readers can distinguish between adoration and veneration. 

Catholics do not worship statues or pictures. We do honor images of the Blessed Virgin and the saints, not in themselves but inasmuch as they represent. In the same way, you honor a picture of your mother and father. The difference between adoration, homage paid to God alone, and veneration, the honoring of the saints, is evident in the Litanies. In addressing God we say: "Lord have mercy on us," but to the saints we say: "Holy Mary (St. 

John), pray for us." 

All forms of idolatry, worshiping false gods or adoration of statues and pictures are sins against the first commandment. Catholics are forbidden to consult Christian Science pseudo-healers, because such an action recognizes a false cult and fosters sinful superstition. The

wearing of medals to honor our Lord or the saints is not a superstitious practice. Catholics do not ascribe any power or charm to the medals which serve to remind them of God and His saints, or to the medals or insignia worn to designate membership in a parochial or other Catholic society. As with a photo of one's parents, it is not the value of the reproduction itself but the fact that it represents our very dear ones that makes us respect and cherish it. We honor statues, pictures and medals portraying the saints inasmuch as they represent. 

Those, on the contrary, who wear medals as charms or good-luck amulets sin against the first commandment. 

In the same category are those guilty of sacrilege misuse of sacred persons, places or things; simony, bartering the spiritual for material gain — for example, selling indulgences for money. Those also commit sin who believe in dreams, consult fortunetellers, or participate in séances with or without the presence of a medium. In these the evil consists in attributing to creatures perfections and powers which belong to God alone. Those who make use of spells and such like are for the same reason guilty of sin.

Spiritists deceive ignorant people by pretending to communicate with the dead. Mediums assume themselves to be the mouthpieces or messengers of spirits. Fortunetellers hoodwink the simple-minded by reading palms, horoscopes, and the stars. Their claims to knowing the future are spurious.

To indulge in these and other superstitious practices reveals a lack of confidence in God, who sustains us and watches over us with a Father's loving solicitude.

Chapter XL THE SECOND COMMANDMENT 

"Thou shalt not take the name of the Lord thy 

God in vain." (Exodus 20:7)

BY 

OY THE light of his mind, the intelligent man concludes that respect and reverence are due the holy name of God. In ancient times people did not dare to pronounce the name of the Deity. They spoke of God as "He who is," which means the essence of being. Jesus, in answer to a request from one of the Apostles: "Lord, teach us to pray" (St. Luke 11:1), composed the Our

Father. Thus He gave us the consoling privilege of thinking of Him and praying to Him as a lovingly solicitous Father.

The second commandment, in a positive way, requires due reverence for the name of God and whatever may be related to Him. It forbids: (a) The use of God's name in vain mere byword. (b) Blasphemy. that is, as a 

Blasphemy (from the Greek "blasphemia"-evilspeaking) means addressing God or referring to Him in insulting language. Blasphemy includes disrespectful reference to anyone or anything dedicated to God, such as 378

THE SECOND COMMANDMENT 

379 the Blessed Virgin Mary, the angels, the saints, heaven, the cross or the sacraments. (c) False, rash or unnecessary oaths.

By an oath we call upon God to vouch for the truth of our statement or to witness the sincerity of a promise. 

When sufficient reason and absolute veracity are present, an oath is meritorious. The oath demanded by social clubs is classed as unnecessary and sinful. When secret societies impose the oath of secrecy on members, binding them to keep whatever secrets are. entrusted to them, such an oath is unjust and intrinsically evil. Should a member of a secret society be entrusted with a secret concerning a plot that trespasses against private or public rights, or a plot against the state—if he actually puts God and country before the obligations imposed by a secret society, he would be obliged to divulge such a plot. With good and sufficient reasons the Catholic Church forbids its members to affiliate with certain secret societies, such as the Freemasons, Oddfellows, Knights of Pythias, and the Sons of Temperance. Some of these societies claim to be a religion. Catholics cannot belong to two religions. In fact, Catholics who join a group that in itself constitutes a religious body are not only attempting to belong to two religions but to two different faiths whose beliefs are directly contradictory to each other. 

An oath founded on falsehood, or deception, or one intended to deceive, is perjury — a mortal sin. An oath extracted under threat or duress, or to support something unjust, has no binding force. Thus Catholics who join other religions or secret societies and take an oath of

stability are not bound by such a promise. To disregard a lawful promise made under oath is also a grave sin against the second commandment. The welfare and security of society depend on respect for an oath administered by the Church or the State. Public officials who violate an oath required by civil authority commit a grave sin. Again, one who freely swears to keep an important secret which involves no harm to an individual or to the state, has a solemn duty to respect this contract. (d) The breaking of a vow. 

Also under sins against the second commandment comes the breaking of a vow, a grave violation that in some cases constitutes sacrilege. A vow may be defined as a deliberate and firm promise to God to do something pleasing to Him. It must be made without threat or promise of reward, and the person making it binds himself under pain of sin to do the thing vowed. St. Thomas teaches that the same work done with a vow is better and more meritorious than when done without a vow. 

In Holy Scripture we read: 

"... it is much better not to vow than after a vow not to perform the things promised.' 

(Ecclesiastes 5:4)

Chapter XLI THE THIRD COMMANDMENT 

"Remember that thou keep holy the sabbath day." (Exodus 20:8)

ABBATH (from the Hebrew "shabbath") means a day of rest. In the Old Law, God decreed that the last day in the week should be set apart for rest from manual labor, and He further commanded that man sanctify the day. This was to be accomplished by special worship of God, including prayers and acts of charity. In memory of Christ's Resurrection on Sunday, and because the Holy Ghost descended on Pentecost Sunday, the Apostles changed the day of worship and of rest from 

Saturday to Sunday, which is now observed as the Lord's day.

St. Peter and the Apostles had the authority from 

Christ to change the Lord's day from Saturday to Sunday. "For the Son of man is Lord even of the Sabbath" (St. 

Matthew 12:8).

SANCTIFY THE LORD'S DAY 

In the new dispensation, the Church obliges, under penalty of sin, men, women and children who have reached the age of seven, to worship God by offering Holy Mass with the priest on Sunday. In commanding us to observe Sunday with becoming reverence, the Church 381

recommends the worthy reception of Holy Communion and the performance of spiritual and corporal works of mercy and other good works. Rest and innocent recreation, reading and other intellectual pursuits, afford relaxation on Sundays, and hence are permissible. Illness, the urgent care of the sick, and other pressing demands of charity may excuse one from hearing Mass on Sundays and holydays of obligation.

On the negative side, the proper observance of Sunday excludes manual labor and commercial activities. 

Loss of faith follows the desecration of Sunday. All unnecessary servile work is forbidden, but necessary household duties, work required for the common good (such as the disposal of perishable goods), or work necessary for earning one's livelihood, are permitted. For example, the Sunday-morning opening of a store selling milk to accommodate people in hot climates, where poverty precludes refrigeration in the homes, would be allowed. 

The work of maids, cooks, waitresses, of pharmacists, and drug-store clerks is necessary and so is not forbidden. 

Art crafts, painting, embroidering, typing, playing music, recreational trips by car or boat, are not servile work even though they may require considerable exertion. 

Gardening could be a hobby; on the other hand, neighbors might take scandal at seeing such an occupation on Sundays, not knowing of the hobby angle. Manual labor does not include such physical exercise as swimming, diving, tennis, and playing handball.

The precept which obliges the faithful to Sunday Mass also applies to holydays of obligation which, in the

THE THIRD COMMANDMENT 

383 

United States, are: Christmas, the octave day of the Nativity (New Year's Day), Ascension Thursday, All Saints, the Immaculate Conception and the Assumption of the Blessed Virgin Mary. These solemn festivals emphasize some of the principal truths of our holy faith. 

The first history of the Church, the Acts of the Apostles, confirms the practice of the early Christians' fulfilling their Sunday obligation: "And on the first day of the week, when we were assembled to break bread, Paul discoursed with them.... 

(Acts of the Apostles 20:7) 

St. Justin Martyr, who lived during the second century, writes in detail about the Sunday Mass with its prayers, instruction and the reading of the Bible from the 

Old and New Testaments.

Tertullian, who lived from approximately 160 to 230 

A. D., refers to avoiding servile work: .. deferring even our business on the Lord's day, lest we give place to the devil." 

(De Orat., XXIII) 

The Holy Mass has three principal parts: the Offertory, the Consecration and the Communion. To satisfy the Sunday precept one must be present for all three parts, and know that Mass is being celebrated. A person asleep during the Mass would fail in his duty. People going to confession and mothers attending their children in adjacent lounges, are morally present, and thus fulfill their obligation.

Chapter XLII THE FOURTH COMMANDMENT 

"Honor thy father and thy mother, that thou mayest be long-lived upon the land which the 

Lord thy God will give thee."

TH (Exodus 20:12) 

HE fourth commandment introduces us to the seven laws engraved on the second stone tablet-which God proclaimed on Mount Sinai and entrusted to Moses to make known to the people. This one particular directive promises a temporal reward of long life for those found faithful to it. Those who fail in their obligation to their parents may expect severe punishment: "Cursed be he that honoreth not his father and mother. And all the people shall say: 'Amen.'" (Deuteronomy 27:16) 

DUTY TO PARENTS 

The word "honor," used in the fourth commandment, includes (1) love, which in turn expresses appreciation, because next to God we owe everything to our parents. 

To be genuine, this filial love strives to exclude all that could grieve them. The word "honor" also implies (2) reverence, and respect for the position of our parents, our superiors by nature. If parents need material support, 384

THE FOURTH COMMANDMENT 

385 their children must not fail in this solemn duty — even if it means deferring a vocation to the priesthood or religious life. In a word, our parents have priority. "Honor thy father, in work and word, and all patience." 

(Ecclesiasticus 3:9) 

(3) "Honor" also includes obedience as long as one lives under the parental roof. This directive requires due regard for the parents' wishes and observance of the regulations made for the good name and security of their home. Mistakes or human weaknesses on the part of the parents do not lessen one's duties to them. 

Children are not held to obey their parents in any matter that involves sin, nor in their choice of a vocation or a life companion. In these matters, ordinarily the advice of the parents should be sought, but one is not strictly obliged to follow it. 

IN PATIENCE AND UNDERSTANDING 

Let the Holy Spirit of wisdom confirm the teaching that parents are to be respected and aided: "Son, support the old age of thy father: and grieve him not in his life; and if his understanding fail, have patience with him, and despise him not when thou art in thy strength: for the relieving of the father shall not be forgotten.... And in justice thou shalt be built up, and in the day of affliction thou shalt

be remembered: and thy sins shall melt away as the ice in the fair warm weather." (Ecclesiasticus 3:14-15, 17)

On the negative side, to strike one's parents is a mortal sin: "He that striketh his father or mother, shall be put to death." (Exodus 21:15) 

Also, children who curse their parents sin grievously: "He that curseth his father, or mother, shall die the death." (Exodus 21:17)

Under this heading comes any insolence to one's parents or refusal to help them in need.

Parents on their part accept the responsibility to edify, nourish and educate their children—and to provide for their spiritual welfare.

CIVIC DUTY 

In 1925 the Supreme Court of the United States handed down a verdict in the Oregon case that the parents, not the State, have the right to select a school for their children and to direct their education.
