The family, which existed before the State, enjoys certain rights and prerogatives. In civil matters, however, citizens have obligations to the State. They must share the burdens of the government in its effort to provide security for life,

THE FOURTH COMMANDMENT 

387 liberty and the pursuit of happiness. Duty to country demands that citizens express their loyalty and patriotism by voting for qualified candidates in public elections. It is also the duty of citizens to defend their country when necessary.

LOYAL SUPPORT 

The fourth commandment includes the obligation of respect, obedience and support of our ecclesiastical superiors, who are to be considered our spiritual fathers in 

Christ. St. Paul teaches: "Obey your prelates and be subject to them.

For they watch as being to render an account of your souls." (Hebrews 13:17)

Bishops and pastors have serious obligations to the people entrusted to their spiritual care. The ordinary of the diocese, the pastor of each parish, offer Mass on Sundays, and on certain other occasions, for the faithful within their jurisdictions. These religious leaders must teach their flocks the truths revealed by Jesus Christ; they will also make certain to facilitate the worthy reception of the sacraments and to provide opportunity for the faithful to assist at Holy Mass.

MATER ET MAGISTRA 

The duty of the worker to be loyal, trustworthy, honest and respectful to his employer comes under the fourth commandment. Management has the corresponding duty

to provide sanitary working conditions for all those employed, to pay a just wage sufficient for the worker to gradually acquire a home, to enjoy some of life's comforts, and to educate his children.

EFFECTIVE DISTRIBUTION 

"It is not enough to assert the natural character of the right to private property, including productive property, but also the effective distribution among all social classes is to be insisted on" (John XXIII, Mater et Magistra, May 15, 1961).

Chapter XLIII THE FIFTH COMMANDMENT 

IN "Thou shalt not kill." (Exodus 20:13) 

N A positive way, God commands us in the fifth commandment to use ordinary care in the preservation of our body, and to have regard for our spiritual welfare. 

By virtue of creation and the Redemption, we belong to God and we serve Him as trustees of our earthly career, so that, among other things, an account of our stewardship regarding the use and abuse of our health must be rendered.

This commandment also enjoins us to seek the spiritual and physical welfare of our neighbor. All forms of cruelty, mental or physical, and all unnecessary mutilations offend this law. The fifth commandment likewise forbids cruelty to animals.

To clarify the obligation set forth in this commandment in regard to our bodily well-being: We must practice temperance both in eating and drinking, in getting the proper rest, and in providing ordinary care of the body. 

This, in turn, entails consultation of a doctor in illness as well as following competent medical direction, including the prescribed remedies. Moralists hold that ordinary care of health would demand the undergoing of minor surgery when indicated. We are not bound to extraor- 389

dinary care of the body, as reflected in some types of major surgery.

WILLFUL MURDER 

The fifth commandment forbids willful murder, suicide, injury to or mutilation of another person, cruelty, either mental or physical, provoking another to anger, and whatever could injure another to the point of shortening his life. In short, an injury to body or soul violates this commandment. St. Paul clarifies these points: "The works of the flesh are manifest: which are: . . . enmities, contentions, . . . wraths, quarrels..., murders, drunkenness, revellings and such like. Of the which I foretell you, as I have foretold to you, that they who do such things shall not obtain the kingdom of God." (Galatians 5:19, 20, 21) 

Children should refrain from whatever would grieve their parents or bring them to an early grave. Ill-will, outbursts of anger and uncontrolled temper are forbidden because they prompt us to harm others.

We are also forbidden to expose our lives to harm, mutilation, or unnecessary dangers. This should not be construed as a denial of self-protection - it is lawful to repel force with force. The force with which we repel, however, must not be greater than that used against us. 

For example, if a burglar enters a home brandishing a revolver, he may be disarmed, even by the extreme measure of disabling him with a revolver (or, preferably

THE FIFTH COMMANDMENT 

391 knocking him senseless with a club if time permits), because a burglar or anyone with evil intent menacing with a deadly weapon has forfeited his right to live. In selfdefense one may not exceed what becomes necessary for protection. For example, it would be wrong to kill an assailant if self-defense could be had by wounding him. 

A woman may kill to defend herself against criminal attack. Life or valuable property may be defended against enemies, even if killing them is indicated. Mobs and unauthorized persons have no right to destroy life. Lynching is murder. Duelling is evil because it amounts to suicide and murder combined. War may not be waged except as a last resort. Priests, doctors, nurses who risk their health and lives to rescue others from physical or spiritual death may expect a great reward. "He that findeth his life shall lose it: and he that shall lose his life for Me shall find it.” (St. Matthew 10:39)

INTRINSICALLY Evil 

The use of contraceptives, in themselves intrinsically evil and calculated to frustrate the laws of nature, is absolutely forbidden. Moreover, contraceptive devices often result in septic poisoning and death. This is nature's protest against interference with the natural law. Also forbidden is the taking of pills or tablets with the bad intention of preventing conception. When the motivation is evil, the act following is evil. Medicine prescribed by a reputable physician to correct a pathological condition and not to frustrate conception or to injure the offspring, may

be taken. Doubts or problems on this subject should be submitted to one's confessor. 

Under the heading of the fifth commandment, moralists place bad example and scandal. Both do harm to our neighbor's soul. 

Bad example, wrongdoing in the presence of others, causes scandal. St. Augustine shows the connection between scandal and the fifth commandment: "If you persuade your neighbor to sin, you are his murderer." 

Our divine Lord emphasized the gravity of scandal: "Woe to that man by whom the scandal cometh. And if thy hand or thy foot scandalize thee, cut it off and cast it from thee. It is better for thee to go into life maimed or lame than, having two hands or two feet, to be cast into everlasting fire." (St. Matthew 18:7-8)

The following concrete examples are sources of bad example and scandal: risqué talk or double-meaning references; circulating pornographic books, magazines, pictures; immodest dress; singing impure or suggestive songs; intoxication; blasphemy; character-assassination; open defiance of superiors, civil or ecclesiastical; unjust criticism of priests and sisters—often the cause of weakening the faith of those, especially children, who are exposed to it; writing against religious or Church authorities; public violation of any of the ten commandments.

Chapter XLIV THE SIXTH COMMANDMENT 

IN "Thou shalt not commit adultery." (Exodus 20:14)

NA positive way we are directed by our Almighty Creator to be pure in thought, word and deed. Modesty, a proper reserve, one of the fruits of the Holy Spirit, guards innocence. Custody of the eyes, consistent rejection of evil books, papers, magazines, lewd and suggestive stories win sufficient grace for man to rise above sins of the flesh. Violations of God's laws, which are the laws of nature, corrupt the mind and heart. If not controlled, they weaken one's moral perception. God detests these sins and has left on record the example of the severe punishments meted out to Sodom and Gomorrha. Prayer and frequenting the sacraments build up self-restraint and vigilance in guarding the holy virtue of purity. "Or know you not that your members are the temple of the Holy Ghost, who is in you, whom you have from God: and you are not your own? For you are bought with a great price. Glorify and bear God in your body.” (1 Corinthians 6:19-20) 

In a negative way, the sixth commandment forbids loose conduct, undue familiarity, impure acts performed 393

alone or with another, and adultery. It prohibits any type of impure looks or actions, whether by ourselves or with others.

The sixth commandment also prohibits the reading of bad books, indecent magazines and newspapers, as also the telling of or the encouraging of risqué stories, even "in fun." The pure-minded exclude all these occasions of sin in order to "keep their souls unspotted from this world." "The body is not for fornication, but for the 

Lord: and the Lord for the body." (1 Corinthians 6:13) 

The most powerful tower of strength to protect young and old from sins against this commandment is loving devotion to the Blessed Virgin Mary. The prompt recitation of the Hail Mary, aspirations of love to Jesus and

Mary, a prayer for humility, can put to flight a legion of tempters. We cannot, however, trifle with danger. Those who play with fire can expect to get burned. "He that loveth danger shall perish in it." (Ecclesiasticus 3:27) 

Sources of danger to the preservation of purity are excesses in eating and drinking, idleness, sinful curiosity, bad companions, immodest dress, indecent plays, undue familiarity, books that feed the mind with lust, and pornography in all forms.

THE SIXTH COMMANDMENT 

395 In all these matters, let us follow divinely inspired teaching: 

"But fornication and all uncleanness or covetousness, let it not so much as be named among you, as becometh saints." 

(Ephesians 5:3)

Chapter XLV THE SEVENTH COMMANDMENT 

"Thou shalt not steal." (Exodus 20:15)

TEALING is the unjust taking of what belongs to another, the owner being reasonably unwilling to let it go. 

Violation of the seventh commandment includes robbery (theft accompanied by violence—that is, a threat to do personal injury with a deadly weapon), charging usurious interest and/or exorbitant prices, selling adulterated foods, demanding excessive fees, defrauding laborers of a just wage, failure to pay debts, contracting obligations beyond one's ability to pay, unlawful retention of valuable found articles, purchase of or receiving property known to be stolen, vandalism (when performed by children, often part of the responsibility devolves on parents who have been lax in their duties), damage done to another's business or property, circumventing contracts, employing false weights and measures, insidious techniques of double-dealing, cheating and deception; taking unfair advantage of any situation. To the above we may add the acquisition of all or part of an estate contrary to the expressed wish of the testator, even though the settlement has been approved by a civilcourt decision. Under the seventh commandment also 396

THE SEVENTH COMMANDMENT 

397 comes the obligation on the part of employees to do an honest day's work, wasting neither time nor materials; to take reasonable care of the machinery and tools entrusted to them; to put into each assignment their best efforts, excluding all slipshod or defective workmanship. "The sinner shall borrow, and not pay: but the just showeth mercy and shall give. 

(Psalm 36:21) 

The seventh commandment recognizes man's right to possess property. Related to the subject are the moral virtues of prudence, justice, temperance, and fortitude. 

Moral conduct and a sense of fair play hinge on these cardinal virtues. A virtue is a stable disposition, a wellgrounded habit which influences a person to do right. 

You have heard the expression: "He can be counted on for a square deal. He always plays the game on the level." The Bible refers to St. Joseph as "a just man."

He had all the virtues crystallized in justice. 

THE KEY VIRTUE 

In every human being a key virtue or a key vice sways conduct. In St. Joseph the key virtue was obedience. He did not question the wisdom or prudence of orders but obeyed them. Purity distinguished St. Aloysius Gonzaga, humility another saint. The history of mankind records great benefactors of the human race whose holy and useful lives have edified and uplifted their fellow men. In each saint, however, one virtue has dominated and has most influenced his or her daily conduct. The same prin-

ciple may be applied to vice. Often one unconquered vice opens the door to other sins.

THE HINGE 

The exercise of the cardinal virtues builds up a sturdy moral structure. Prudence tempers the intellect. Justice guides the will to demand equity in all things. Temperance regulates and controls the appetite. Fortitude inspires courage.

THE KINDS OF JUSTICE 

Justice, one of the four cardinal virtues, disposes the will of man to render to everyone his due. Moralists divide this virtue into three classifications:

Distributive justice regards the obligation of rulers to assign burdens and rights equally among their subjects. 

Legal justice demands that both rulers and subjects unite for the common good of all. 

Commutative justice, which is what is generally understood when we speak of "justice," imposes the duty on each individual to be honest in every transaction. 

Recall the ancient expression: "Fiat justitia — Let justice be done." The popular formula put into practice by many people who have never been briefed on Christian ethics is the "Golden Rule," which appropriately comes under the category of commutative justice: "Do unto others as you would have them do to you." The statue of a blindfolded woman holding in her hands a pair of perfectly balanced scales personifies justice. The blindfold symbolizes impartiality.

THE SEVENTH COMMANDMENT 

399 When summoned before the Divine Tribunal, every individual must render an account of his stewardship. "Take heed and beware of all covetousness: for a man's life doth not consist in the abundance of things which he possesseth." (St. Luke 12:15) 

To clarify the right of private ownership, let us note that a right is defined as an inviolable moral claim belonging to one person or a family which both the civil power and all citizens are obliged to recognize and respect.

NO ABSOLUTE OWNERSHIP 

The temporal goods that God bestows on a man are not his by absolute ownership, but only for his use. He may not squander or wantonly destroy them, as this would menace public security. What is superfluous to the owner should benefit the poor.

The poor have a claim upon us. To preclude starvation, we must divide the loaf. One sins gravely by turning a deaf ear to the agonized cries of the suffering destitute. "If thou have much give abundantly: if thou have little, take care even so to bestow willingly a little." (Tobias 4:9)

The right of private ownership stems from the nature of man. "And it came to pass after many days, that

Cain offered, of the fruits of the earth, gifts to the Lord. Abel also offered of the firstlings of his flock" (Genesis 4:3-4). Hence we know that Abel owned a flock, while his brother Cain owned a field. To steal or unlawfully retain the property of another offends justice.

The gravity of moral guilt depends on the value of the stolen property, or the damage inflicted on another. The theft of a small amount from a very poor person is a grave matter.

RESTITUTION 

Justice demands reparation for injury inflicted on another. Even at great personal sacrifice, we must, to the best of our ability, repair the damage we have done. 

There can be no remission of sin for him who refuses to make restitution. Property stolen should be restored to its rightful owner; or, if he is dead, to his heirs. If these cannot be located after a diligent search, the ill-gotten goods are to be given to the poor or to some charity. Restitution may be made secretly or through a third party. 

Deep regret and even sincere repentance do not remove injustice. “A thing cries out for its rightful owner.” "The beginning of a good way is to do justice; and this is more acceptable with God than to offer sacrifices.... Better is a little with justice, than great revenues with iniquity.” (Proverbs 16:5, 8) 

Restitution may not be delayed. Injury done to another not only by theft and double-dealing, but by vio-

THE SEVENTH COMMANDMENT 

401 lence - i. e., killing, wounding, disabling a person so that he cannot earn a living-must be repaired. The same holds true for those who conspire in crimes of theft or robbery.

If the injury done or the property stolen constitutes grave matter, the sin is mortal. If one steals small amounts, intending thereby to accumulate a large sum, he commits a mortal sin.

In determining the real guilt, circumstances can aggravate the case-for example, depriving a worker of tools or of the opportunity to earn a day's wages is a serious sin. One who robs a church adds sacrilege to theft. "If any man hurt a field or a vineyard, and put in his beast to feed upon that which is other men's: he shall restore the best of whatsoever he hath in his own field, or in his vineyard, according to the estimation of the damage. 

If a fire breaking out light upon thorns, and catch stacks of corn, or corn standing in the fields, he that kindled the fire shall make good the loss." (Exodus 22:5-6) 

Moralists rate as absolutely serious matter in every instance the theft of what is equivalent to the weekly wages of a worker. In view of dollar-depreciation, in the 

United States the amount of $60.00 constitutes grave matter in any theft.

The ten commandments of God and all related precepts constitute the norms of objective morality. Note how exacting was the Old Law: "If any man steal an ox or a sheep and kill or sell it: he shall restore five oxen for one ox, and four sheep for one sheep.' 

(Exodus 22:1) 

It should be added that found articles, as opposed to stolen articles, may be kept by the finder if the lawful owner cannot be located.

Chapter XLVI THE EIGHTH COMMANDMENT 

"Thou shalt not bear false witness against thy neighbor." (Exodus 20:16) 

HUS we are exhorted to speak the truth.

Lying and TH all forms of deception offend God and oppose truth, which consists in conformity of fact. The positive side of this mandate demands the truth. It includes respect for another's good name and the favorable reputation which he holds.

Keep in mind that the power of expression is one of the most ennobling gifts God has vouchsafed to rational creatures. To use this gift to do harm, to express what we know to be false, abuses the divine endowment and disfigures man, who is made to the image and likeness of God. Man's speech should conform to his thoughts. 

To faithfully observe this law, to ring true, man must be a lover of truth, charitable in thought and word at all times. Lying is never permitted, even for a good purpose. 

The end does not justify the means. "I am the Way, and the Truth, and the Life." (St. John 14:6) "You shall not lie.” (Leviticus 19:11) 403

Prohibited by the eighth commandment also are calumny, detraction, insult, violation of secrecy, rash judgments, unfounded and insidious double talk, and deceit in every form.

A lie is an assertion which we know or suspect to be false, made in order to deceive. "Lying lips are an abomination to the Lord." (Proverbs 12:22) 

Parents, guardians, and superiors have a right to the truth from their subjects.

We are not bound to reveal the facts to everyone. 

The civil courts recognize what is called privileged information. This safeguards the professional secrets of priests, doctors, lawyers, and nurses. Those addicted to telling lies and "cutting the corners" soon forfeit the trust and esteem of others. "A thief is better than a man that is always lying: but both of them shall inherit destruction." (Ecclesiasticus 20:27) 

Calumny falsifies, injuring our neighbor's reputation. 

Detraction unjustly reveals the faults of another and lowers esteem for him. Calumny and detraction violate justice, because everyone has a right to his good name. "A good name is better than great riches, and good favor is above silver and gold." (Proverbs 22:1)

THE EIGHTH COMMANDMENT 

405 

Calumny, lying, detraction, are mortal sins if they seriously damage the reputation of another. To repair the injury inflicted in calumny, retraction must be made.

In case of detraction, something good must be said of the person detracted, in an effort to counterbalance the injury done to his good name. Gossipmongers who directly or indirectly injure the good name of others are guilty before God. It does not negate the malice if gossipers try to excuse themselves by asserting: "I don't know anything about them, but this is the tale that came to me.' 

There are exceptions to the law forbidding us to reveal the sins of another. (a) When an innocent person is falsely accused of a crime, he may expose the real criminal. (b) In a court of justice, civic welfare demands that we affirm or swear to the truth as we know it, when we are questioned (except in the case of privileged information). (c) When the good of society is in jeopardy, it may be obligatory to reveal to authority the grave wrongdoing of another person. 

Note that a priest may not break the seal of confession even for the good of society or to save his own life. 

Flattery and hypocrisy violate the eighth commandment. "A man that speaketh to his friend with flattering and dissembling words spreadeth a net for his feet.” 

(Proverbs 29:5)

"Woe to you that call evil good, and good evil; that put darkness for light, and light for darkness.' 

(Isaias 5:20)

Violation of a secret entrusted to one on condition of secrecy, or of a secret which we have promised to keep, can be seriously sinful according to the damage inflicted by divulging the secret.

Evasion may be employed to withhold facts from those who have no right to question. If those in authority demand an answer to what they have a right to know, evasion is sinful.

Chapter XLVII THE NINTH COMMANDMENT 

"Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's wife."

GO (Deuteronomy 5:21) 

OD commands His rational creatures, made "a little less than the angels," to preserve purity in thought and desire. "You have heard that it was said to them of old: "Thou shalt not commit adultery.' But

say to you that whoever looks on a woman to lust after her hath already committed adultery in his heart." (St. Matthew 5:27-28)

Impure thoughts, imaginations, desires for another's wife or husband, willfully indulged in, are grave sins. 

Our Creator has, in the sacraments of Penance and the Holy Eucharist, lavished on us the sacramental safeguards of innocence. He has a right to expect us to exemplify courage and put up a fight under trial and temptation. We battle bad thoughts and desires with prayer, prompt and sincere. We should not argue with filth of any kind. You don't have to wade through slime to know that it is dirty. The practice of frequenting the sacraments of confession and Holy Communion builds up a strong moral character, ready to overcome temptations. 407

To clarify doubts that may come, be assured that temptations to improper thoughts accompanied by motions of the flesh are not in themselves sinful; they are only so when we willfully consent to them. Should a doubt arise as to whether or not we gave consent, we should make a sincere act of contrition. It is true, however, that sins against the sixth and ninth commandments do not admit parvity of matter. Assuming that we commit them deliberately, with sufficient reflection, and full consent of the will, they are all mortal. Custody of the eyes, conservative reading, and total rejection of suggestive stories and/or pictures shut out the occasions of evil thoughts and desires.

In the matter of impure desires, partial consent or even a tardy rejection may be a venial sin. But the alert

Christian determined to serve God will school himself to avoid both the occasion or near occasion of sin. "Blessed are the clean of heart: for they shall see God." (St. Matthew 5:8)

Chapter XLVIII THE TENTH COMMANDMENT 

"Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's goods." (Cf. Deuteronomy 5:21)

HIS injunction demands charity in action—that is, TH in thought and deed - greatness of mind and heart, rejoicing in the success of others. It forbids us to covet unjustly what belongs to others. Efforts to increase our material possessions are lawful, assuming that all forms of double-dealing have been excluded. The tenth commandment prohibits in thought what the seventh commandment forbids in deed.

The tenth commandment permits us to strive by all fair means to increase our financial income. We may lawfully desire the property of others if we plan to acquire it by just means. Heads of families have the duty to make prudent provisions for the education of their children. They must budget to take care of future emergencies, such as illness or loss of employment, and should provide for the advancing years when they will be unable to work.

TRANSGRESSIONS 

The malice of covetousness centers around the desire to obtain the property of others unjustly. (b) Children sin against this mandate when they wish the death of parents in order to fall heir to their property. 409

(c) Those who, directly or indirectly, promote social disorder or various hardships with the intention of profiting thereby; and those who denounce or try to interfere with the right to private ownership, also sin against this commandment.

COMMUNISM 

Communism condemns itself by attempting to destroy human rights, not only in its confiscation of private property but in its enslavement of rational creatures who have been endowed by their divine Creator with intellect and free will. The Catholic Church, ever since the warning of Pope Pius IX in 1846, has consistently condemned the errors of communism and has shown it to be materialistic and anti-Christian. The Church keenly discerned the evils of communist teaching and forbade Catholics to embrace it, because it was found to be "absolutely contrary to the natural law itself, ... an infamous doctrine, which if adopted could utterly destroy the rights, property, and possessions of all men and even society itself" (Pope 

Pius IX, Qui Pluribus, 1846).

That Red leaders should persecute the Church is understandable. Much of the conflict and discord in the world today can be traced to communism and its atheistic drivel. Industrial problems have never been solved by empty promises from the teachers of a false doctrine.

Pope Leo XIII in his encyclical Rerum novarum (1891); 

Pope Pius XI in the encyclicals Quadragesimo anno (1931) and Caritate Christi compulsi (1932); and Pope 

John XXIII in Mater et Magistra (1961), have taught

THE TENTH COMMANDMENT 

411 explicitly the norms of honesty in economic matters and a just distribution of property, clearly defining the violations that invade fundamental human rights. 

MANAGEMENT AND THE WORKER 

No one can reasonably deny that respect is due both management and the worker. The employer is entitled to a fair return from the money invested and from the talents he applies to promote a successful business. To the employer, the services of the worker are worth not just a bare subsistence but a living wage, which includes some protection for the future. The worker has the right to enjoy some of life's comforts, to receive an income adequate to permit him to live in a manner becoming to the human person. His salary should be sufficient for him to acquire a permanent home and to educate his children. 

The worker may claim not only a just wage but also safe and sanitary working conditions, together with security. The employee's craft or efficiency, derived from education and experience, amounts to his life's investment. Because the rational creature has basic rights, such as the necessary means for sustenance and the opportunity to pursue the purpose of life, he has, logically, the right to private ownership — a divine right by reason of man's nature.

This right of ownership cannot, however, be construed as absolute. The right is limited by the first and second commandments, which set forth man's duties to God and his neighbor. For example, we all have the obligation to "divide the loaf" with the destitute,

SOCIAL JUSTICE 

In order to carry out her divine mission of sanctifying and saving the souls of all mankind, the Church defines and champions the principles of truth and social justice. Fearlessly she teaches truth and applies the laws of God to the solution of economic problems. Pope Leo 

XIII's encyclical Rerum novarum is appropriately called the Magna Charta of the social order a document calculated to preclude practices which are a downright violation of the seventh and tenth commandments. 

To enforce equity and to preserve labor's rights, union organizations are necessary under the current economic system. Abandoned to unethical, grasping men without religion or religious training, the worker would be reduced to poverty and degradation. Therefore, assuming that inherent rights have been ignored or violated, labor unions may call a strike to enforce their contract or to acquire a better living wage. The unions in a strike have the solemn obligation to avoid violence and any other extreme measure that could compromise the reputation of employer or employee. Here the law of Christian charity enters the picture. To love one's neighbor with the required benevolence implies not only respect for his rights but consideration for his needs, both spiritual and material. As children of one heavenly 

Father, we must strive to bear one another's burdens. "And as you would that men should do to you, do you also to them in like manner." (St. Luke 6:31)

THE TENTH COMMANDMENT 

413 Charity obliges us to relieve the suffering and want of our neighbor. We cannot pretend to be followers of 

Christ and yet ignore a fellow man who is reduced to extreme necessity - that is, lacking what he and his family need for the preservation of life. 

The law of charity also obliges us to show solicitude for the spiritual welfare of our neighbor. This means that we must extend fraternal correction and counsel dissuasion from evil when necessary, and that we must avoid giving scandal in any way. Efficacious and helpful to all whom we contact is the silent teaching of good example.

Chapter XLIX THE PROBLEM OF EVIL 

NLY in the world to come will the problem of the ON existence of evil be solved. We are expected to have confidence in the all-wise providence of God.

While making no pretense to solve every possible difficulty, the Church in her office of teacher and guardian of truth condemns the following regarding the mystery of evil: (a) Manichean dualism, which asserts the existence of two principles of equal power, one good and the other evil. (b) The excessive pessimism of Schopenhauer and von Hartmann, which leads to the notion that the world was too evil to have been created by God. (c) The unfounded optimism of Leibnitz. (d) The false teachings of Nietzsche, who viciously attacked Christianity and led many of his followers to suicide. (e) The fanciful theory of Christian Science, which regards evil as an illusion of the "mortal mind." (f) The fabrications of H. G. Wells, who proposed a finite god who could share the burden of evil and thus lighten it. 414

THE PROBLEM OF EVIL 

415 St. Thomas Aquinas defines evil as "the deficiency of some good which ought to be present" (Summa Theologica, II, Q. 59, a. 6). God cannot be the cause of evil, even though He permits it because He will not interfere with the gift of free will bestowed upon His rational creatures. God's wisdom often draws good out of evil. "For who hath known the mind of the Lord? 

Or who hath been His counselor?" (Romans 11:34) 

One consoling fact is certain: "That to them that love God all things work together unto good." (Romans 8:28) 

In the above phrase, "all things," some theologians include even our sins.

Chapter L THE INQUISITION 

HIS book holds to positive doctrine.

It aims to ex- TH plain the fundamentals of the Catholic religion, coordinated in a systematic, intelligible manner. Actually, all the teachings of the Church demonstrate unity of faith. 

While historical discussions do not ordinarily come within the scope of a book like this, in view of the many questions posed regarding the Inquisition, the subject merits special attention. Regrettably, no fact of medieval history has been so distorted and misunderstood. 

The word "Inquisition" (from the Latin "inquisitio" — investigation) refers to a judicial body, a court or tribunal, authorized to investigate charges of heresy. 

A knowledge of the history and characteristics of Europeans of the first twelve Christian centuries is important to the accurate study of the Inquisition. Nations like 

France, England, Spain, and Portugal were entirely Catholic. The masses of the people held their faith tenaciously, cherished it as a gift of God and not as a matter of private judgment. The faithful, therefore, guarded their religion, because it proved to be the best guarantee of their civil rights. Hence they held heresy to be a crime not only against the Church but also against society - an invasion of their personal right to live in peace, security, and the pursuit of happiness. This attitude was not theoretical, because heretics agitated sedition against the government and stirred up hatred much 416

THE INQUISITION 

417 like the anarchy and communism of later periods. Of course, the effort to expose the falsehood of heresy and to suppress it is as old as Christianity. Not merely the 

Church, but the people too, protested vigorously against heretics. In fact, while it was the Church that handed down to posterity the deposit of the truths revealed by Jesus Christ, the Popes and early Fathers condemned all force of violence towards unbelievers. St. Paul considered excommunication of heretics sufficient punishment: "A man that is a heretic, after the first and second admonition, avoid; knowing that he that is such a one is subverted, and sinneth, being condemned by his own judgment." (Titus 3:10-11)

FREE WILL, NOT COMPULSION 

Tertullian, one of the earliest apologists, points out that the natural law authorizes man to follow the law of individual conscience in the practice of religion, since the acceptance of religion is a matter of free will, not of compulsion (Ad Scapulam, II). The Popes and theo logians of the Church took an absolute stand against cruelty and force. In every age they have championed the cause of religious liberty. While on the one hand a man must follow his conscience in religious matters, still, on the other hand he is obliged to seek the objective truth. 

The Church respects the conscience of heretics and repudiates methods of force and coercion, but she does not approve of the errors of heresy.

Two phases of the subject merit careful study: (1) the Inquisition of the Middle Ages; (2) the Spanish

Inquisition, at its height during the sixteenth century. It is the record of history that the civil authority, the imperial successors to Constantine, ambitious for power, encroached on ecclesiastical rights and issued penal edicts against heretics.

To get the true perspective of the Inquisition, one must study the political and religious background of the times, consider the firm faith of the majority and the concordats that existed in various countries between the Catholic Church and the State. The State regarded heresy as treason because it undermined security. Keep in mind that the punishment and, in some cases, the execution of heretics, were meted out by civil authorities, not by the Church, which protested vehemently against the extreme measures with which the heretics were treated. But the civil authorities were influenced by popular indignation. 

The people insisted on the punishment of those convicted of heresy.

It is true that Inquisition boards numbered among their members many Catholic priests and other scholars. 

The personnel of the tribunal was appointed by the Crown. The Church had no control whatever over these judicial groups. Official tribunals investigated charges of heresy, and that was the extent of their jurisdiction. 

They weighed the evidence in a given case. Then the board of Inquisitors, after a careful verifying of the facts, reported its findings to the State authorities, who assessed the punishment and executed it.

THE INQUISITION 

419 

DUAL POWER 

A study of the Inquisition must weigh the fact that many medieval monarchs falsely assumed the role of divine representatives and thus tried to exercise control over the Church. History shows attempts on the part of kings to intrude or trespass on the realm of the Church's authority. Ostensibly this was in order to promote real uniformity but, in fact, the rulers were motivated by political intrigue and strategy.

Even though heretics comprised a small number, they worked in a dangerous underground which threatened the security of the government. A renowned English historian, Reginald Trevor Davies of Oxford, writes of the

Spanish Inquisition: 

"The Inquisition was used by the Crown to achieve secular aims in cases where the ordinary courts were difficult to move, or where the laws and customs of the country were used to place obstacles in the path of the advancing monarchy. The familiars of the Inquisition, exercising ceaseless vigilance in the remotest corners of Spain, may be fittingly compared with the justices of the peace who did so much to uphold the throne of Tudor England. Though frequently popular among 

Spaniards of pure Old Christian descent, the new inquisitorial activities met with strong protest in Aragon, where it was established in 1484. 

"The Inquisitor of Saragossa, Pedro de Arbues 

(1441-85), was murdered — probably at the instigation of rich Marranos (baptized Jews).

Throughout the centuries the chief victims of the Inquisition were men of Morisco or Marrano ancestry. It cannot be denied that the 

Inquisition was guilty of abuses and cruelties in the course of its long history, but it was no more unjust or inhumane than most other courts of the Europe of its day."* 

According to the Protestant historian Leopold von

Ranke, the Popes did not condone the Spanish Inquisition. He writes: "It was in spirit and tendency a political institution. The Pope had an interest in thwarting it and he did so; but the king had an interest in constantly upholding it."**

Historian Davies has made a thorough study of the Spanish Inquisition. He indicates that during the threehundred-year span of the Inquisition, not more than two thousand persons, many of them criminals, were executed. The fact is that no reliable historian shows approval of the Inquisition by the Popes, who constantly employed only the weapons of truth and prayer. The 

Church, possessing the wisdom of the ages, understands very well that violence and persecution cannot change man's religion. Pope Sixtus IV, in a warning to King 

Ferdinand and Queen Isabella of Spain, declared:

Sixteenth and Seventeenth Centuries (London: John Murray, 1840).

THE INQUISITION 

421 ce ... mercy towards the guilty is more pleasing to God than the severity which they [the high officials of the state] are using."

History records that Pope Sixtus not only gave asylum to four hundred and fifty refugees accused of heresy, but encouraged hundreds of others to flee to Rome, where he offered them protection. In other cases, the Popes excommunicated the Inquisitors themselves. 

We cannot defend the abuses and cruelties committed in the name of the Inquisition, which indeed followed the pattern of the other courts of Europe in these times; nor can we forget that the Catholic Church has suffered cruel persecutions in every age. Footprints of blood have marked her progress under such wicked rulers as Nero, Diocletian, Henry VIII, Cromwell, Hitler, Stalin, Mao 

Tse-tung, Tito, and Khrushchev, and many others. During the first three hundred years of Christianity, over a million Catholic martyrs shed their blood for belief in Jesus Christ. The Church, however, rejoices in the victories of martyrs because they are the seeds of faith. 

Referring to martyrdom, Cardinal Newman writes: "Every death is the parent of a thousand lives."

The Catholic Church has emerged from every persecution revitalized, zealous for new trials, while her persecutors pale into oblivion. "Unless the grain of wheat falling into the ground die, then itself remaineth alone. But if it die it bringeth forth much fruit.” (St. John 12:24-25)

Chapter LI TEMPTATION 

SOM OME of God's greatest saints suffered terrifying temptations. They are in heaven today not because they were never tempted, but because they overcame temptation. One fights temptation with prayer and fortitude. This grace comes with the sacraments especially through frequent confession and Communion, all of which verifies the charge of the Holy Spirit in The Book: "The kingdom of heaven suffereth violence and the violent bear it away.' 

(St. Matthew 11:12)

This means that only those who do violence to themselves by chastising their bodies and bringing them under subjection shall win heaven. Divine Providence has its way of testing to determine if we qualify for the kingdom of heaven. "Son, when thou comest to the service of God, stand in justice and in fear: and prepare thy soul for temptation." (Ecclesiasticus 2:1) "As silver is tried by fire, and gold in the furnace: so the Lord trieth the hearts." (Proverbs 17:3) 422

TEMPTATION 

423 St. Paul was sorely tempted and begged Almighty God to remove this trial. Our Lord reminded St. Paul: "My grace is sufficient for thee: for power is made perfect in infirmity.” (2 Corinthians 12:9)

No one should complain about temptation because, when courageously overcome, temptations become the source of merit and renewed strength. They build a stalwart character. All who reach the age of reason are subject to temptation. 

From the Book of Job we learn that Satan cannot tempt us without God's permission. Often temptations stem from the imagination. "Every man is tempted by his own concupiscence." (St. James 1:14) 

Even if one should surrender to a temptation and fall, he can always beg forgiveness and get up again — just as in the military ranks, a soldier does not mourn because he is wounded nor because he suffers fatigue. 

Keep in mind that a temptation, no matter how persistent or how horrible or violent, is not a sin. Not until we consent does guilt enter the picture. We should have the utmost confidence in divine protection if we appeal for help - for: "God is faithful, who will not suffer you to be tempted above that which you are able: but will make also with temptation issue, that you may be able to bear it." (1 Corinthians 10:13)

Chapter LII 

SIN SIN IN is the willful transgression of God's laws-a violation of any one or more of the ten commandments of God or the precepts of the Church. Sins may be committed by positive acts, by omission, by thought and by word. Theologians define sin as an "aversio a 

Deo," a turning away from God or turning one's back on God. 

Sins may be mortal or venial—that is, grievous or slight offenses against the law of God. As children, we learned in the little catechism the definitions that still stand out in our minds. 

Mortal sin, a serious offense against the law of God, takes its name from the effect namely, the loss of the supernatural life of the soul. Those living in mortal sin are spiritually dead. Mortal sin may be grave in itself: murder, for instance; or grave in its related circumstances: for example, a lie that inflicts character-assassination. Note the difference between vice, an inordinate habit of evil; and a defect, an inherent disorder. 

Venial sin is a slight offense against the law of God in matters of less importance; or in matters of great importance if committed without sufficient reflection or full consent of the will. Venial sin, so called because more easily forgiven, does not destroy the supernatural life of the soul. 424

SIN "For in many things we all offend." (St. James 3:2)

St. Paul specifies certain grave sins that exclude one from the eternal happiness of life after death. "Neither fornicators nor idolaters nor adulterers, . . . nor drunkards.. the kingdom of God." shall possess (1 Corinthians 6:9, 10) 

To commit a mortal sin, one must have: 

(a) SERIOUS MATTER 

For example, normally, stealing a slice of bread or an apple would not be serious matter; but to steal the equivalent of a worker's weekly salary or about $60.00 is a serious matter. Willful violations of the commandments are sinful. In regard to the eighth commandment, when one falsifies regarding a minor detail, such as saying that John Smith wore a black tie when, as a matter of fact, he wore a brown tie, this is not of sufficient importance to be serious. If, on the other hand, one fabricates a story to the effect that John Smith was caught stealing $100.00 from a store, the damage to character makes the sin mortal. Here, mere sorrow for sin does not suffice. 

Restitution of John Smith's good name is necessary to prove good faith and balance the injustice done. 

(b) SUFFICIENT REFLECTION 

Our divine Creator equips us with reason, by which we can detect the difference between good and evil. By

the still small voice of conscience God makes known to us our duty to shun evil and do good. 

One must be thoroughly conscious of committing sin and must realize what he is doing. Normally it is not possible to commit sin while asleep, because then there is no reflection. Philosophers present different views about just how much reflection can be called "sufficient." 

As a governing principle, it would be difficult to gauge precisely the minimum of sufficient reflection. For example, in the case of an evil thought, welcomed and indulged in with no effort to banish it, there could be sufficient reflection. 

If one does not know that an action is bad- for example, a person might exchange a counterfeit coin in good faith and there is no opportunity to learn that the act is bad, one cannot be blamed. Such ignorance is "invincible." If, on the other hand, a doubt arises regarding the morality of an act to be performed, one must determine whether the act is right or wrong-otherwise the ignorance is vincible. If overtaken by fear, one must stand firm. If, however, one surrenders under duress, he is less blameworthy. If fright becomes so intense that one does not know what he is doing, there is no sin. 

(c) FULL CONSENT OF THE WILL 

This requirement means that no one can force another to commit a mortal sin — for example, at the point of a revolver or by otherwise threatening loss of life or property. An act performed under such a threat is not free; the will does not operate, and there is no sin if

SIN one's consent to the wrong is totally withdrawn. If one consents partially in such a case, he is guilty to a certain degree. A learned moralist gave this example regarding the consciousness of committing a mortal sin. He taught: "It's like smashing your head into a stone wall. You know you've hit something and there's no doubt about it." 

On the other hand, our divine Creator has left a thin line of demarcation between mortal and venial sin to discourage us from ever attempting venial sins, which lessen the love of God in our hearts and cut down the barriers of protection.

Before the committing of every mortal sin, a subconscious struggle takes place between our conscience, the still small voice of God, and the evil tempter. Actual graces enlighten and urge us to avoid evil and do good. 

Divine grace tries, so to speak, but does not compel. 

In a word, we still have free will and, therefore, in every sin we make the decision - either in response to the voice of God, or in surrender to the devil, father of lies and all manner of deception.

Let it be clearly understood that a good end cannot justify a bad means. Neither can a bad end be justified by a good means. Good acts as such must conform to reason. In a word, freedom from disorder in their object and circumstances determines the morality. 

The question is often asked: "How can the good God condemn a soul to hell?" Actually, God does not condemn souls to hell. The rational being himself chooses between God and the devil; so that if death overtakes the sinner, the devil claims his soul, notwithstanding the

fact that God has lavished sufficient graces on His creature for him to overcome temptation or to repent. Catholics have the means to avoid sin in the sacrament of Penance, and in frequent, worthy reception of Holy Communion, in prayer and instant response to the promptings of divine grace.

Theologians teach that souls consigned to eternal misery go there glorifying the justice of God. Wherefore, the Holy Spirit warns us: "In all thy works remember thy last end, and thou shalt never sin." 

(Ecclesiasticus 7:40)

Chapter LIII THE SEVEN CAPITAL SINS THE 

HE seven capital sins derive their name from the 

Latin "caput," which means "head." They are so called because they are the fountainheads, the source, and the cause of many other sins. 

In one sense the seven capital sins are a disposition of the heart of man, corrupted by sin and "prone to evil from... youth" (Genesis 8:21). 

1. PRIDE is an inordinate esteem of our own excellence. Other sins, such as presumption, arrogance, vainglory, boasting, insolence, and even hypocrisy, stem from pride. The estimate we take of ourselves "at sea level" can cure this malady, because from this low vantage point we get the right latitude and longitude for the spiritual life. 

2. COVETOUSNESS, a form of avarice, puts the ac cumulation of wealth before the interests of the soul. 

It is sinful and foolish because it often causes injustice, fraud, and hardness of heart. Consistent and uniform generosity, self-sacrifice, even doing without, often afford a remedy, and build noble-minded manhood and the charm of gracious womanhood. 

3. LUST, an inordinate appetite for the pleasures of the flesh, degrades lawful love and sins against purity.

The remedies are prayer, chastity, frequent confession, vigilance, and habits of self-control. 429

4. ANGER, traceable to lack of poise and self-control, fails in charity. Anger promotes quarrels, strife, and often violence. Cultivation of meekness, prompt recourse to prayer before any hasty words are spoken, will prevent sin on our part. 

5. GLUTTONY is the excessive use of food and drink. 

Failure to control our appetite causes ill health and, often, sensuality. Self-discipline, temperance, spiritual reading will correct this vice. 

6. ENVY resents the wealth or well-being of others. 

If left unbridled, it results in hatred, detraction, calumny, and suspicion. Jealousy is in the same category. It stamps one as petty and deficient in the uniform charity expected of God's children. Christ teaches us to foster mutual love, to keep the "unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace" 

(Ephesians 4:3). Our divine Redeemer pleads with us: 

"Learn of Me, because I am meek, and humble of heart" (St. Matthew 11:29). 

7. SLOTH is inordinate laziness of body or mind.

It coddles weak characters to neglect or defer performance of their duty. It leaves the door open to temptation, and disregards both spiritual and temporal obligations. 

For one to overcome sloth, he should beg the Holy Spirit to alert, fortify and enlighten him; for "the night cometh, when no man can work" (St. John 9:4). No one can walk out on work and expect contentment: "Better a day of strife than a century of sleep" (Father Abram 

J. Ryan).

To banish these and other temptations, we must frequently beg the Holy Spirit to illumine our minds and 'create a right spirit within us."

Chapter LIV INDIFFERENTISM 

IN 

NDIFFERENTISM rests on the sophistry that one religion is as good as another and that all, therefore, are true and acceptable to God. Logic sound reasoning refutes this error. One religion is not as good as another, because one contradicts another. Two contradictory statements cannot be true at the same time. For example, one religion teaches that Jesus Christ is the Son of God, while others deny this fundamental. The Catholic religion declares that Jesus established one Church with seven sacraments, and that its primacy is vested in the Pope. All other creeds deny this truth.

The true religion teaches: "What therefore God hath joined together, let not man put asunder" (St. Mark 

10:9). Others distort these words of Jesus and declare in effect: "You may divorce and remarry as often as you wish." In distorted liturgical rites, in both moral and dogma, the various sects hold doctrines that oppose and contradict one another. It stands to reason that the God of unity and of wisdom could not sanction four hundred ninety-nine different sects opposed to one another and all claiming to represent Him and to have authority to teach in His name. 

The trite fallacy that it makes no difference what you believe "so long as you are good" leads to infidelity and apostasy. "So long as you are good" begs the question. 431

Religion was never intended to exclude sinners. Rather, the purpose our divine Saviour had in founding only one true religion was to afford all mankind the means whereby all could become good and stay good-by frequenting the sacraments. Experience shows clearly that indifferentists, who say it makes no difference what you believe, conclude that it makes no difference what you do. 

At first sight it does sound narrowminded to claim there is only one true religion established by Jesus Christ, but a bit of analysis reveals that the rational mind can admit nothing else. For example, seven people sit around a table arguing how much two plus two makes. One says four- another holds five; two claim six. One insists that two plus two equals seven. Two others present withhold comment. In comes an indifferentist who boldly proclaims: "You are all a little right and all a little wrong. Really, it makes no difference how much two plus two equals." The seven are sufficiently intelligent to reject the indifferentist; because they know that if one keeps books on the theory that it makes no difference what one does with figures, he will wind up in confusion. Witness the confusion among all the false religions, which explains why they divide, subdivide, reform, revise, and merge, with the result that the members of Protestant churches have not attained any definite unified belief. Moreover, neither the Bible nor Tradition authorizes a multiplicity of religions. A counterfeit coin points to a true original. The problem stems from man-made

INDIFFERENTISM 

433 creeds that spring from darkness of the mind and the impact of original sin. St. Paul exposes such creeds: "Because that, when they knew God, they have not glorified Him as God or given thanks: but became vain in their thoughts and their foolish heart was darkened. For, professing themselves to be wise, they became fools. 

And they changed the glory of the incorruptible God into the likeness of the image of a corruptible man and of birds, and of four-footed beasts and of creeping things." (Romans 1:21-23) 

The Catholic Church condemns the false and insidious pretensions of indifferentism, the subtle and misleading enemy of truth.

The indifferentist defaults through intellectual pride. 

He asserts that culture demands a kindly toleration of all creeds in a futile effort to belittle the stability of the

Catholic Church, "the pillar and ground of the truth" (1 Timothy 3:15).

Chapter LV  

HE false theory that souls are transferred after death

TH to other bodies has no support from the Bible or from reason. That part of theology usually referred to as eschatology (a word derived from the Greek) treats of death, judgment, heaven and hell. The subject pertains to the end of man's probation here on earth, the particular judgment following death, and the sentence decreed at that hearing: Or: "Come, ye blessed of My Father, possess you the kingdom prepared for you. . . . 

(St. Matthew 25:34) "Depart from Me, you cursed, into everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels." (St. Matthew 25:41) DEATH

One of the results of the original sin of our first parents, Adam and Eve, is death-the separation of the immortal soul from the body. The mortal remains dissolve into dust. At the general judgment which marks the end of the world, the body will be reunited with the soul. 434

"... And the dust return into its earth, from whence it was, and the spirit return to God, who gave it." 

(Ecclesiastes 12:7) 

With few exceptions, all people agree that sooner or later life will terminate for each one of us. Only two men, Henoch and Elias, are recorded in The Book as having left the earth without dying (cf. Ecclesiasticus 

44:16; 4 Kings 2:11). The Holy Spirit inspired St. Paul to write: "And... it is appointed unto men once to die, and after this the judgment." (Hebrews 9:27) 

We recite in the Apostles' Creed that Christ will come again "to judge the living and the dead." "The living" refers to those who survive at the end of the world. When, where, and under what circumstances death shall overtake us, we do not know. "... for at what hour you think not, the Son of man will come." (St. Luke 12:40)

Many times, every day, we beg the Blessed Virgin 

Mary to "pray for us sinners, now and at the hour of our death." In the Litany of the Saints, we pray to be spared from a "sudden and unprovided death." Moreover, our divine Lord has constantly warned us to be ready for the final summons, because He has told us

death will come "as a thief in the night" (1 Thessalonians 5:2). When does a thief come? When we least expect him.

THE LAST SACRAMENTS 

Catholics invoke the Holy Spirit for the grace of receiving the last sacraments, which ensure a holy and happy death. "Blessed are the dead who die in the Lord" (Apocalypse 14:13). "To die in the Lord" presupposes the reception of Holy Communion under the form of 

Viaticum, which means that our divine Lord Himself accompanies us on the final journey. Indeed, every worthy reception of Jesus in the Blessed Sacrament pledges everlasting happiness. The sacrament of Extreme Unction, the last anointing, instituted by Christ, was announced by St. James in his epistle.

The first anointing occurs in Baptism, the second in Confirmation, the third for those who receive Holy Orders; but for all, Extreme Unction is the last anointing. The goodness of our Saviour has provided the sacrament of Extreme Unction to comfort us in sickness and to strengthen the soul against the final attack of the evil one, to prepare us for entrance into heaven by removing venial sins and the remnants of sin, to grant the grace of resignation, and to restore health when conducive to our eternal salvation.

THE PARTICULAR JUDGMENT 

This doctrine stems from Tradition and from many passages in the Bible.

Re it is easy before God in the day of death to reward everyone according to his ways." (Ecclesiasticus 11:28)

The particular judgment follows at once upon the death of each individual: "For we shall all stand before the judgment seat of Christ.” (Romans 14:10) "I... desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ." (Philippians 1:23)

Here St. Paul implies a reward immediately following death. "And the rich man also died: and he was buried in hell." (St. Luke 16:22)

The Bible account of the repentant thief confirms the doctrine of the particular judgment: "Amen I say to thee: This day thou shalt be with Me in paradise." (St. Luke 23:43)

All of us shall hear one of two verdicts from the Supreme Judge: "Depart from Me, you cursed, into everlasting fire, which was prepared for the devil and his angels." (St. Matthew 25:41)

Or we shall hear the joyful sentence: "Come, ye blessed of My Father, possess you the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world. For I was hungry, and you gave Me to eat; I was thirsty, and you gave Me to drink; I was a stranger, and you took Me in; naked, and you covered Me; sick, and you visited Me; I was in prison, and you came to Me." (St. Matthew 25:34-36) 

And the bewildered soul will ask: "Lord, when did we see Thee hungry, and fed 

Thee: thirsty, and gave Thee drink? And when did we see Thee a stranger and took 

Thee in? Or naked and covered Thee? Or when did we see Thee sick or in prison and came to Thee? ... Amen I say to you, as long as you did it to one of these My least brethren, you did it to Me." (St. Matthew 25:37-39, 40)

This judgment remains irrevocable. That Catholics pray for the dead and offer Masses for their happy repose does not imply that the particular judgment can be modified. In these suffrages the Church, in spirit, envisions the time before the soul left the body and prays for its resurrection from eternal death. God, in His mercy and omnipresence, knows of the prayers and Masses that will be said for each individual.

HELL There are some who pretend to accept every word in the Bible and yet reject the doctrine of eternal punishment, notwithstanding the fact that Jesus refers to it fifteen times in the New Testament.

SANCTION Of the Law 

Although the word has different meanings, here hell designates the place or state where sinners who persist in their iniquity and who willfully reject God unto the very last feeble pulsation of life, receive eternal punishment. The justice and wisdom of God and the best interest of the rational creature demand a sanction. To be effective, a law must have a sanction — i. e., a reward for observing it and a punishment for violating it. Federal, state and municipal laws are supported by sanctions. In fact, a law without a sanction becomes ineffective. To be respected, God's laws must levy sanctions. Reason also dictates this; otherwise, man could falsely conclude that no difference exists between right and wrong, a dangerous attitude, destructive of justice. Reflect that by mortal sin man turns his back on God and, in effect, prefers the devil. Our Saviour taught us: "If anyone abide not in Me, he shall be cast forth as a branch and shall wither; and they shall gather him up and cast him into the fire; and he burneth.' 

(St. John 15:6) 

1 St. John 3:8) "He that committeth sin is of the devil."

Over and above the dictates of reason we have the warning of Jesus Christ who, while on earth, cautioned sinners against giving scandal by pointing out its punishunquenchable fire: where their worm dieth not, and the fire is not extinguished" (St. Mark 9:42-43). 

The "worm" here signifies remorse; the "fire" excruciating torture, the pain of sense. ment:

Mortal sin is a voluntary separation from God. The knowingly unrepentant sinner deprives himself of God, the Eternal Good. Life here on earth is a time of probation. Our sense of justice confirms the salutary warning to be ready for the summons, for we know not the day nor the hour. 

The punishment of hell includes: (1) the pain of loss, which reflects the malice of sin — in effect, the rejection of God, our last end; (2) the pain of sense, inflicted by evil spirits and by hell fire; (3) remorse of conscience, often called "the gnawing worm," mental anguish the realization of guilt for having turned a deaf ear when Christ knocked at the door of the heart; for having bartered eternal happiness for "a mess of pottage." 

PURGATORY 

Venial sins are a slight offense against the law of God. These, too, must be atoned for, because nothing defiled can enter heaven. We are also warned in Holy 

Scripture that the last farthing must be paid. The only way we can accomplish this is by a period of atonement in Purgatory.

The second Book of Machabees confirms the Cath olic doctrine of Purgatory: "It is therefore a holy and wholesome thought to pray for the dead, that they may be loosed from sins." (2 Machabees 12:46)

This is the tradition of faithful people who offer their prayers and the Holy Mass to help the souls in Purgatory. 

HEAVEN 

Heaven may be defined as a place and a state of perfect everlasting happiness, where just souls- that is, those who die in the state of grace — will be rewarded with the vision of God. St. Paul teaches: ce eye hath not seen, nor ear heard: neither hath it entered into the heart of man, what things God hath prepared for them that love Him." (1 Corinthians 2:9) 

When Holy Scripture uses the terms "eternal life," or "eternal happiness," or an "everlasting kingdom," heaven is understood. "And these shall go into everlasting punishment: but the just, into life everlasting." (St. Matthew 25:46) 

Happiness that could terminate would not be perfect.

The just shall enter into eternal life (cf. St. Matthew 

25:46). The eternal reward, which will start as soon as

judgment has been passed — before soul and body are reunited consists essentially in the possession of God. 

You possess the sight of a field of beautiful lilies by seeing; you possess the sounds of classical music by hearing; you possess God by contemplation, or the Beatific Vision. 

St. Paul clarifies this doctrine: "We see now through a glass in a dark manner: but then face to face. Now I know in part: but then I shall know even as I am known." (1 Corinthians 13:12) 

During this life we know God by His image and His power as reflected in the works of creation - obscurely, feebly, as "through a glass." In heaven the blessed shall have a perfect knowledge. Seeing God clearly, face to face, they are drawn irresistibly towards Him by His beauty and intense love. This results in indescribable joy. "Enter thou into the joy of thy Lord." (St. Matthew 25:21)

Evidently man was created to enjoy the wonderful bliss of heaven. Nothing material can satisfy the yearning of the soul for God. This is a cogent reason why 

St. Paul expressed his ardent desire to be dissolved and to be with Christ our one true everlasting love. 

The happiness of heaven consists in contemplation, love and joy. The contemplation of God, the cause of love and joy, is a supernatural gift. The boundless

goodness of God holds out this reward to man and grants. him sufficient grace to attain it. Not reason alone, but the promises of Christ, ensure the reward of heaven.

While the contemplation of God is beyond our natural capacity, God will elevate man's capacity by a supernatural gift called the "lumen gloriae" -the light of glory. 

Beyond the intense happiness of seeing God face to face in heaven, the blessed will experience accidental joys which add to their bliss, such as the companionship of saints and angels. 

The reward of heaven, while not capable of increase or development, is in proportion to the degree of one's merit. "He who soweth sparingly shall also reap sparingly; and he who soweth in blessings shall also reap blessings." (2 Corinthians 9:6)

THE GENERAL JUDGMENT 

Revealed religion unmistakably teaches the doctrine of a general judgment at the end of the world: "And when the Son of man shall come in His majesty, and all the angels with Him, then shall He sit upon the seat of His majesty.

And all nations shall be gathered together before Him; and He shall separate them one from another, as the shepherd separateth the sheep from the goats." (St. Matthew 25:31-32)

"for them ALSO”

The general judgment will vindicate the goodness and mercy of God and make known to all mankind the glorious triumph of Jesus Christ. "And immediately after the tribulation of those days, the sun shall be darkened and the moon shall not give her light and the stars shall fall from heaven and the powers of heaven shall be moved. And then shall appear the sign of the Son of man in heaven.

And then shall all tribes of the earth mourn: and they shall see the Son of man coming in the clouds of heaven with much power and majesty. And He shall send His angels with a trumpet and a great voice: and they shall gather together His elect from the four winds, from the farthest parts of the heavens to the utmost bounds of them." (St. Matthew 24:29-31)

St. Matthew thus indicates the signs that will precede the destruction of the world. This was intended as an image or graphic description of the terrifying Day of Doom the termination of the universe.

The details and the time of the general judgment are withheld from us. "But of that day and hour no one knoweth: no, not the angels of heaven, but the Father alone." (St. Matthew 24:36)

The days of probation having concluded, the blessed in heaven and the lost in hell shall survive. According to 

St. Paul, nature sighs for the hour of deliverance. "For the expectation of the creature waiteth for the revelation of the sons of God." (Romans 8:19)

As a corollary of the last judgment, we may look for the fulfillment of another truth set forth in the Apostles' 

Creed: “... the resurrection of the body," which in reality is the completion of the work of redemption. "Wonder not at this: for the hour cometh, wherein all that are in the graves shall hear the voice of the Son of God. And they that have done good things shall come forth unto the resurrection of life: but they that have done evil, unto the resurrection of judgment." 

The Apostles stressed the (St. John 5:28-29) resurrection of the body: "But some man will say: 'How do the dead rise again? Or with what manner of body shall they come?'"' (1 Corinthians 15:35) 

At the general judgment, the wisdom of God, His justice and mercy in dealing with mankind will be made manifest. Instead of coming as our divine Redeemer, scourged and crowned with thorns, He will appear triumphant and glorious. His kingdom on earth, the Church,

maligned and persecuted and rejected by so many through the centuries, shall appear in splendor. "Sown in corruption, it shall rise in incorruption. It is sown in dishonor; it shall rise in glory. It is sown in weakness; it shall rise in power. It is sown a natural body; it shall rise a spiritual body. and when this mortal [body] hath put on immortality, then shall come to pass the saying that is written: 'Death is swallowed up in victory.'... Thanks be to God, who hath given us the victory through our 

Lord Jesus Christ" (1 Corinthians 15:42-44, 54, 57). ..."

Chapter LVI THE COMMUNION OF SAINTS 

HE revealed word of God gives us the beautiful and 

THE 

profess it in the Apostles' Creed. It consists of the blessed souls in heaven, the suffering souls in purgatory, and the Church militant here on earth. Our Saviour heads these three groups of His kingdom; all are members of one body in Christ.

Children of our eternal Father share with one another their prayers, sacrifices and intercession. The one Mediator between God and man makes possible this mutual assistance. "And He is the head of the body, the Church, who is the beginning, the firstborn from the dead; that in all things He may hold the primacy." (Colossians 1:18)

As the invisible Head of the Church, Jesus constantly guards her and lavishes His graces on her faithful. He too presides over the souls in purgatory, and communicates to them special gifts to preserve supernatural life. 

In heaven, as King of glory, Jesus Christ bestows the 

Beatific Vision as well as the additional "light of glory." 

To answer the oft-repeated question, "Why are members of the Church militant here on earth sometimes referred to as saints (as in the phrase 'the communion 447

of saints') ?" we reply that all having been sanctified in baptism are called to sanctity, and because of the eminent holiness of so many dedicated people. 

The saints in heaven help us by their intercession. 

The souls in Purgatory profit by our prayers and the Holy Masses offered for them. They, too, can beg graces for us. The soldiers of Christ, who form the Church militant, share in the treasure of the Church and in the infinite fruits of the adorable Sacrifice of the altar. 

When we pray for the souls of our dear departed and offer Mass for them, we humbly submit our petitions to the wisdom and mercy of God.

Epilogue A THOUGHT TO CHERISH 

UST assume that today you are summoned before the

JUST divine Judge to give an account of your stewardship. 

Before your trial starts, you beg the Almighty to defer your case so as to permit you to return to your home city for three hours. 

The Eternal Judge would ask for what reason you wish to return. You submit, "There are relatives and friends there who used to tell me what they thought they knew. Now I would like to go back and tell them what I know.”

Divine Wisdom answers: "There was a lost soul here, Dives, who made the same request. He too asked to return in order to advise his people of his unfavorable condition in the unseen world. We told Dives that his people had had Moses and the prophets and that if they would not believe them, they would not believe him, risen from the dead. "Moreover, over nineteen hundred years ago, our

Lord Jesus Christ, born of the Virgin Mary in a stable at Bethlehem, began his missionary career at the age of thirty. He spoke My message in the common language of the people. He expressed the truth that I wish to rule, not by fear but by love and mercy and understanding. . . .

But the people, led on by Pharisees, staged a fake trial wherein every vestige of justice was suppressed. The mob called for the crucifixion of My divine Son. 449

"Notwithstanding the fact that He 'went about doing good,' healing the sick, restoring strength and health, driving out devils and raising the dead to life, they said 'He hath a devil' (St. John 10:20); that He was a 'winedrinker' (St. Matthew 11:19). To show that they meant what they said, they hung Him on a cross between two thieves. If your relatives would not believe Him, they would not believe you, risen from the dead. "Furthermore, in your native city there are many churches where I dwell sacramentally in the tabernacle; where the people are welcomed with love and mercy and where tribunals are set up with duly commissioned judges empowered to forgive sins. In these churches, there are 'other Christs' who still preach the burning love of My 

Sacred Heart; who continue the unbloody Sacrifice of the 

Cross and break the Bread of Life for the faithful. Your relatives and friends have been warmly welcomed to My tabernacles on earth. If they will not believe My ambassadors, the priests of the New Law, they would not believe you, risen from the dead. "Although you didn't mean it that way, what you request is a reflection on My generosity, mercy and love.

It's as much as to say that I expect to reap where I have not sown; so your petition must be denied." "Mother and teacher of all nations, the universal

Church has been instituted by Jesus Christ so that all who in the long course of centuries come to her loving embrace may find the fullness of higher life and the guarantee of salvation" (John XXIII, Mater et Magistra, May 

15, 1961).
